Output-sensitive approximate counting via a measure-bounded hyperedge oracle, or: How asymmetry helps estimate k k -clique counts faster
Keren Censor-Hillel, Tomer Even, Virginia Vassilevska Williams

Abstract
Dell, Lapinskas and Meeks [DLM SICOMP 2022] presented a general reduction from approximate counting to decision for a class of fine-grained problems that can be viewed as hyperedge counting or detection problems in an implicit hypergraph, thus obtaining tight equivalences between approximate counting and decision for many key problems such as k k -clique, k k -sum and more. Their result is a reduction from approximately counting the number of hyperedges in an implicit k k -partite hypergraph to a polylogarithmic number of calls to a hyperedge oracle that returns whether a given subhypergraph contains an edge. The main result of this paper is a generalization of the DLM result for output-sensitive approximate counting, where the running time of the desired counting algorithm is inversely proportional to the number of witnesses. Our theorem is a reduction from approximately counting the (unknown) number of hyperedges in an implicit k k -partite hypergraph to a polylogarithmic number of calls to a hyperedge oracle called only on subhypergraphs with a small “measure”. If a subhypergraph has u i u_{i} nodes in the i i th node partition of the k k -partite hypergraph, then its measure is ∏ i u i \prod_{i}u_{i} . Using the new general reduction and by efficiently implementing measure-bounded colorful independence oracles, we obtain new improved output-sensitive approximate counting algorithms for k k -clique, k k -dominating set and k k -sum. In graphs with n t n^{t} k k -cliques, for instance, our algorithm ( 1 ± ϵ ) (1\pm\epsilon) -approximates the k k -clique count in time O ~ ϵ ​ ( n ω ⁡ ( k − t − 1 3 , k − t 3 , k − t + 2 3 ) + n 2 ) , \tilde{O}_{\epsilon}(n^{\omega(\frac{k-t-1}{3},\frac{k-t}{3},\frac{k-t+2}{3})}+n^{2}), where ω ⁡ ( a , b , c ) \omega(a,b,c) is the exponent of n a × n b n^{a}\times n^{b} by n b × n c n^{b}\times n^{c} matrix multiplication. For large k k and t > 2 t>2 , this is a substantial improvement over prior work, even if ω = 2 \omega=2 .



1
Introduction

For many computational problems, approximately counting the number of solutions is known to be reducible to detecting whether a solution exists. This is true in a large number of scenarios. For instance, Valiant and Vazirani
[
VV86
]
showed that if one can solve SAT in polynomial time, then one can also approximately solve
\#
SAT (or any problem in
\#
P) in polynomial time, within an arbitrary constant precision. Similar statements are true in the FPT setting
[
Mül06
]
and the subexponential time setting
[
DL21
]
.

For the fine-grained setting, Dell, Lapinskas and Meeks
[
DLM22
,
DLM24
]
(following
[
DL21
]
) presented a general tight reduction from
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate counting to decision for any problem that can be viewed as determining whether an
n
-node
k
-uniform
k
-partite hypergraph contains an edge.

Let’s take
k
-clique as an example. It is known that detecting, finding and counting
k
-cliques in an
n
-node graph is equivalent to the respective problem in
k
-partite graphs with
n
nodes in each part. Then, given a
k
-partite graph
G
, one can mentally define a
k
-partite
k
-uniform hypergraph
H
with
n
nodes in each part where
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
is a hyperedge in
H
if and only if
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})
is a
k
-clique in
G
. Counting the number of
k
-cliques in
G
approximately then corresponds to approximately counting the number of hyperedges in
H
.

Many other problems parameterized by their solution size can be viewed in this way:
k
-SUM,
k
-Dominating set,
k
-OV and so on.

Dell, Lapinskas and Meeks
[
DLM22
]
present a reduction that given an oracle
O
that can determine whether a
k
-partite hypergraph has no hyperedges (a “colorful independence oracle”), for any constant
k
, one can obtain with high probability a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-approximation to the number of hyperedges in
H
in time
(n/\varepsilon^{2})\textrm{polylog}(n)
and with
(1/\varepsilon^{2})\textrm{polylog}(n)
queries to
O
:

Theorem 1

(Dell-Lapinskas-Meeks’22)
.

Let
G
be a
k
-partite hypergraph with vertex set
V^{\mathrm{input}}=V^{\mathrm{input}}_{1}\sqcup V^{\mathrm{input}}_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V^{\mathrm{input}}_{k}
, where
\left\lvert V^{\mathrm{input}}\right\rvert=n
,
and a set
E
of
m
(unknown) hyperedges.
There exists a randomized algorithm Count
(G,\varepsilon)
that takes as input the vertex set
V^{\mathrm{input}}
and has access to a colorful independence oracle
O
, which given
X_{1}\subseteq V^{\mathrm{input}}_{1},\ldots,X_{k}\subseteq V^{\mathrm{input}}_{k}
returns whether the subhypergraph of
G
induced by
\cup_{i}X_{i}
contains a hyperedge. Count outputs
\hat{m}
such that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}=m(1\pm\varepsilon)\right]\geq 1-1/n^{4}\;.

Count runs in
O(nT)
time and queries
O
at most
T
times, where

T=(k\log n)^{O(k)}/\varepsilon^{2}.

This generic reduction shows that for all problems that can be cast in the
k
-partite hypergraph framework, the running time for
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-approximate counting of solutions is within a
(1/\varepsilon^{2})\textrm{polylog}(n)
factor of the running time for detecting a “colorful” solution, i.e. one that has exactly one vertex in each node partition in a
k
-partite input. For a large number of problems such as subgraph pattern problems like
k
-clique and directed
k
-cycles detecting a colorful pattern is equivalent to detection (without the colors), so that this theorem gives a tight reduction from approximate counting to detection.

For many problems, when the witness count is large, detection and approximate counting can be done faster. For instance, consider the Triangle (i.e.
3
-clique) problem in
n
node graphs. If
G
contains at least
t
triangles, then by sampling
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n^{3}/t)
triples of vertices and checking them for triangles, we can estimate
t
within a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
factor. That is, the approximate counting running time is
inversely
proportional to the number of copies. Algorithms with running times that depend on the number of solutions are called
output-sensitive
.

For triangles, Tětek
[
Tět22
]
obtained an even better output-sensitive approximate counting running time,
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n^{\omega}/t^{\omega-2})
, where
\omega<2.37134

[
ADV+25
]
is the matrix multiplication exponent. Censor-Hillel, Even and Vassilevska W.
[
CEV24
]
improved the bound further using rectangular matrix multiplication and also obtained similar algorithms for approximately counting
k
-cycles in directed graphs for any constant
k\geq 3
. They also showed that their algorithms are conditionally optimal, based on assumptions from fine-grained complexity.
These works implicitly reduce output-sensitive triangle and
k
-cycle approximate counting to detection. A very natural and important question is

Can the general theorem of Dell-Lapinskas-Meeks be extended for output-sensitive approximate counting?

If this can be done, then what does the running time for output-sensitive approximate counting look like for various important problems in fine-grained complexity: for
k
-clique,
k
-SUM,
k
-dominating set?

1.1
Our results.

Our main theorem is an extension of Dell-Lapinskas-Meeks’ theorem for output-sensitive approximate counting. The theorem statement involves a function
\mu
on subsets
U:=U_{1}\cup U_{2}\cup\ldots\cup U_{k}
where for each
i\in[k]
,
U_{i}\subseteq V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}
, and where
V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}
is the
i
th node partition of the input
k
-partite hypergraph. We define
\mu(U)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}|U_{i}|
. An
Hyperedge-Oracle
is an oracle that takes as input a set
U
and returns whether the induced subhypergraph
G[U]
contains a hyperedge or not.

Theorem 2

(Main Theorem)
.

Let
G
be a
k
-partite hypergraph with vertex set
V^{\mathrm{input}}=V^{\mathrm{input}}_{1}\sqcup V^{\mathrm{input}}_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V^{\mathrm{input}}_{k}
, where
\left\lvert V^{\mathrm{input}}\right\rvert=n
,
and a set
E
of
m
hyperedges.
There exists a randomized algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
that takes as input the vertex set
V^{\mathrm{input}}
and has access to a
Hyperedge-Oracle
, which
outputs
\hat{m}
such that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}=m(1\pm\varepsilon)\right]\geq 1-1/n^{4}\;.

The algorithm queries the oracle at most
T
times, while only querying sets
U
that satisfy
\mu(U)\leq R
, for

\displaystyle T=(k\log n)^{O(k^{3})}/\varepsilon^{2k}\;,

\displaystyle R=\frac{\mu(V^{\mathrm{input}})}{m}\cdot\frac{(k\log n)^{O(k^{3})}}{\varepsilon^{2k}}\;.

Given our new general theorem, we turn to implementing
Hyperedge-Oracle
for several fundamental problems of interest:
k
-Clique,
k
-Dominating set and
k
-SUM.

A new approximate counting algorithm for
k
-Clique.

The fastest algorithm for
k
-Clique detection and exact counting is by Nešetril and Poljak
[
NP85
]
who reduced the problem to triangle counting in a larger graph.
The running time of the algorithm is

O(n^{\omega(\lfloor k/3\rfloor,\lceil k/3\rceil,k-\lfloor k/3\rfloor-\lceil k/3\rceil)}),

where
\omega(a,b,c)
is the exponent for
n^{a}\times n^{b}
by
n^{b}\times n^{c}
matrix multiplicaiton. (The best known bounds for these rectangular exponents can be found in
[
ADV+25
]
).

When an
n
-node graph has
m=n^{t}

k
-cliques, one can easily estimate
m
in
\tilde{O}_{\varepsilon}(n^{k}/m)
time by sampling
k
-tuples of vertices and checking if they form a
k
-clique.
Since
\Omega(n^{2})
time is necessary when
m\leq n^{k-2}

[
ERS20
,
ER18
]
1
1

1

The lower bound is
\Omega\left(e\right)
where
e
is the number of edges in the graph, or
\Omega\left(\frac{n}{m^{1/k}}+\min\left\{\frac{e^{k/2}}{m},e\right\}\right)
, which is
\Omega\left(n^{2}\right)
for

e=\Omega\left(n^{2}\right)
and
m\leq n^{k-2}
.
and the sampling algorithm gets at least
\Omega(n^{2})
then, the interesting regime is when
m\leq O(n^{k-2})
.

What is the best running time for approximate
k
-clique counting when
m\in[\Omega(1),O(n^{k-2})]
?

As mentioned earlier, for
k=3
, i.e. approximate triangle counting, a faster algorithm is possible, running in time
\tilde{O}_{\varepsilon}(n^{\omega(1-t,1,1)})

[
CEV24
,
Tět22
]
.
We could apply Nešetril and Poljak’s reduction from
k
-clique to triangle and then use the approximate triangle counting algorithm of
[
CEV24
]
. How good is this? Say that
k
is divisible by
3
. Then Nešetril and Poljak’s reduction creates a graph on
O(n^{k/3})
vertices that has a number of triangles that is exactly
{k\choose k/3}{2k/3\choose k/3}
times the number
m=n^{t}
of
k
cliques in the original graph. Applying the algorithm of
[
CEV24
]
on this graph we get a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
-approximation to
m
in
\tilde{O}_{\varepsilon}(n^{\omega(k/3-t,k/3,k/3)})
time.

We show that we can do better:

Theorem 3

(Clique (Simplified))
.

Let
G
be a given graph with
n
vertices and let
k\geq 3
be a fixed integer.
There is a randomized algorithm that outputs an approximation
\hat{m}
for the number
m
of
k
-cliques in
G
such that

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[(1-\varepsilon)m\leq\hat{m}\leq(1+\varepsilon)m\right]\geq 1-1/n^{2}
, for any constant
\varepsilon>0
. Let
r
be such that
n^{r}=n^{k}/m
.
The running time is bounded by
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}({n^{\psi}+n^{2}})
, where

\psi=\frac{1}{3}\max\left\{\omega(r-1,r-1,r+2),\omega(r-2,r+1,r+1)\right\}\leq\frac{1}{3}\omega(r-1,r,r+2).

If
m=n^{t}
and
r=k-t
, the running time of our
k
-clique approximate counting algorithm is never worse than

\tilde{O}_{\varepsilon}(n^{\omega(\frac{k-t-1}{3},\frac{k-t}{3},\frac{k-t+2}{3})}+n^{2}).

Thus for large
k
, we are essentially distributing the
-t
term almost equally among the three inputs to
\omega
, getting a running time closer to
n^{\omega(k-t)/3}
. For
t>2
, our running time is substantially better than the
\tilde{O}_{\varepsilon}(n^{\omega(k/3-t,k/3,k/3)})
time that follows from composing
[
CEV24
]
with the Nešetril and Poljak reduction. The easiest way to see this is when
\omega=2
: then
\omega(k/3-t,k/3,k/3)=2k/3
, whereas
\omega(\frac{k-t-1}{3},\frac{k-t}{3},\frac{k-t+2}{3})=2k/3-2(t-2)/3
.
In other words, our algorithm dependency in
t
substantially improves upon previous approach even if
\omega=2
.

We obtain our result using our
Theorem

2
and by designing a specialized
Hyperedge-Oracle
for
k
-clique. This
Hyperedge-Oracle
is obtained by a modification of Nešetril and Poljak’s reduction aimed at optimizing the running time for
k
-clique detection in unbalanced
k
-partite graphs. See the overview section.

For the special case of
k=4
we get the following running time, which is faster than the best known running time of
O(n^{\omega(2,1,1)})
for
4
-clique detection, for every
m=n^{\Omega\left(1\right)}
.

Theorem 4

(
4
-clique)
.

Let
G
be a given graph with
n
vertices.
There is a randomized algorithm that outputs an approximation
\hat{m}
for the number
m=n^{t}
of
4
-cliques in
G
such that

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[(1-\varepsilon)m\leq\hat{m}\leq(1+\varepsilon)m\right]\geq 1-1/n^{2}
, for any constant
\varepsilon>0
. The running time is bounded by
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}({n^{\psi_{4}}+n^{2}})
, where

\displaystyle\psi_{4}=\max\left\{\omega(1,1,2-t),\left(1-\frac{t}{4}\right)\cdot\omega(1,1,2),\omega\left(1,1-\frac{t}{3},2-\frac{2t}{3}\right)\right\}\;.

We note that

\psi_{4}\leq\max\left\{t+(1-t/2)\omega(1,1,2),\left(1-\frac{t}{4}\right)\cdot\omega(1,1,2),(t/3)+(1-t/3)\omega\left(1,1,2\right)\right\}\leq\omega(1,1,2)-t/2,

and so the running time of our
4
-clique approximate counting algorithm is at most
\tilde{O}(n^{2}+n^{\omega(1,1,2)}/\sqrt{m})
.

(a)

k=4

(b)

k=5

:
The above figures illustrate the comparison between our work and prior work for
k=4
and
k=5
in the
k
-clique problem. The blue line depicts the running time of
[
NP85
]
, which does not depend on
m
. The orange line depicts the running time of the sampling algorithm.
The green line depicts our running time specified in
Theorem

3
, and the red line depicts our running time specified in
Theorem

4
.

1
and
2
are plotted based on
[
Ren21
]
.

New approximate counting algorithms for
k
-Dominating set.

The fastest algorithm for
k
-dominating set detection and exact counting is by
Eisenbrand and Grandoni
[
EG04
]
who reduced this problem to counting triangles in a tripartite graph, with vertex set of size
n,n^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
and
n^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
.
The running time of the algorithm is

O(n^{\omega(1,\lfloor k/2\rfloor,\lceil k/2\rceil)})\;.

The problem of detecting whether a graph contains a
k
-dominating set is in the class
W[2]
, and also has a tight conditional lower bound of
n^{k-o(1)}

based on SETH
[
PW10
]
.
On the other hand, the previously mentioned algorithm of
[
EG04
]
obtains a running time of
n^{k+o(1)}
for
k\geq 8
, and if
\omega=2
then also for
k\geq 2
.
The question of deciding whether a graph contains a
k
-dominating set when the graph is sparse was also studied in
[
FKR24
]
.

The simple sampling algorithm for approximate counting of
k
-dominating sets is to sample
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n^{k}/m)
sets of size
k
and check if they form a
k
-dominating set, where testing if a set of
k
vertices is a
k
-dominating set can be done in
O(kn)
time, and therefore, for constant
k
we get an upper bound of
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n^{k+1}/m)
time.
Other than that, to the best of our knowledge, there is no known output-sensitive algorithm for
k
-dominating set approximate counting or detection, and we provide the first such algorithm:

Theorem 5

(
k
-DS (Simplified))
.

Let
G
be a given graph with
n
vertices and let
k\geq 3
be a fixed integer.
There is a randomized algorithm that outputs an approximation
\hat{m}
for the number
m=n^{t}
of
k
-dominating sets in
G
such that

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[(1-\varepsilon)m\leq\hat{m}\leq(1+\varepsilon)m\right]\geq 1-1/n^{2}
, for any constant
\varepsilon>0
. The running time is bounded by

\displaystyle\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n^{\omega(1,(k-t-1)/2,(k-t+1)/2)})\;.

If
k-t\geq 1.64
(see footnote
2
2

2

When
1\leq\alpha\cdot(k-t-1)/2
, where
\alpha\geq 0.321334
is the largest known value s.t.
\omega(1,\alpha,1)=2
, we have that
\omega(1,(k-t-1)/2,(k-t+1)/2)=k-t
.
) or if
\omega=2
, the running time is
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n^{k}/m)
.

Thus, we obtain an improvement over the simple
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n^{k+1}/m)
sampling algorithm.

Theorem 6

(
3
-dominating set)
.

Let
G
be a given graph with
n
vertices.
There is a randomized algorithm that outputs an approximation
\hat{m}
for the number
m=n^{t}
of
k
-dominating sets in
G
such that

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[(1-\varepsilon)m\leq\hat{m}\leq(1+\varepsilon)m\right]\geq 1-1/n^{2}
, for any constant
\varepsilon>0
. The running time is bounded by

\displaystyle\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n^{2}+n^{\psi})\;,

\displaystyle\text{where }\quad\psi=\max\left\{\omega(1,1-\frac{t}{3},\frac{2(t-1)}{3}),\omega(1,1,2-t)\right\}\leq\omega(1,1,2(1-\frac{t}{3}))\;.

k=3

(b)

New approximate counting algorithms for
k
-sum.

In the
k
-sum problem, we are given a set of
n
numbers and we are asked to decide if there exists a
k
-tuple of numbers that sum to zero.
This problem can be deterministically reduced to a “colorful” version, where we are given
k
sets
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
of
n
numbers each, and we are asked to decide if there exists a
k
-tuple of numbers, one from each set, that sum to zero.
Moreover, the reduction preserves the number of solutions, and if the original problem had
m
solutions, then the colorful version has
m\cdot k!
solutions.
The problem of
k
-sum detection is one of the most studied problems in fine-grained complexity, and it is conjectured that for every constant
\varepsilon>0

it does not have a
O(n^{\lceil k/2\rceil-\varepsilon})
time algorithm (see e.g.
[
AL13
,
VW09
,
Vas18
]
).

The
k
-sum problem also has a simple sampling algorithm that samples
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n^{k}/m)

k
-tuples of numbers and checks if they sum to zero, but to the best of our knowledge, there is no known output-sensitive algorithm for
k
-sum approximate counting.

We provide the first output-sensitive algorithm for
k
-sum approximate counting, for
k\geq 3
.

Theorem 7

(
k
-Sum (Simplified))
.

Let
A
be a set of
n
integers, and let
k\geq 3
be a fixed integer.
There is a randomized algorithm that outputs an approximation
\hat{m}
for the number
m=n^{t}
of
k
-sum tuples in
A
such that

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[(1-\varepsilon)m\leq\hat{m}\leq(1+\varepsilon)m\right]\geq 1-1/n^{2}
, for any constant
\varepsilon>0
. The running time is bounded by
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n+\min\left\{n^{(k-t+1)/2},n^{\lceil k/2\rceil}\right\})
.

The next theorem provides a better bound for smaller value of
k
.

Theorem 8

(
3
-Sum)
.

Let
A
be a set of
n
integers.
There is a randomized algorithm that outputs an approximation
\hat{m}
for the number
m=n^{t}
of
3
-sum tuples in
A
such that

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[(1-\varepsilon)m\leq\hat{m}\leq(1+\varepsilon)m\right]\geq 1-1/n^{2}
, for any constant
\varepsilon>0
. The running time is bounded by
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n+n^{2(1-t/3)})
.

(a)

k=3

(b)

k=9

Figure 3
:
The above figures illustrate the comparison between our work and prior work for
k=3
and
k=9
in the
k
-sum problem. The blue line depicts the folklore running time of
n^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
, which does not depend on
m
.
The orange line depicts the running time of the sampling algorithm.
The green line depicts our running time specified in
Theorem

7
, and the red line depicts our running time specified in

.

Additional Related Work.

The problem of detecting or approximately counting subgraphs has also been explored within the framework of sublinear algorithms. In the standard sublinear model
[
PR02
,
KKR04
]
, an algorithm may make three types of queries:

(i)

degree queries, which return a vertex’s degree,

(ii)

adjacency queries, which indicate whether two vertices are adjacent, and

(iii)

i
th neighbor queries, which return the
i
th neighbor of a given vertex.

A stronger, augmented model
[
ABG+18
]
allows a fourth type of query that yields a uniformly random edge.

In the general case, detecting a subgraph
H
requires
\Omega(m)
queries because the entire input must be read. Consequently, sublinear algorithms focus on settings with many copies of
H
(denoted
t
), aiming to devise algorithms whose complexity decreases as
t
increases.

For instance, for approximate counting the number of triangles
[
ELRS17
]
establishes query time of

\Theta\bigl(\frac{n}{t^{1/3}}+\min\left\{m,\frac{m^{3/2}}{t}\right\}\bigr)
, with running time of
O(\frac{n}{t^{1/3}}+\frac{m^{3/2}}{t})
,
and
[
ERS20
]

provides an
O\bigl(\frac{n}{t^{1/k}}+\frac{m^{k/2}}{t}\bigr)
time and query complexity algorithm for approximate counting of a
k
-clique.
In the augmented model, there are results for general subgraphs
H

[
AKK18
,
FGP20
,
BER21
]
.
For approximate counting a general subgraph
H

[
AKK18
]
presents a sublinear-time algorithm in
\tilde{O}\bigl(\frac{m^{\rho(H)}}{t}\bigr)
time, and
\tilde{O}\bigl(\min\left\{m,\frac{m^{\rho(H)}}{t}\right\}\bigr)
query complexity, where
\rho(H)
denotes the fractional edge-cover number of
H
, with a matching lower bound on the number of queries for odd length cycles.
For sampling a uniform copy of
H
in the augmented model,
[
FGP20
]
presents a sublinear-time algorithm with the same time and query complexity as
[
AKK18
]
, and a matching lower bound for query complexity of sampling almost uniform
k
-cliques.

Our work focuses on the regime where sublinear query complexity is unattainable, for example when
\frac{m^{\rho}(H)}{t}\geq m
. We provide algorithms that depend on
n
the number of vertices, while being output-sensitive. The techniques we use are very different.

1.2
Technical Overview

The Framework.

Our algorithm is based on the following very general framework (consider the
k
-clique counting problem).
We sample (induced) subgraphs, check whether they contain at least one
k
-clique using a detection oracle, and use this information to approximate the number of
k
-cliques in the graph.
To analyze the complexity of the algorithm, we need to bound the number of queries to the oracle and the time it takes to answer each query.
The question of approximating the number of
k
-cliques in a graph, or more generally the number of hyperedges in a
k
-partite hypergraph, while minimizing the number of queries to the oracle, was previously studied in
[
BBGM24
,
DLM22
,
DLM24
]
, which roughly showed that the number of queries is
O_{k}(\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right)/\varepsilon^{2})
.
However, the size of the sets that are queried is not taken into consideration in the above work.
Therefore, because there is no known
k
-detection oracle that is faster than an exact counting algorithm, the obtained algorithm for
k
-clique approximate counting is not faster than the exact counting version.
In a recent work
[
DLM24
]
, the authors provided a lower bound on the total running time of the algorithm for approximately counting the number of hyperedges in a
k
-partite hypergraph using a detection oracle, where every query on a set
U
has cost that is proportional to
\left\lvert U\right\rvert
.

We develop a new framework for approximately counting the number of hyperedges in a
k
-partite hypergraph, using a detection oracle which is sensitive to the number of hyperedges in the graph.
This allows us to provide a new algorithm for approximating the number of
k
-cliques in a graph
G
with
n
vertices, assuming the graph has
m

k
-cliques, which is faster than both the matrix multiplication approach and the naïve sampling algorithm, and also provides a negative dependency on the number of
k
-cliques.

To do so, we restrict the size of the sets that are queried with respect to some measure
\mu
, which we define as follows.

The Measure
\mu
.

For a
k
-partite hypergraph
G
with vertex set
V=V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V_{k}
, and a set of hyperedges
E
containing
m
hyperedges, we define
\mu(U)
by
\mu(U)\triangleq\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left\lvert U\cap V_{i}\right\rvert
.
To get some intuition on this measure, we look at the naïve sampling algorithm that samples
k
vertices at random and queries the oracle on the sampled vertices to learn whether they form a hyperedge or not.
By querying the oracle
\tilde{O}(\frac{n^{k}}{m\varepsilon^{2}})
times, we can approximate the number of hyperedges in
G
.
The number of queries is very large, and the measure
\mu
of the queried set is constant.
On the other hand, in
[
DLM22
,
DLM24
,
BBGM24
]
they achieve an algorithm with a polylogarithmic number of queries, while querying sets with possibly maximum measure of
\mu(V)
.

In comparison, our approach gives a polylogarithmic number of queries, while only querying sets
U
that satisfy
\mu(U)\leq\mu(V)/m
,
which is comparable to the number of queries of the naïve sampling algorithm.
In other words, we switch from
\tilde{O}(n^{k}/m)
many queries of constant measure to a polylogarithmic number of queries and of measure at most
\tilde{O}(n^{k}/m)
.
Note that for the case of
k
-cliques, one could implement a detection oracle that answers queries on sets of
k
vertices in constant time:
Given a set
U\subseteq V
with
\mu(U)\leq M
, one could trivially implement the detection oracle in time
O(k^{2}\cdot M)
, by iterating over all possible
k
-tuple of vertex sets hyperedges in
G
and counting the number of
k
-tuples that form a hyperedges.
However, to benefit from the new framework, we must provide a faster implementation of the detection oracle, which means it has to be
sublinear
in the measure of the set.
To this end, we show that if the detection oracle takes time
T
to answer the query on the entire vertex set
V
, then for every set
U\subseteq V
with
\mu(U)\leq\mu(V)/N
, the detection oracle can be implemented on
U
in time
O(T/N^{\Omega(1)})
.

To conclude, we provide a new algorithm for approximating the number of
k
-cliques in a
k
-partite graph
G
with
n
vertices, assuming the graph has
m

k
-cliques, where
k
is a constant, by reducing the problem to implementing a fast detection oracle on sets
U
with
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/m)
, where the oracle is queried at most
O(\mathsf{poly}(\log n,\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}))
times.
We emphasize that we do not require that the queried sets have smaller cardinality, and in fact we query sets of size
\Omega(n)
.

In addition, we show that if our detection oracle is optimal, then so is the approximate counting algorithm we obtain.
In other words, assume there exists an algorithm
A
that computes a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for the number of
k
-cliques in a graph
G
that takes time
T
, assuming the graph has at least
m

k
-cliques.
Then, we can implement a detection oracle for
k
-cliques on sets
U
with
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/m)
in time
\tilde{O}(T)
.
This detection oracle can be used to obtain an approximate counting algorithm for
k
-cliques that takes time
\tilde{O}(T)
.
In other words, the framework we present is sufficiently strong to get an optimal approximate counting algorithm for the
k
-clique problem, and reducing the problem to implementing a detection oracle for
k
-cliques on sets
U
with
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/m)
, is in some sense inherent.

The Sampling Lemma.

Recall that our algorithm is based on the following very general framework, which samples (induced) subgraphs, checks whether they contain at least one
k
-clique, and use this information to approximate the number of
k
-cliques in the graph.
In what follows we explain according to which distributions we sample the subgraphs, and how these distributions are related to the measure
\mu
.
Let
G=(V,E)
be a
k
-partite hypergraph with
m
edges.
We define the following asymmetric sampling, which is tight in a sense we explain shortly.
For every vector
P\in[0,1]^{k}
, with
P=(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k})
, we define the weight of the vector
P
by
w(P)\triangleq\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}
.
We sample a subset of vertices
V[P]
using the vector
P
by adding to
V[P]
every vertex
v\in V_{i}
independently with probability
p_{i}
.
The sampling lemma states that if
G
has at least
\Omega\left(m\cdot\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right)\right)
hyperedges, then there exists a vector
P
with
w(P)\leq 1/m
, such that the probability that
G[V[P]]
contains a hyperedge is at least
\Omega\left(1\right)
.
This is the best that we can hope for, as the expected number of hyperedges in
G[V[P]]
is
m\cdot w(P)=\tilde{O}(1)
.
In fact it provides us with something stronger. For every vertex
v
with degree at least
\Lambda\cdot\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right)
, there exists a vector
P
with
w(P)\leq 1/\Lambda
, such that the probability that
v
is non-isolated in
G[V[P]]
is at least
(1-1/e)^{k}
. Moreover, for every vertex
v
with degree less than
\Lambda
, and any vector
P
with
w(P)\leq 1/\Lambda
, we have that the probability that
v
is non-isolated in
G[V[P]]
is at most
1/4^{k}
.
This allows us to distinguish between vertices with high degree and vertices with low degree, which is a crucial step in our algorithm, on which we elaborate later.

While the space of possible vectors
P
is infinite, we show that it suffices to consider only a finite subset of
\log^{k}(4n)
vectors. In particular, it suffices to consider only the set of vectors
P=(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k})
such that
p_{i}=2^{-j}
for every
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\log n+1\right\}
. We call such a vector a
simple
vector. An important observation is the following connection between
\mu
and the Sampling Lemma: For any sampling vector
P
, the set
V[P]
has measure
\mu(V[P])=\mu(V)\cdot w(P)
.

A similar version of this sampling lemma was used in
[
DLM22
]
together with a detection oracle for hyperedge making only a polylogarithmic number of queries. The number of queries was later improved in
[
DLM24
]
. However, both of these approaches don’t lead to queries on subgraphs of reduced measure, and therefore are not applicable for our use.
Following the approach of
[
CEV24
]
do lead to queries on subgraphs of reduced measure, but there a stronger detection oracle is used, which returns the degree of each vertex.

Implementation of the Detection Oracle.

An additional crucial step in our algorithm is how to implement the detection oracle on sets
U
obtained by sampling subset of vertices from
V
using a vector
P
with
w(P)\leq 1/m
, and therefore with
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/m)
.
Moreover, how can we do so in time that is sublinear in the size of
\mu(U)
?
Intuitively, how can we benefit from the fact that the set
U
is partitioned into
k
parts of possibly very different sizes?

For example for
k
-cliques, to do so, we modify a well-known reduction from
k
-clique detection to triangle detection
[
NP85
]
.
The original reduction from
k
-clique detection starting from an
n
-node forms a new auxiliary graph whose vertices are those
k/3
-tuples that are also
k/3
cliques (if
k/3
is not an integer, there are three types of nodes:
\lceil k/3\rceil
,
\lfloor k/3\rfloor
and
k-\lceil k/3\rceil-\lfloor k/3\rfloor
-cliques).
Then two
k/3
-cliques (i.e. nodes in the auxiliary graph) are connected by an edge if and only if together they form a
2k/3
clique.

Our reduction is similar but with a twist. We start with a
k
-partite graph on parts
U_{1},\ldots,U_{k}
and instead of creating nodes corresponding to cliques of size
k/3
, we consider different sized cliques which have nodes from a pre-specified subset of the
U_{i}
s.
In other words, first a sampling vector is chosen, and then the sets
U_{i}
are partitioned into
3
parts, were the partition attempts to keep all parts of the same size, or at least minimize the difference between the sizes of the parts.

With a similar framework in mind, we provide implementations of a detection oracle also for
k
-dominating set, and
k
-sum, and show that the running time of the detection oracle on sets
U
with
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/m)
is sublinear in
\mu(U)
.

This simple idea can also be applied to the
k
-dominating set problem, and
k
-sum problem, because the optimal detection oracle for these problems follows the same structure as the detection oracle for
k
-cliques.
In other words, both problems have an algorithm that partitions the set
[k]
into
2
sets
I_{1}
, and
I_{2}
, and constructs the sets
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
where
W_{1}=\bigtimes_{j\in I_{1}}U_{j}
, and
W_{2}=\bigtimes_{j\in I_{2}}U_{j}
.
In the language of
k
-cliques, we partitioned the set
[k]
into
3
sets, and constructed the sets
W_{1}
,
W_{2}
, and
W_{3}
, and removed from
W_{j}
any element that is not a
\left\lvert I_{j}\right\rvert
-clique.
We conclude that any problem with an algorithm with such a structure can benefit from our framework.
Another example of a problem that can benefit from our framework is the
k
-cycle problem, as seen in
[
CEV24
]
.

We elaborate on how to get a fast detection oracle on sets
U
with
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/m)
, where the running time is sublinear in the size of
\mu(U)
, in
Section

7
.

Approximate Counting Hyperedges Using Oracle Detection.

The second part of this paper is dedicated to proving
Theorem

2
– How to approximate the number of hyperedges in a
k
-partite hypergraph
G=(V,E)
with
n
vertices and
m
hyperedges using a detection oracle, where only sets
U
with
\mu(U)\leq\tilde{O}(n^{k}/m)
are queried, and the oracle is queried at most
O(\mathsf{poly}(\log n,\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}))
times.
We highlight the techniques and tools we use in what follows.

The Recursive Algorithm.

We now explain how these detection oracles are used.
The recursive algorithm is an algorithm that takes as input a set of vertices
V
, of a
k
-partite hypergraph
G
, and computes a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for the number of hyperedges in
G
, using a detection oracle.
Given an algorithm that produces an approximation
\hat{m}
that falls within the interval
m\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon)
with probability of at least
2/3
, we can get an algorithm that produces an approximation
\hat{m}
such that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}=m(1\pm\varepsilon)\right]\geq 1-1/n^{4}
, by taking the median of
10\log n
independent executions of the algorithm.
Therefore, we focus on getting an algorithm that succeeds with probability of at least
2/3
.
We also assume that the algorithm is given a coarse approximation
for
m
the number of hyperedges in
G
, and the algorithm is required to output an approximation
\hat{m}
such that
\hat{m}=m\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon)
with probability of at least
2/3
.
This assumption can be removed, and we explain how to do so later on.

The main goal of the recursive algorithm is to compute a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for the number of hyperedges in a
k
-partite hypergraph
G=(V,E)
with
n
vertices and
m
hyperedges, by querying the oracle only
\tilde{O}(1)
times, while only querying sets
U
that satisfy
\mu(U)\leq\tilde{O}(n^{k}/m)
.
Recall that we say that a hypergraph is
k
-partite if the vertex set
V
is partitioned into
k
disjoint sets
V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{k}
, and every hyperedge
e\in E
intersects every vertex set
V_{i}
in exactly one vertex.

A tempting (but unsuccessful) approach for obtaining a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m
is to sample a subset of vertices
U
by sampling each vertex
v\in V
independently with probability
p
, and to compute an approximation
\hat{m}
for
m
based on
m_{U}
the number of hyperedges in the subhypergraph
G[U]
.
This approach fails because the probability that a hyperedge
e_{1}
is in
G[U]
is not independent of the probability that another hyperedge
e_{2}
is in
G[U]
, when
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
share a vertex.
Moreover, the variance of the random variable
m_{U}
is of order of
O(mp\cdot\Delta)
, where
\Delta
is the maximum degree of a vertex in
G
.
In other words,
\mathsf{Var}\left[m_{U}\right]=O(\mathbb{E}\left[m_{U}\right]\cdot\Delta)
.
Using this observation, we can explain the main goal of our recursive algorithm.
We say that a vertex
v
is
\Lambda
-heavy if its degree is at least
\Lambda
, and we say that it is
\Lambda
-light otherwise.
We say that a hyperedge is
\Lambda
-heavy if it contains at least one
\Lambda
-heavy vertex, and it is
\Lambda
-light otherwise.
The recursive algorithm approximates the heavy hyperedges and the light hyperedges separately, and outputs the sum of the approximations.
To approximate the number of
\Lambda
-heavy hyperedges, it finds a superset of the
\Lambda
-heavy vertices
V_{\Lambda}
and approximates the number of
\Lambda
-heavy hyperedges, which are the hyperedges that contain at least one vertex from
V_{\Lambda}
.
The procedure for finding the heavy vertices is called
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
, and the procedure for approximating the number of heavy hyperedges is called
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
. Both procedures heavily relay on the detection oracle, as they do not have any other way to access the hyperedges of the graph.

After finding the heavy vertices and approximating the number of heavy hyperedges, the algorithm removes the set
V_{\Lambda}
from the graph, and approximates the number of hyperedges in the subhypergraph
G[V\setminus V_{\Lambda}]
, which contains only
\Lambda
-light vertices.
To approximate the number of light hyperedges, it samples a subset of vertices
U
by sampling each vertex
v\in V\setminus V_{\Lambda}
independently with probability
p=1/2
, and approximates the number of hyperedges in
G[U]
.
To approximate the number of hyperedges in the new sampled induced hypergraph
G[U]
, the algorithm recursively approximates the number of
\Lambda^{\prime}
-heavy and light hyperedges in
G[U]
, with
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda\cdot p^{k}
.

These two steps are repeated recursively until the graph becomes sufficiently small and has only a polylogarithmic number of hyperedges.
At this point, the maximum degree of the graph is at most polylogarithmic in
n
, and the dependencies between the vertices are limited.
Let
W
denote the set of vertices of the graph
G
at this point.
We switch to the algorithm presented in
[
DLM22
]
that we refer to as
\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}(W,\varepsilon)
(detailed in
Theorem

1
), which approximates the number of hyperedges in the graph
G[W]
by querying the oracle at most
O(\log^{3k+5}n/\varepsilon^{2})
times, while querying sets
U
with
\mu(U)=\mu(W)
.
We show that at this point, the number of remaining vertices in
W
is sufficiently small, so that
\mu(W)\leq\mu(V)/m
.
The algorithm switches to this algorithm as soon as the heaviness threshold
\Lambda
is smaller or equal to
1
.
We also note that using a faster algorithm for this case would not result in an improved running time, and therefore is not considered.

The key observation of this recursive framework is that the two steps have the opposite effect on the variance of the random variable
m_{U}
.
Removing the heavy vertices in each step before sampling a new graph ensures that the variance of the random variable
m_{U}
is not too large, and that we get a good estimation with probability
1-1/\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right)
at each step.
Sampling a new graph increases the variance of the random variable
m_{U}
, but allows us to work with a smaller graph.
The reason that we need to work with a smaller graph is that we gradually decrease the heaviness threshold, and finding the
\Lambda
-heavy vertices becomes more expensive as
\Lambda
decreases.
To make sure that the cost of every level in the recursion is at most the cost of the previous level, we need to sample a subhypergraph with fewer vertices.

On a more intuitive level, the recursive algorithm tries to split the vertices into buckets by their degree, and approximates the sum of the degrees of each bucket separately.
Splitting into buckets here is helpful because given a set of vertices
S
that have degree within the interval
[d,2d]
, one can approximate the sum of the degrees of vertices in
S
by approximating the degree of only
O(\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right))
many vertices from
S
. This is done by approximating the average degree of the vertices in
S
, and multiplying it by
\left\lvert S\right\rvert
.
While the sum of the degrees and the number of hyperedges in the graph, which include at least one vertex from
S
, are not identical, they are related. By applying a few additional techniques, we can also approximate the number of these hyperedges using a similar method.
So if we could partition the vertices into buckets, then we could approximate the degree of only
O(\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right))
vertices from each bucket, and get an approximation for the number of hyperedges in the graph.
However, constructing the buckets is not trivial, and could be too expensive.
Therefore, we avoid constructing these buckets explicitly, and we instead build them “on the fly” as follows.
We first construct the heaviest bucket which contains the set of all
\Lambda_{0}=m\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{\log n}
-heavy vertices.

Let
V_{\Lambda_{0}}
be the vertices that are in the first bucket.
We then compute an approximation
\hat{m}_{0}
for the number of hyperedges that intersect this bucket, which means that they contain at least one vertex from this set, and remove the set
V_{\Lambda_{0}}
from the
V
.
Computing the approximation is done by computing an approximation for the average degree of the vertices in
V_{\Lambda_{0}}
, and multiplying it by
\left\lvert V_{\Lambda_{0}}\right\rvert
.
We then sample a new vertex set
U_{1}
that contains every vertex
v\in V-V_{\Lambda_{0}}
independently with probability
p=1/2
.
In the following iteration, we construct the next bucket that contains the set of all
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{0}\cdot 2^{-k}
-heavy vertices, and use it to
compute an approximation
\hat{m}_{i}
for the number of hyperedges that intersect this bucket. As before, we do so by approximating the average degree of the vertices in this set, and multiplying it by
\left\lvert V_{\Lambda_{i}}\right\rvert
.
We continue in this manner for
\log n
steps, and output the approximation
\sum_{i=0}^{\log n}\hat{m}_{i}\cdot 2^{i}
.
The key observation here is that instead of constructing the buckets explicitly and then sampling
O(\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right))
vertices from each bucket,
we can construct the first bucket, and then sample half of the vertices and then construct the next bucket, and so on.

The formal property that we prove is that if a bucket is “important”, i.e., initially at least
\Omega(m\cdot\varepsilon/\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right))
hyperedges intersect it,
then the approximation
\hat{m}_{i}
is a good approximation for the number of hyperedges that intersect this bucket, with probability of at least
1-1/\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right)
, which means we can use a union bound over all (important) buckets to show that the sum of the approximations is a good approximation for the number of hyperedges in the graph.

To the best of our knowledge, this framework was used first in
[
DL21
]

for the significantly simpler case of bipartite graphs (i.e.
k=2
), and in

[
Tět22
]
for approximately counting the number of triangles in a graph (
k=3
).
In
[
CEV24
]
the running time for approximating the number of triangles was improved, where the paper also extends this framework for approximate counting of
k
-cycles in a graph.

Challenges (Compared to the Previous Work).

There are several key differences between our work and the previous work of
[
CEV24
]
.
First, as previously discussed, we use the sampling lemma in a different way.
Moreover, in
[
CEV24
]
the paper uses an access to a special stronger type of oracle, that takes a subset of vertices
U
as input and outputs the degree of
every vertex
in
U
over the subhypergraph
G[U]
.
Moreover, this oracle can exactly compute the degree of every set of
n^{\alpha}
vertices, over the entire hypergraph
G
, or any subhypergraph
G[U]
, in time
O(n^{2+o(1)})
. Recall that
\alpha\geq 0.321334

[
ADV+25
]
is the largest known value s.t.
\omega(1,\alpha,1)=2
.
In contrast, we only assume we have access to a detection oracle that takes a subset of vertices
U
as input and informs us whether
G[U]
contains a hyperedge or not.

This raises two new challenges in the implementations of the two procedures
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
and
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
which lie at the heart of the recursive algorithm.
The
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
procedure takes as input a subset of vertices
U
and a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
, and with probability of at least
1-1/n^{4}
outputs a superset
V_{\Lambda}
of all vertices of degree at least
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}=\tfrac{\Lambda}{4^{k}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}}
in
G[U]
, that contains all vertices of degree at least
\Lambda
in
G[U]
.
In
[
CEV24
]
, this procedure involved sampling a subset of vertices
U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{r}
, for
r=\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right)
, according to some distribution, and adding to
V_{\Lambda}
every vertex
v
that was non isolated (had a positive degree) at least
\tau
times in
G[U_{i}]
, for every
i\in[r]
.
The challenge here is that this method relied on a stronger oracle that could retrieve the entire set of vertices of non-zero degree in each sample. However, in our work, we can find the non-zero degree vertices only when the number of such vertices is very small.
That is, we develop a deterministic tool to find the set
S
of all non-zero degree vertices over
G[U]
using
O(\left\lvert S\right\rvert\cdot k\log n)
queries to the oracle. Therefore, we must ensure that the distribution we use to sample the vertices
U_{i}
is such that the number of non-zero degree vertices in
G[U_{i}]
is very small in sufficiently many samples.

The
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
procedure takes two subsets of vertices
U
and
V_{\Lambda}
, together with a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
and a precision parameter
\varepsilon
, and with probability of at least
1-1/n^{4}
, outputs an approximation for the number of hyperedges in
G[U]
that contain at least one vertex from
V_{\Lambda}
.
We denote this set of edges by
E_{U}(V_{\Lambda})
and its cardinality by
m_{U}(V_{\Lambda})
.
Computing an
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{U}(V_{\Lambda})
using the stronger oracle, can be used by sampling a subset
S
of
r=\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right)
from
V_{\Lambda}
uniformly at random, and computes the exact degree of each vertex in
S
over
G[U]
using the stronger oracle, which gives us an estimation
\hat{d}_{S}
for the average degree of vertices in
S
over
G[U]
. Using
\hat{d}_{S}
one can get a coarse approximation for
m_{U}(V_{\Lambda})
by computing
\hat{d}_{S}\cdot\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert
.
This coarse approximation can be refined using additional tools.

In our case, with access only to the weaker oracle, even computing the approximate degree of a single vertex is not trivial, and requires new tools.
To approximate the degree of a vertex
v
in a
k
-partite hypergraph
G
, we use the following recursive approach. Define a
(k-1)
partite hypergraph
G^{\prime}
, where the vertex set is
V^{\prime}=V\setminus V_{\ell}
, where
V_{\ell}
is such that
v\in V_{\ell}
, and the hyperedges of
G^{\prime}
are defined as follows.
For every hyperedge
e
in
G
, if
e
contains
v
, then
e\setminus\left\{v\right\}
is added to
G^{\prime}
.
We then apply our algorithm to approximate the number of hyperedges in
G^{\prime}

using a detection oracle. We can simulate a query
U
on
G^{\prime}
by querying the oracle on
U\cup\left\{v\right\}
on
G
.
The problem with this approach is that while
G
had many hyperedges, and therefore we could get a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation fast, the graph
G^{\prime}
might have very few hyperedges, and then computing the number of hyperedges in
G^{\prime}
would be too slow.
For that reason we slightly change our approach, and develop a new approximation algorithm called
\mathbf{GuessApx}
which takes as input a set of vertices
U
and a heaviness threshold
L
.
The algorithm either outputs a value
\hat{m}
which serves as an approximation for the number of hyperedges
m
in
G[U]
or outputs a message
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
indicating that the number of hyperedges in
G[U]
is too small to complete the computation of a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{U}
.
In other words, this algorithm has the guarantee that its running time is bounded by the running time on graph with at least
\Omega\left(L\right)
hyperedges.
Moreover, if
L\leq m_{V}/2
, then the algorithm outputs a value
\hat{m}
which is a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{U}
with probability of at least
1-1/n^{6}
.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notation

For the entire paper,
G
will denote a
k
-partite hypergraph with vertex set
V^{\mathrm{input}}=(V^{\mathrm{input}}_{1},V^{\mathrm{input}}_{2},\ldots,V^{\mathrm{input}}_{k})
, and hyperedges
E
. Each hyperedge
e\in E
intersects every vertex set
V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}
in exactly one vertex.
For subset of vertices
A\subseteq V^{\mathrm{input}}
we define the hyperedge set
E_{A}
by

E_{A}\triangleq\{e\in E\mid v\in e\implies v\in A\}\;.

In words,
E_{A}
is a subset of hyperedges from
E
, where every
e\in E
is also in
E_{A}
if and only if all vertices that are in
e
are also in
A
.
For two vertex sets
A,B
we use
E_{A}(B)
to denote the subset of hyperedges in
E_{A}
, where every
e\in E_{A}
is also in
E_{A}(B)
if and only if at least one vertex in
e
is also in
B
.
We also
m_{A}(B)
to denote the number of hyperedges in
E_{A}(B)
, where
m\triangleq m_{V^{\mathrm{input}}}(V^{\mathrm{input}})
.
For a single vertex
v
, we use
d_{U}(v)
to denote the number of hyperedges in
E_{U}(v)
, and
d(v)
to denote
d_{V^{\mathrm{input}}}(v)
.
For a vertex set
U
, we define the induced subhypergraph
G[U]
as a hypergraph with vertex set
U
and hyperedge set
E_{U}
.
The set of non-isolated vertices over
G[U]
is denoted by
A_{U}
.
That is,
A_{U}
is the set of vertices in
U
that have non-zero degree over
G[U]
.
We say that a vertex
v
is
\Lambda
-heavy if
d(v)\geq\Lambda
, and
\Lambda
-light otherwise. We say that an hyperedge
e
is
\Lambda
-heavy if it contains at least one
\Lambda
-heavy vertex, and
\Lambda
-light otherwise.
Note that a
k
-partite hypergraph
G
with
m
hyperedges contains at most
km/\Lambda
vertices that are
\Lambda
-heavy, where this is sometimes referred to as the handshaking lemma.

We define a measure
\mu
from every
U\subseteq V^{\mathrm{input}}
, by
\mu(U)\triangleq\prod_{i=1}^{k}|U\cap V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}|
. We refer to
\mu(U)
as the
query measure
of
U
.
We say that an algorithm with oracle access has query measure at most
R
if it only queries the oracle on sets
U
for which
\mu(U)\leq R
.

Throughout the paper,
\varepsilon
denotes a fixed precision parameter, where we assume that
\varepsilon\leq 1/4
. If this is not the case, we can always replace
\varepsilon
with
\min\left\{\varepsilon,1/4\right\}
.
We also use
\varepsilon^{\prime}
defined as
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\min\left\{1,\varepsilon\right\}}{4\log n}
.

We use standard notation for the matrix multiplication exponents
\omega(a,b,c)
to denote the smallest real number
w
so that
n^{a}\times n^{b}
by
n^{b}\times n^{c}
matrices can be multiplied in
O(n^{w+\delta})
time for all
\delta>0
. As is standard in the literature, for simplicity, we omit
\delta
in the running time exponents of our algorithms and instead just write
\omega(a,b,c)
.
Equivalently, we also write
\mathsf{MM}\left(n^{a},n^{b},n^{c}\right)
for the running time of the matrix multiplication algorithm that multiplies
n^{a}\times n^{b}
by
n^{b}\times n^{c}
matrices. That is
n^{\omega(a,b,c)}
and
\mathsf{MM}\left(n^{a},n^{b},n^{c}\right)
denote the same running time.

2.2
Tools

We also distinguish between
\Lambda
-heavy vertices, which are vertices with degree at least
\Lambda
, and
\Lambda
-light vertices, which have degree at most
\Lambda
.
We say that a hyperedge is
\Lambda
-heavy if it contains at least one
\Lambda
-heavy vertex, and
\Lambda
-light otherwise.
Throughout the paper we use the following observation.
A
k
-partite graph
G
with
m
edges, contains at most
km/\Lambda
vertices that are
\Lambda
-heavy.

To compute the number of hyperedges in an induced subhypergraph
G[W]

that has only
\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right)
hyperedges, we use the algorithm of
[
DLM22
,
BBGM24
]
, which we refer to as
\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}(W,\varepsilon)
.
This algorithm outputs a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for the number of hyperedges in the graph
G[W]
, while minimizing the number of queries to the oracle. However, it queries the oracle on set with maximum measure
\mu(W)
, and therefore we use this algorithm only when
\mu(W)\leq\mu(V)/m
.

Theorem 9

(
[
DLM22
,
BBGM24
]
)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a
k
-partite hypergraph with
n
vertices.
There exists a randomized algorithm, denoted by
\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}(V,\varepsilon)
.

\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}
outputs
\hat{m}
such that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)\right]\geq 1-1/n^{4}\;.

The algorithm makes
\frac{(k\log n)^{O(k)}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
queries to a CID-query oracle.

Probabilistic Bounds.

Let
V=V_{1}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V_{k}
be a set.
Let
V_{p}
denote a random subset of
V
where each element in
V
is included independently with probability
p
.
We prove a concentration bound on the size of
V_{p}\cap V_{i}
, and on the size of
\mu(V_{p})
, which as we previously mentioned is defined by
\mu(V_{p})=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left\lvert V_{p}\cap V_{i}\right\rvert
.

Claim 10
.

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mu(V_{p})\geq\mu(V)\cdot(p\cdot 6\log n)^{k}\right]\leq k/n^{6}\;.

Proof.
We first prove that for every
i\in[k]
, it holds that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\left\lvert V_{p}\cap V_{i}\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert V_{i}\right\rvert p\cdot 6\log n\right]\leq 1/n^{6}\;.

This follows by Chernoff’s inequality, as
\left\lvert V_{p}\cap V_{i}\right\rvert
is the sum of
n
independent Bernoulli random variables with success probability
p
.
By union bound over
i\in[k]
, we get that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mu(V_{p})\geq\mu(V)\cdot(p\cdot 6\log n)^{k}\right]\leq k/n^{6}\;,

which completes the proof.
∎

3
The Recursive Algorithm

Organization.

This section is dedicate to proving the following theorem, which captures the correctness guarantee that we use in
Section

4
for proving
Theorem

2
. Informally, it shows that we can approximate the number of hyperedges given a good guess
L
that bounds it.

Theorem 11

(Approximating the number of hyperedges given a guess)
.

For a vertex set
V
, a value
L
, and a parameter
\varepsilon
, there exists an algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)

that produces either
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
or a value
\hat{m}_{V}
such that
\hat{m}_{V}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.
If
L\leq m_{V}/2
, then the probability that the algorithm outputs
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
is at most
1/n^{6}
.

This section starts with an overview of our algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
, which is based on the recursive framework of
[
Tět22
,
CEV24
]
. See
Lemma

12
for a formal statement on the output of the algorithm.
We then explain how to implement the recursive algorithm using two randomized procedures,
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
and
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\Lambda)
, which find the set of
\Lambda
-heavy vertices
V_{\Lambda}
and approximate
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
, respectively.
In
Section

3.1
we show that the recursive algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
outputs
\bot
with probability at most
1/\log n
.
In
Section

3.2
we prove
Theorem

11
, where the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
specified in
Theorem

11
can be viewed as an amplified version of the recursive algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
, that also employs a doubling technique to search for the right heaviness threshold.

As promised, we start with an overview of the recursive algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
.
We aim to get an algorithm for approximating the number of hyperedges in a
k
-partite (
k
-uniform) hypergraph, using a
Hyperedge-Oracle
, which is an oracle that detects whether a given set of vertices contains a hyperedge or not.

More formally, the input hypergraph
G
has
k
disjoint vertex sets
V^{\mathrm{input}}=V^{\mathrm{input}}_{1},\ldots,V^{\mathrm{input}}_{k}
and
a set of hyperedges
E
, where each hyperedge contains exactly
k
vertices and intersects
V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}
in exactly one vertex, for each
i\in[k]
.
We denote the number of vertices by
n
and the number of hyperedges by
m
.
The
Hyperedge-Oracle
takes as input a set of vertices
S\subseteq V^{\mathrm{input}}
. It outputs
1
if there exists an hyperedge
e\in E
such that
e\subseteq S
, and it outputs
0
otherwise.
The algorithm holds the set of vertices
V^{\mathrm{input}}
and has access to the
Hyperedge-Oracle
, but it can not access the hyperedges
E
directly, apart from using this oracle.

We provide a randomized algorithm for outputting a value
\hat{m}
that is a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m
w.h.p.
An important note that will be useful for the application of this approximation algorithm is that it only makes
\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right)
calls to the
Hyperedge-Oracle
,
while only querying the
Hyperedge-Oracle
with sets of vertices of
U
for which
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left\lvert U\cap V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}\right\rvert\leq\tilde{O}(n^{k}/m)
.
We define
\mu(U)\triangleq\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left\lvert U\cap V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}\right\rvert
, to denote this quantity.
We also note that instead of computing a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m
, we compute a
(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})
approximation for a smaller precision parameter
\varepsilon^{\prime}\triangleq\frac{\min\left\{\varepsilon,1\right\}}{4\log n}
. The value of
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is set such that the interval
(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{2\log n}
is contained in the interval
(1\pm\varepsilon/2)
.

The algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
takes as input two parameters: a set of vertices
V
and a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
.
We assume we have some coarse estimate
\Lambda_{0}=\Theta(m/\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right))
for the number of hyperedges in the graph.
Later, we explain how to obtain such an estimate using doubling.
Recall that we say that a vertex is
\Lambda_{0}
-heavy if it has degree at least
\Lambda_{0}
, and otherwise we say that the vertex is
\Lambda_{0}
-light.
We say that a hyperedge is
\Lambda_{0}
-heavy if it contains at least one
\Lambda_{0}
-heavy vertex, and we say that the edge is
\Lambda_{0}
-light otherwise.
The algorithm approximates the number of
\Lambda_{0}
-heavy hyperedges and the number of
\Lambda_{0}
-light hyperedges separately.

Let
m_{\Lambda_{0}}
denote the number of
\Lambda_{0}
-heavy hyperedges in the graph.
To approximate
m_{\Lambda_{0}}
, the algorithm finds a set of
\Lambda_{0}
-heavy vertices and uses it to compute an approximation
\hat{m}_{\Lambda_{0}}
for
m_{\Lambda_{0}}
.
To approximate the number of
\Lambda_{0}
-light hyperedges, the algorithm samples a subset of vertices
U
from
V
, by choosing each vertex independently with probability
p=1/2
. Then it computes a
(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})
approximation for the number of hyperedges in the random induced subhypergraph
F=G[U]
recursively, by invoking a recursive call
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(U,\Lambda_{0}\cdot p^{k})
. Let
m_{U}
denote the number of hyperedges in
F
, and let

\hat{m}_{U}
denote the output of the recursive call.
The algorithm then outputs
\hat{m}=\hat{m}_{\Lambda_{0}}+\hat{m}_{U}/p^{k}
as the approximation for
m
the number of hyperedges in
G
.

Our algorithm builds upon two procedures.
The first one, called
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
, is given a set of vertices
V
and a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
, and
finds a superset
V_{\Lambda}
of the
\Lambda
-heavy vertices without any
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices over
G
, for some
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}=O(\Lambda/\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right))

The second one, called
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
, takes as input the previously computed set
V_{\Lambda}
and approximates the number of hyperedges
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})

which is the number of hyperedges in
G
that contain at least one vertex in
V_{\Lambda}
.

In
Sections

5
and
6
, we provide details on how to implement these procedures using the
Hyperedge-Oracle
.
Formally, the procedures that we use are the following.
The
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
procedure is:

Define
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\triangleq\Lambda/(4^{k}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}})
.

Input:

A subset of vertices
V
and a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
.

Output:

A
subset of vertices
V_{\Lambda}\subseteq V
, such that with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{4}}
, for every
v\in V
:

1.

If
d_{V}(v)\geq\Lambda
, then
v\in V_{\Lambda}
.

2.

If
d_{V}(v)<\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
, then
v\notin V_{\Lambda}
.

If the set of vertices
V_{\Lambda}
outputted by the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}{(V,\Lambda)}
indeed does not contain any
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices, then it contains at most
k\cdot m_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
vertices.
This follows from the fact that for any subset of vertices
V
and any threshold
X
, the number of
X
-heavy vertices in
V
is at most
k\cdot m_{V}/X
.
The procedure makes at most
O(\psi\cdot k\cdot m_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}})
queries to the
Hyperedge-Oracle
, where
\psi\triangleq 4^{k}\cdot k^{2}\log(k)\cdot\log^{k+2}n
is a fixed parameter that depends on
k
and
n
.
We also define a parameter
\zeta
that will be set later.

We will use an additional procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}{(V,\Lambda)}
, which is a variant of
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}{(V,\Lambda)}
.
Informally, this procedure simulates the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}{(V,\Lambda)}
while only allowing it to make
O\left(\psi\cdot\zeta\right)
queries to the
Hyperedge-Oracle
.
If the simulation does not complete within
O\left(\psi\cdot\zeta\right)
steps, or it does complete but the computed set
V_{\Lambda}
contains more than
\zeta
vertices, then the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
outputs
\bot
.
Otherwise, it outputs the set
V_{\Lambda}
computed by the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
. These guarantees are summarize below.

Define
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\triangleq\Lambda/(4^{k}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}})
.

Input:

A subset of vertices
V
, a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
, and a threshold
\psi
.

Output:

Either
\bot
or a
subset of vertices
V_{\Lambda}\subseteq V
containing at most
\zeta
vertices,
such that with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{4}}
, for every
v\in V
:

1.

If
d_{V}(v)\geq\Lambda
, then
v\in V_{\Lambda}
.

2.

If
d_{V}(v)<\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
, then
v\notin V_{\Lambda}
.

If
k\cdot m_{V}/\Lambda\leq\zeta
, then the probability of outputting
\bot
is at most
1/n^{4}
.

Without an upper bound on the probability of outputting
\bot
, this procedure is useless, as it might output
\bot
on every input.
To avoid this, we require that if
km_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\leq\zeta
, then the probability of outputting
\bot
is at most
1/n^{4}
. Note that a
k
-partite hypergraph
G[V]
with
m_{V}
edges has no more than
k\cdot m_{V}/X
vertices which are
X
-heavy, for every positive
X
. Thus, the condition
km_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\leq\zeta
implies that the number of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy vertices in
V
is at most
\zeta
, and therefore that the number of
\Lambda
-heavy vertices in
V
is at most
\zeta\cdot\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/\Lambda
, which is also bounded by
\zeta
. Thus, the condition applies to smaller than
\zeta
values of the size of the set of
\Lambda
-heavy vertices, and hence does not contradict the requirement that if the set of
\Lambda
-heavy vertices contains at least
\zeta
vertices, then the procedure should output
\bot
.

Next, the
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
procedure is:

Input:

A vertex set
V
, a vertex set
V_{\Lambda}\subseteq V
, a heaviness threshold
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
, and a precision parameter
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

Output:

A value
\hat{m}_{\Lambda}
, such that
if every vertex
v\in V
satisfies
d_{V}(v)\geq\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
, then

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}_{\Lambda}=m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{n^{4}}
.

We now provide two implementations for the recursive algorithm, denoted by
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
and
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
.
The reason we use two versions is that the first implementation uses the efficient procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}{(V,\Lambda)}
to find a superset of the
\Lambda
-heavy vertices in
V
, while the second implementation is easier to analyze. We claim that if the first algorithm does not output
\bot
and both algorithms use the same randomness, then they produce the same output.

Choosing the Parameters.

We set the parameters as follows.
Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2]
be the precision parameter and let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\min\left\{1,\varepsilon\right\}}{4\log n}
.
We set
p=1/2
,
Q=5k\cdot 2^{k}\cdot\log^{3}n
,

\zeta=k\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}\cdot\frac{Q}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot\log^{4}n
,
and
\psi=4^{k}\cdot k^{2}\log(k)\cdot\log^{k+2}n
.
We keep the specified symbols instead of their assigned value for readability.

The algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
is as follows.

Algorithm 1

\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,\Lambda\right)

algorithm]alg:template recA

Input:

A vertex set
V
, a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
, and a precision parameter
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

1

V_{\Lambda}\leftarrow\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
;
\quad\triangleright

V_{\Lambda}
is a superset of
\Lambda
-heavy vertices in
G[V]
with no
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices w.h.p.

2

if

V_{\Lambda}=\bot

then

return

\bot
;

3

if

\Lambda\leq 1

then

4

return

\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}(V,\varepsilon^{\prime})
;
\triangleright\;

Approximate the number of hyperedges in
G[V]
using
Theorem

9
.

5

\hat{m}_{\Lambda}\leftarrow\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\tfrac{\Lambda}{2})

\quad\triangleright

\hat{m}_{\Lambda}
is a
(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})
approximation for
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
(the number of hyperedges intersecting
V_{\Lambda}
)

6

U\leftarrow V[p]-V_{\Lambda}

\quad\triangleright

U
is a random subset of
V-V_{\Lambda}

7

\hat{m}_{U}\leftarrow\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(U,\Lambda\cdot p^{k}\right)

8

return

\hat{m}_{\Lambda}+\hat{m}_{U}/p^{k}

We emphasize that if one recursive call of the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
outputs
\bot
then every preceding call, as well as the first call, also outputs
\bot
.

The algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}
is as follows.

Algorithm 2

\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(V,\Lambda\right)

algorithm]alg:template recB

Input:

A vertex set
V
, a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
, and a precision parameter
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

1

V_{\Lambda}\leftarrow\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)

\quad\triangleright

V_{\Lambda}
is a superset of
\Lambda
-heavy vertices in
G[U]
with no
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices w.h.p.

2

if

\Lambda\leq 1

then

3

return

\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}(V,\varepsilon^{\prime})
;
\triangleright\;

Approximate the number of hyperedges in
G[V]
using
Theorem

9
.

4

\hat{m}_{\Lambda}\leftarrow\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\tfrac{\Lambda}{2})

\quad\triangleright

\hat{m}_{\Lambda}
is a
(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})
approximation for
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
(the number of hyperedges intersecting
V_{\Lambda}
)

5

U\leftarrow V[p]-V_{\Lambda}

\quad\triangleright

U
is a random subset of
V-V_{\Lambda}

6

\hat{m}_{U}\leftarrow\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(U,\Lambda\cdot p^{k}\right)

7

return

\hat{m}_{\Lambda}+\hat{m}_{U}/p^{k}

Informally, the guarantees of the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
are as follows.
It outputs either
\bot
or an approximation
\hat{m}
that falls inside the interval
m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)\pm O_{k}(\log^{3}n\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime})
with probability at least
1-1/\log n
.
Moreover, if
\frac{k\cdot m_{V}}{\Lambda}\leq\frac{\zeta}{\log^{4}n}
, then the algorithm outputs
\bot
with probability at most
1/\log^{3}n
.
The formal guarantees of the algorithm are specified in the following lemma.

Lemma 12

(Guarantees for the
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
Algorithm)
.

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1/4]
,
the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
outputs either
\bot
or a value
\hat{m}_{V}
such that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)=m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm Q\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{\log n}\;,

where
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\min\left\{1,\varepsilon\right\}}{4\log n}
, and
Q=5k\cdot 2^{k}\cdot\log^{3}n
.
If
\frac{k\cdot m_{V}}{\Lambda}\leq\frac{\zeta}{\log^{4}n}
, then the probability that the algorithm outputs
\bot
is at most
2/\log^{3}n
.

We explain how
Lemma

12
is used to prove
Theorem

11
.
The first tool we need is a method to amplify the probability that the recursive algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
, specified in
Lemma

12
, outputs a value
\hat{m}
that falls within the interval
I_{\Lambda}\triangleq m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm Q\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
For that, we use the median of means technique, a standard technique for amplifying the success probability of an algorithm.
This includes executing the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
for
\log n
times and taking the median of the outputs.
If too many executions yield
\bot
, then the algorithm outputs
\bot
.
Let
\mathbf{MedianApx}
denote this amplified algorithm, which we formally define in
Lemma

16
.
The output of
\mathbf{MedianApx}
is either
\bot
or falls inside the interval
I_{\Lambda}
for some
\Lambda
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.

The second tool we need is doubling, which is used to find the right heaviness threshold
\Lambda
.
We start with the maximal possible heaviness threshold
\Lambda=\mu(V)=n^{k}
, and invoke a call to
\mathbf{MedianApx}
with output
\hat{s}
.
If
\hat{s}=\bot
, then we set
\Lambda\leftarrow\Lambda/2
and invoke another call to
\mathbf{MedianApx}
with the new heaviness threshold.
We continue in this manner until we find a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
such that the output of
\mathbf{MedianApx}
, denoted by
\hat{s}
, is not
\bot
.
We then check if
\hat{s}\geq\rho\cdot\Lambda
, where
\rho
is a parameter we set later. If this condition is met, then we output
\hat{s}
as our final output.
Otherwise, we set
\Lambda\leftarrow\Lambda/2
and again invoke a call to
\mathbf{MedianApx}
.

This completes the description of how
Theorem

11
is derived using
Lemma

12
. In
Section

3.1
we prove
Lemma

12
and in
Section

3.2
we prove
Theorem

11
.

3.1
Analyzing the Recursive Algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}

To prove
Lemma

12
, we split the proof into two parts. First, we show that the probability of outputting
\bot
is small (
Lemma

13
).
Second, we analyze a slightly different algorithm, denoted by
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}
, which is detailed in
.
Recall that
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}
operates like
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
but uses the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}{(V,\Lambda)}
instead of
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}{(V,\Lambda)}
, and therefore never outputs
\bot
(
Lemma

15
).
Note that if the algorithms
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
and
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}
use the same randomness and the former does not output
\bot
, then the two algorithms produce the same output.
Therefore, to bound the probability that
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
outputs a good approximation, we analyze probability that
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}
outputs a good approximation and the probability that
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
does not output
\bot
.

In this subsection we prove that the probability that the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
outputs
\bot
is bounded by
O(1/\mathrm{polylog}\left(n\right))
as formally stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 13
.

Recall that
\zeta=k\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}\cdot\frac{Q}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot\log^{4}n
.
Define the event

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{\bot}}(V,\Lambda)\triangleq\left\{\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,\Lambda\right)=\bot\right\}
.
Then, for every
\Lambda
such that

\displaystyle\frac{k\cdot m_{V}}{\Lambda}\leq\frac{\zeta}{\log^{4}n}\;,

(1)

we have that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{\bot}}(V,\Lambda)\right]\leq\frac{2}{\log^{3}n}
.

We need the following definition of the depth of the recursive calls.

Definition 1

(
\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)
)
.

We define the depth of the call
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
by

\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)=\max\left\{\left\lceil\log_{1/p^{k}}(\Lambda)\right\rceil,0\right\}
, where
p=1/2
.

We can assume without the lost of generality that
\Lambda\leq n^{k-1}
,
as a
k
-partite hypergraph with
n
vertices has at most
n^{k}

hyperedges and maximal degree of
n^{k-1}
.
Therefore, there are no
(2n^{k-1})
-heavy vertices
and we get that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)+1\leq(k-1)\log n/\log(1/p^{k})+1\leq\log n+1-\log n/k\leq\log n\;.

Consider the recursive calls
\{\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(U_{i},\Lambda_{i}\right)\}_{i=0}^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)}
made by the recursive algorithm, where
U_{0}
and
\Lambda_{0}
are the values given to the initial invocation of
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
.
For every
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)\right\}
we use
U_{i}
to denote the set of sampled vertices in the
i
-th recursive call, and we use
\Lambda_{i}
as the heaviness threshold in the
i
-th recursive call, where
\Lambda_{i}=\Lambda_{i}\cdot p^{ki}
.
We use
m_{i}
to denote the exact number of hyperedges in
G[U_{i}]
, we use
R_{i}
to denote the ratio
k\cdot m_{i}/\Lambda_{i}
, and we also use
R_{\max}
to denote the maximum value of
R_{i}
over
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)\}
.
Before proving
Lemma

13
, we prove the following claim that upper-bounds the ratio
R_{i}
in each recursive call.

Claim 14
.

For every
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)\}
and every positive
X
, we have that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[R_{i}\geq R_{0}\cdot X\right]\leq 1/X
.

Proof of
Claim

14
.
We first prove that for every
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)\right\}
we have that
\mathbb{E}\left[m_{i}\right]\leq m_{0}\cdot p^{ik}
.
Since
R_{i}=k\cdot m_{i}/\Lambda_{i}
, we get that
\mathbb{E}\left[R_{i}\right]=k\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[m_{i}\right]/\Lambda_{i}\leq k\cdot m_{0}/\Lambda_{0}=R_{0}
.
We then use Markov’s inequality to get that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[R_{i}\geq R_{0}\cdot X\right]\leq 1/X
. To complete the proof, we therefore need to show that
\mathbb{E}\left[m_{i}\right]\leq m_{0}\cdot p^{ik}
.

For that, we define a random variable
s_{i}
as follows
Define
C_{0}\triangleq V
, and
C_{i+1}=C_{i}[p]
.
That is,
C_{i+1}
is obtained by sampling each vertex from
C_{i}
independently with probability
p
.
Recall that the set
U_{i+1}
is also obtained by sampling every vertex from a
subset
of
U_{i}
independently with probability
p
,
where this subset is defined as the set of vertices in
U_{i}
that were not identified as
\Lambda_{i}
-heavy in the
i
-th recursive call.
Let
s_{i}
denote a random variable that is equal to the number of hyperedges in
G[C_{i}]
.
We show that
\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}\right]=m_{0}\cdot p^{ki}
and that if we use the same randomness then
m_{i}
is smaller than
s_{i}
, which means that
\mathbb{E}\left[m_{i}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}\right]
, which completes the proof.

To see why
\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}\right]=m_{0}\cdot p^{ki}
, note that
\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i+1}\mid s_{i}=y\right]=y\cdot p^{k}
, and therefore
\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i+1}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}\right]\cdot p^{k}
, which means that
\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}\right]=s_{0}\cdot p^{ki}=m_{0}\cdot p^{ki}
.

Next, we show that
\mathbb{E}\left[m_{i}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}\right]
and therefore conclude that
\mathbb{E}\left[m_{i}\right]\leq m_{0}\cdot p^{ki}
.
As we previously explained, if we use the same randomness to sample the sets
U_{i}
and
C_{i}
, then it must be the case that
U_{i}\subseteq C_{i}
, and therefore the number of hyperedges in
G[U_{i}]
is at most the number of hyperedges in
G[C_{i}]
.
In other words, we showed that for every value
z\geq 0
, it holds that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[m_{i}\geq z\right]\leq{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[s_{i}\geq z\right]
, which implies that
\mathbb{E}\left[m_{i}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}\right]
, and therefore completes the proof.

∎We are now ready to prove
Lemma

13
, which bounds the probability that
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
outputs
\bot
.

Proof of
Lemma

13
.

We define two sequences of events.
For
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)\right\}
,
let
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}(i)
denote the event that
\{R_{i}\leq\zeta\}
, and
let
\mathcal{F}_{\bot}(i)
denote the event that the
i
-th recursive call to
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
does not output
\bot
. We also define the events

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\bot}\triangleq\left\{\bigcap_{i=0}^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)}\mathcal{F}_{\bot}(i)\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}\triangleq\left\{\bigcap_{i=0}^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}(i)\right\}\;.

Note that if the event
\mathcal{F}_{\bot}
occurs then no call to
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
outputs
\bot
, and therefore the algorithm does not output
\bot
. This means that the events
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{\bot}}(V,\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\bot}
are complements, i.e.,
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{\bot}}(V,\Lambda)\right]=1-{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{\bot}\right]
.
Using the definition of conditional probability, we have that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{\bot}\right]\geq{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{\bot}\mid\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}\right]\cdot{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}\right]\;.

We analyze the two terms on the right-hand side of the inequality.
Fix some
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)\right\}
.
Note that by the guarantee of the
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
procedure, we have that
if
R_{i}\leq\zeta
then the
i
-th call
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(U_{i},\Lambda_{i},\zeta)
does not output
\bot
with probability at least
1-1/n^{4}
.
In other words, if
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}(i)
occurs
\mathcal{F}_{\bot}(i)
occurs with probability at least
1-1/n^{4}
.
We therefore get that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{\bot}(i)\mid\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}(i)\right]\geq 1-1/n^{4}
.
We also get
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{\bot}\mid\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}\right]\geq 1-\log n/n^{4}
by a union bound over at most
\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)<\log n
events.

We are left with proving that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}\right]\geq 1-1/\log^{3}n
, which we show under the assumption that
R_{0}\leq\zeta/\log^{4}n
.
We have the following inequalities:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}(i)\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[R_{i}>\zeta\right]\leq{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[R_{i}>R_{0}\cdot\log^{4}n\right]\leq 1/\log^{4}n\;,

where the first inequality follows by the assumption that
R_{0}=k\cdot m_{U_{0}}/\Lambda_{0}\leq\zeta/\log^{4}n
, and the second inequality follows by
Claim

14
.
Therefore, we get that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}\right]\geq 1-1/\log^{3}n

by a union bound over at most
\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda_{0}\right)<\log n
events.
We plug in the computed values of
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{\bot}\mid\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}\right]
and
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}\right]
, and get that
