\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{\bot}\right]\geq{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{\bot}\mid\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}\right]\cdot{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{{\mathrm{ratio}}}\right]\geq\left(1-\frac{\log n}{n^{4}}\right)\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{\log^{3}n}\right)\geq 1-\frac{2}{\log^{3}n}\;.

As previously mentioned, we have
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{\bot}}(V,\Lambda)\right]=1-{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}_{\bot}\right]
. Therefore, we get that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{\bot}}(V,\Lambda)\right]\leq 2/\log^{3}n
, which completes the proof.
∎

Recall that if we run the algorithms
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
and
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}(V,\Lambda)
with the same randomness and
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
does not output
\bot
, then the two algorithms produce the same output.
Therefore, to bound the probability that the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
outputs a correct approximation it suffices to bound the probability that the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}(V,\Lambda)
outputs a correct approximation and the probability that the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
does not output
\bot
.
We already obtained an upper bound on the probability of the latter event in
Lemma

13
.
The next lemma provides us with an upper bound on the probability that the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}(V,\Lambda)
outputs a correct approximation.

Lemma 15

(Guarantees for the
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}
Algorithm)
.

For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2]
and every
\gamma\geq 1
, we have that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(V,\Lambda\right)=m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm\gamma\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right]\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot 2^{k}\cdot\log^{2}n}{\gamma}\;,

where
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\min\left\{1,\varepsilon\right\}}{4\log n}
.
Moreover, if
\Lambda\leq m_{V}\cdot\frac{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}{\gamma}
, we get that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(V,\Lambda\right)=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)\right]\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot 2^{k}\cdot\log^{2}n}{\gamma}\;.

The proof that the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
outputs a correct approximation follows the same line as in
[
CEV24
]
, and we therefore include it in the appendix (
Section

8
) for completeness.

Proof of
Lemma

12
using
Lemma

15
.

Lemma

13
guarantees that the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
outputs
\bot
with probability at most
2/\log^{3}n
assuming that

\frac{k\cdot m_{V}}{\Lambda}\leq\frac{\zeta}{\log^{4}n}
.
We are left with proving that if the output of
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
is not
\bot
, then the output falls inside the interval
m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm Q\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-1/\log n
.

Note that assuming that the output of
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
is not
\bot
, the output of
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
and
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}(V,\Lambda)
is the same.
Moreover, the probability that the output of
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}(V,\Lambda)
falls inside the interval
m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm\gamma\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}
is at least
1-\frac{4k\cdot 2^{k}\cdot\log^{2}n}{\gamma}
by
Lemma

15
.
Note that
1-\frac{4k\cdot 2^{k}\cdot\log^{2}n}{\gamma}
is at least
1-\frac{4}{5\log n}
, by plugging in
\gamma=Q=5k\cdot 2^{k}\cdot\log^{3}n
, we get that if the output of
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
is not
\bot
, then the probability that the output of falls inside the interval
m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm Q\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}
is at least
1-\frac{1}{\log n}
, which completes the proof.

∎

3.2
Amplifying the Recursive Algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}

We now show how to amplify the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
to get a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for the number of hyperedges in a
k
-partite hypergraph.
The main result in this subsection is as follows.

Informally, the algorithm either outputs an approximation
\hat{m}_{V}
for

m_{V}
or outputs
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
.
If
m_{V}\geq L
, then the algorithm outputs a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{V}
with high probability.
In case that
m_{V}<L
the algorithm could output the correct approximation, but will probably output
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
.
To implement the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
we need another algorithm ,
\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(V,L,\varepsilon\right)
, where
\mathbf{GuessApx}
is a wrapper around
\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(V,L,\varepsilon\right)
.
We state the guarantees of the algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(V,L,\varepsilon\right)
in the following lemma.

Lemma 16
.

Let
g(k)\triangleq(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}
.
The algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(V,L,\varepsilon\right)
either outputs
\bot
or a value
\hat{m}_{V}
such that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}_{V}=m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm L\varepsilon/8\right]\geq 1-1/n^{6}\;.

If
L\geq m_{V}/g(k)
, then the probability that the algorithm outputs
\bot
is at most
1/n^{7}
.

We explain how to prove
Theorem

11
using
Lemma

16
.

Proof of
Theorem

11
using
Lemma

16
.

We start with a description of the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
.

For
i=0
to
i=k\log n
:

1.

Let
\hat{m}_{i}\leftarrow\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(V,L\cdot 2^{i},\varepsilon\right)
.

2.

If
\hat{m}_{i}=\bot
, then continue to the next iteration.

3.

If
\hat{m}_{i}\geq L\cdot 2^{i}
, then output
\hat{m}_{i}
.

4.

Otherwise, if
\hat{m}_{i}<L\cdot 2^{i}
output
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
.

Output
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
.

Let
\hat{m}_{i}
denote the output of the
i
-th execution of the algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}
.
Denote by
s
the number of iterations that an execution of the algorithm does, and note that
s\leq k\log n+1
.
We denote the final output of the algorithm by
\hat{t}
, which is either
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
, or
\hat{m}_{s}
.
We say that an approximation
\hat{m}_{i}
is
good
if

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{i}=m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm(L\cdot 2^{i})\cdot\varepsilon/8\;.

For every
i\leq s
for which
L\cdot 2^{i}\geq m_{V}/g(k)
, let
\mathcal{F}(i)
denote the event that
\hat{m}_{i}
is good.
For every other
i\leq s
, that is, for which
L\cdot 2^{i}<m_{V}/g(k)
, let
\mathcal{F}(i)
denote the event that
\hat{m}_{i}
is good or that
\hat{m}_{i}=\bot
.
We make this distinction because if
i
satisfies
L\cdot 2^{i}<m_{V}/g(k)
,
then we can use the guarantee of
Lemma

16
, which states that the algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}
does not output
\bot
with high probability.
Let
\mathcal{F}
denote the intersection of the events
\mathcal{F}(i)
for every
i\leq s
.
We have that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]\geq 1-s/n^{7}
by
Lemma

16
and a union bound.
For the rest of this proof we assume that
\mathcal{F}
occurs.
The probability that
\mathcal{F}
does not occur is at most
s/n^{7}\leq 1/n^{6}
, and therefore we can ignore this event.

We prove the output
\hat{t}
is either
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
or
m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
, and that the algorithm completes its execution before iteration
k\log n
.
First, we show that there exists an index
i
for which
\hat{m}_{i}\neq\bot
.
For any
i
such that
L\cdot 2^{i}\geq m_{V}/g(k)
, we have that if
\mathcal{F}(i)
occurs, then by definition
\hat{m}_{i}\neq\bot
.
If
\hat{m}_{i}\neq\bot
, then the algorithm stops before iteration
(i+1)
and outputs either
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
or an approximation for
m_{V}
.
If the algorithm does not reach iteration
i
, then it outputs either
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
or an approximation for
m_{V}
in some iteration
j
for
j<i
.
We conclude that the algorithm stops before iteration
k\log n
as for
i=k\log n/2
we have that
L\cdot 2^{i}\geq m_{V}/g(k)
.

Next, we show that if
L\geq m_{V}/2
, then
\hat{t}\neq\bot
If
L\geq m_{V}/2
, and since we conditioned on
\mathcal{F}
then
\mathcal{F}(1)
occurs, then the output
\hat{m}_{1}
is good and therefore

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{1}=

\displaystyle m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm L\cdot\varepsilon/8

(2)

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm m_{V}\cdot\varepsilon/8

(3)

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)\;.

(4)

Moreover, we get that
\hat{m}_{1}\geq m_{V}(1-\varepsilon)\geq m_{V}/2\geq L
, which means that
\hat{m}_{1}\geq L
, and therefore
\hat{t}
the output of the algorithm is equal to
\hat{m}_{1}
.
Moreover, by
Equation

4
we get that
\hat{m}_{1}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
, and since
\hat{t}=\hat{m}_{1}
, we get that
\hat{t}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
.
This completes the proof that if
L\geq m_{V}/2
, then
\hat{t}\neq\bot
.

Finally, we show that if
\hat{t}=\hat{m}_{s}
, then
\hat{t}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
.
The case that
\hat{t}=\hat{m}_{s}
occurs only if
\hat{m}_{s}\geq L\cdot 2^{s}
, so it suffices to show that if
\hat{m}_{s}
is the output of
\mathbf{MedianApx}(V,L\cdot 2^{s},\varepsilon)
and
\hat{m}_{s}\geq L\cdot 2^{s}
, then
\hat{m}_{s}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
. If
\mathcal{F}
occurs, then
\hat{m}_{s}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
.

For this, we prove the following properties under the assumption that
\hat{m}_{s}
is good, i.e., that
\hat{m}_{s}=m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm(L\cdot 2^{i})\cdot\varepsilon/8
.

1.

If
2^{s}\cdot L\geq 4m_{V}
, then
\hat{m}_{s}<2^{s}\cdot L
.

2.

If
2^{s}\cdot L\leq 4m_{V}
, then
\hat{m}_{s}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
.

Before proving these properties, we explain why they imply that
\hat{t}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
.
By Property (2), we get that if
2^{s}\cdot L\leq 4m_{V}
then
\hat{m}_{s}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
, and therefore
\hat{t}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
.
We want to show that indeed
2^{s}\cdot L\leq 4m_{V}
.
To this end, we assume towards a contradiction that
2^{s}\cdot L>4m_{V}
. However, by Property (1) this would give that
\hat{m}_{s}<2^{s}\cdot L
, which is a contradiction to the assumption that
\hat{t}=\hat{m}_{s}
, because the latter can only occur when
\hat{m}_{s}\geq 2^{s}\cdot L
.
Thus, if
\hat{t}=\hat{m}_{s}
, then
\hat{t}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
. It remains to prove the two properties.

Proof of Property (1).

If
2^{s}L\geq 4m_{V}
then

m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\leq 2^{s}L(1+\varepsilon)/4
. Further, we condition on
\hat{m}_{s}
being good, which means that

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{s}

\displaystyle=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm L\varepsilon/8

\displaystyle\leq 2^{s}L(1+\varepsilon)/4+2^{s}L\varepsilon/8

\displaystyle\leq 2^{s}L\cdot((1+\varepsilon)/4+\varepsilon/8)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{s}L\cdot 3/4<2^{s}L\;,

where the penultimate inequality follows as
\varepsilon\leq 1/4
. This completes the proof of Property (1).

Proof of Property (2).

If
2^{s}L\leq 4m_{V}
then, since we condition on
\hat{m}_{s}
being good, we have

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{s}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm 2^{s}L\varepsilon/8\subseteq m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm m_{V}\varepsilon/2\subseteq m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)\;.

This completes the proof of Property (2), and therefore the proof that if
\hat{t}=\hat{m}_{s}
, then
\hat{t}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
.

To conclude, we have that the algorithm outputs either
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
or a value
\hat{t}
. The value
\hat{t}
is equal to
m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.
If
L\geq m_{V}/2
, then the probability that the algorithm outputs
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
is at most
1/n^{6}
.

∎It remains to prove
Lemma

16
.

Proof of
Lemma

16
.
We start with a description of the algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(V,L,\varepsilon\right)
.

1.

Set
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leftarrow\frac{\min\left\{1,\varepsilon\right\}}{4\log n}\;,\;\Lambda\leftarrow L\cdot{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}/Q\;,\;r\leftarrow 400\log n
.

2.

Make
r
independent calls to the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
, and denote the result of the
i
-th call by
\hat{m}_{i}
.

3.

Let
b
denote the number of calls that output
\bot
.
If
b\geq r/10
, then output
\bot
.

4.

Let
\hat{m}
denote the median of the
r-b
calls that do not output
\bot
, and output
\hat{m}
.

We first prove that if
L\geq m_{V}/g(k)
then the probability that the algorithm outputs
\bot
is at most
1/n^{7}
.
We show that if
L\geq m_{V}/g(k)
then

\displaystyle\frac{k\cdot m_{V}}{\Lambda}\leq\frac{\zeta}{\log^{4}n}\;.

(5)

Recall that by
Lemma

13
if
Equation

5
holds, then the probability that the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
outputs
\bot
is at most
2/\log^{3}n
.
Recall that
g(k)=(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}
,

\zeta=k\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}\cdot\frac{Q}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot\log^{4}n
, and therefore

\zeta=k\cdot\frac{Q}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot g(k)\cdot\log^{4}n
.
We therefore get that

\displaystyle\frac{k\cdot m_{V}}{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\zeta}{\log^{4}n}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\frac{k\cdot m_{V}}{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{k\cdot\frac{Q}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot g(k)\cdot\log^{4}n}{\log^{4}n}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\frac{m_{V}}{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Q}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot g(k)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\frac{m_{V}}{g(k)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Q}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot\Lambda\;.

Note that this completes the proof that if
L\geq m_{V}/g(k)
then
Equation

5
holds, because
\frac{Q}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot\Lambda=L
.
We conclude that the probability that each of the
r
calls outputs
\bot
is at most
2/\log^{3}
.
This means that
b
is a binomial random variable with
r
trails and success probability at most
2/\log^{3}n
. Therefore,
\mathbb{E}\left[b\right]\leq 2r/\log^{3}n
, and by Chernoff’s inequality, we get that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[b\geq r/10\right]\leq 2^{-r}
which is at most
1/n^{9}
, as
r=400\log n
.

We are left with proving that if the algorithm outputs a value
\hat{m}
, then

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}=m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm L\varepsilon/8\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{n^{6}}\;.

In other words, we need to show that if if
b<r/10
, then the median of all the values
\hat{m}_{i}
for which
\hat{m}_{i}\neq\bot
falls inside the interval
m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm L\varepsilon/8
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.
To show that, it suffices to prove that for every
i\in[r]
for which
\hat{m}_{i}\neq\bot
, it holds that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}_{i}=m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm L\varepsilon/8\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{n^{6}}\;,

and then to use the median of means trick, detailed in
Claim

47
in
Section

9
, to prove that the median
\hat{m}
falls inside this interval with probability at least
1-1/n^{7}
. Note that
\hat{m}
is the median of at least
9r/10
values.
By
Lemma

12
, we have that if
\hat{m}_{i}\neq\bot
, then

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}_{i}=m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm Q\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{\log n}\;.

We show that the interval
m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm Q\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}
is contained in the interval
m_{V}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon/2)\pm L\varepsilon/8
, which follows as
Q\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq L\varepsilon/8
.
To see that, we plug in
\Lambda=L\cdot{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}/Q
and get that

\displaystyle Q\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}=2L\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq L\cdot\varepsilon/8\;,

where the last inequality follows as
\varepsilon^{\prime}\leq\varepsilon/4
.
This completes the proof.

∎

4
Proof of
Theorem

2

The main result of this section is the proof of
Theorem

2
given the implementations of
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
and
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
, which are presented in

Sections

5
and
6
respectively.
Recall that for a
k
-disjoint set
V=V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V_{k}
, and a set
U\subseteq V
, we use
\mu(U)
to denote the size
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left\lvert U\cap V_{i}\right\rvert
.
See
2

We describe how to implement the algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
using the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
.

For
i=0
to
i=k\log n
do:

1.

\hat{m}\leftarrow\mathbf{GuessApx}(V^{\mathrm{input}},\mu(V^{\mathrm{input}})\cdot 2^{-i},\varepsilon)
.

2.

If
\hat{m}\neq``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
, then output
\hat{m}
.

As promised by
Theorem

11
, if
L\leq m/2
, then the algorithm outputs
\hat{m}=m(1\pm\varepsilon)
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
, which ensures that this process terminates with the same probability.
To prove that it terminates with the correct output, we use the second guarantee of
Theorem

11
, which states that if the output
\hat{m}\neq``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
, then
\hat{m}=m(1\pm\varepsilon)
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.
In other words, an output which is not
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
is correct with high probability, and the algorithm eventually outputs an output which is not
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
.

To complete the proof of
Theorem

2
, we use the following theorem that bounds the complexity of the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
.

Theorem 17
.

The algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
queries the
Hyperedge-Oracle
at most
T
times, and has query measure of at most
R
, where

\displaystyle T=(k\log n)^{O(k^{3})}/\varepsilon^{2k}\;,

\displaystyle R=\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\frac{(k\cdot\log n)^{O(k^{3})}}{\varepsilon^{2k}}\;.

We prove
Theorem

2
using
Theorem

17
.

Proof of
Theorem

2
using
Theorem

17
.

The algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
invokes the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
at most
k\log n
times.
The
i
-th execution of
\mathbf{GuessApx}
includes the following parameters
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V^{\mathrm{input}},\mu(V^{\mathrm{input}})\cdot 2^{-i},\varepsilon)
.
Let
r
be a random variable that is equal to the number of times that the algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
executes the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
.
By the guarantee of
Theorem

11
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
, the random variable
r
satisfies
\mu(V^{\mathrm{input}})/2^{r}\geq m/4
.
To see why, note that for any
i
for which
n^{k}/2^{i}\leq m/2
, the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
outputs
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
with probability at most
1/n^{6}
.
If it does not output
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
, then the algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
stops and outputs a value obtained from an execution of the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
.
This value is guaranteed to be a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.
This completes the proof of the correctness of the algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
.

We analyze the number of queries and the query measure of the algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
.
Each call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
queries the
Hyperedge-Oracle
at most
T
times independently of the input.
Since the algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
makes at most
k\log n
calls to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
(with different inputs) the total number of times the oracle is queried is at most
k\log n\cdot T
.

Let
M_{0}\triangleq\frac{(k\cdot\log n)^{O(k^{3})}}{\varepsilon^{2k}}
.
Let
R_{i}
denote the query measure of the
i
-th call
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V^{\mathrm{input}},\mu(V^{\mathrm{input}})\cdot 2^{-i},\varepsilon)
.
The query measure of the algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
is
\max_{i}R_{i}
.
From
Theorem

17
, we have that

\displaystyle R_{i}\leq M_{0}\cdot\frac{\mu(V^{\mathrm{input}})}{\mu(V^{\mathrm{input}})\cdot 2^{-i}}=M_{0}\cdot 2^{i}\;.

In other words, the sequence
\left\{R_{i}\right\}_{i}
is monotonically increasing.
Recall that we use
r
to denote a random variable that is equal to the number of calls that the algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
makes to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
. We showed that with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
,
r
satisfies
\mu(V^{\mathrm{input}})/2^{r}\geq m/4
, and therefore we can bound
\max_{i}R_{i}
by
M_{0}\cdot 2^{r}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle M_{0}\cdot 2^{r}\leq 4\cdot M_{0}\cdot\frac{\mu(V^{\mathrm{input}})}{m}\leq\frac{\mu(V^{\mathrm{input}})}{m}\cdot\frac{(k\cdot\log n)^{O(k^{3})}}{\varepsilon^{2k}}\;,

and therefore completes the proof.
∎

For the rest of this section we prove
Theorem

17
.
In
Section

4.1
, we start with an overview of the structure of the recursion created by the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
. In
Section

4.2
, we analyze the number of queries made by the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
.
In
Section

4.3
, we analyze the query measure of the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
. The reader may find it useful to consult with
for the analysis.

\mathbf{GuessApx}_{k}

\mathbf{MedianApx}

\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}

\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}

\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}

\mathbf{GuessApx}_{k-1}

\log n
\log n
calls itself
\zeta

Figure 4
:
This figure presents the call flow of the different algorithms.
A directed edge from algorithm
A
to algorithm
B
indicates that algorithm
A
makes a call to algorithm
B
. A value
x
on such edge indicates an upper bound on the number of times that algorithm
A
makes a call to algorithm
B
. This figure explains the structure of the recursion tree created by the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
. An algorithm with out-degree zero is a leaf in the recursion tree, where only leaves query the
Hyperedge-Oracle
.

We use the following parameters in the analysis.
Recall that

g(k)=(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}
,

\zeta=k\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}\cdot\frac{Q}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot\log^{4}n
,

Q=5k\cdot 2^{k}\cdot\log^{3}n
,

\psi=4^{k}\cdot k^{2}\log(k)\cdot\log^{k+2}n
,
and define
\rho\triangleq Q/{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}
.
Also, for every
\varepsilon
we define
\varepsilon^{\prime}\triangleq\frac{\min\left\{1,\varepsilon\right\}}{4\log n}
.

4.1
Overview of the Recursion

The algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
, detailed in
Theorem

11
, is a recursive algorithm that computes a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for the number of hyperedges
m_{V}
in
G[V]
.
It attempts to compute a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{V}
and will succeed w.h.p. if
L\leq m_{V}/2
. If
L>m_{V}/2
, the algorithm may fail to compute such an approximation and outputs
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
instead.
The advantage of
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
lies in its running time, which depends on
L
rather than on
m_{V}
.
This means that if
m_{V}
is small and approximating it is too costly then the algorithm indicates that this is the case by outputting
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
.

We also have several “internal” algorithms, which are used by the main algorithms. These are the algorithms
\mathbf{MedianApx}
,
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
,
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
, and
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
.

\mathbf{MedianApx}
.

The algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(V,L,\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)
, specified in
Lemma

16
, is an amplification of the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
.
It takes as input a vertex set
V
, a heaviness threshold
L
, and an error parameter
\varepsilon
. Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\min\left\{1,\varepsilon\right\}}{4\log n}
.
The algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}
executes
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,L\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime}/Q\right)
for
\Theta(\log n)
times. Let
\hat{s}
denote the median of the results, while ignoring the outputs that are
\bot
.
If the number of outputs that are
\bot
is too large, the algorithm outputs
\bot
, and otherwise it outputs
\hat{s}
.

\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
.

The algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
, specified in
Lemma

12
, is a recursive algorithm that takes as input a vertex set
V
, a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
, and an error parameter
\varepsilon^{\prime}
. It outputs either
\bot
or a value
\hat{m}
such that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon)\pm Q\cdot 2\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right]\geq 1-1/\log n\;.

If
\Lambda
is sufficiently large then the algorithm outputs
\bot
with probability at most
2/\log^{3}n
.
The algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
operates as follows.
In case that
\Lambda>1
, the algorithm makes three calls in the following order.

1.

A call to
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
, which computes a set
V_{\Lambda}
or outputs
\bot
.

2.

A call to
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}})
, where
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}=\Lambda/(4^{k}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}})
.

3.

A call to
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V[p]-V_{\Lambda},\Lambda\cdot p^{k}\right)
.

It uses the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
to compute the set
V_{\Lambda}
.
It then uses the procedure
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
to approximate
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
which is the number of hyperedges in
G[V]
that contain at least one vertex from
V_{\Lambda}
.
The final call is a recursive call to
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V[p]-V_{\Lambda},\Lambda\cdot p^{k}\right)
, where
p
is a parameter that is set to
1/2
, and the set
V[p]-V_{\Lambda}
is a random subset of vertices taken from the set
V-V_{\Lambda}
, where every vertex is added to the new set independently with probability
p
.

The base case of the recursion is when
\Lambda\leq 1
.
In this case, the algorithm makes only the call to the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
to compute a set
V_{\Lambda}
. This call can also output
\bot
and this case
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
also outputs
\bot
.
If the output of
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
is not
\bot
, then it is a set of vertices
V_{\Lambda}
such that
\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert\leq\zeta
.
Moreover, the set
V_{\Lambda}
contains only
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy vertices and all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices w.h.p., where
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}=\Lambda/(4^{k}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}})
.
Since
\Lambda\leq 1
, the set of
\Lambda
-heavy vertices contains all non isolated vertices in
G[V]
. Therefore,
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
is equal to
m_{V_{\Lambda}}(V_{\Lambda})
which is the number of edges in
G[V_{\Lambda}]
.
The number of edges in
G[V_{\Lambda}]
is approximated by calling the algorithm
\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}(V_{\Lambda},\varepsilon^{\prime})
, which was presented in
[
DLM22
,
BBGM24
]
and is formally detailed in
Theorem

9
.
This algorithm makes at most
\frac{(k\log n)^{O(k)}}{\varepsilon^{\prime 2}}
queries to a
Hyperedge-Oracle
, while querying sets of vertices
U\subseteq V_{\Lambda}
, and therefore has query measure of at most
\mu(V_{\Lambda})\leq\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert^{k}\leq\zeta^{k}
.
The last inequality holds because the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
never outputs a set of vertices of size larger than
\zeta
.
This completes the description of the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
.

\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
.

The procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
, which we introduced in the previous section in
Section

3
, is a procedure that takes as input a vertex set
V
and a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
.
It outputs either
\bot
or a set
V_{\Lambda}
of all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices, and no
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices with probability at least
1-1/n^{4}
, where
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}=\Lambda/(4^{k}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}})
.
Later on, in
Section

6
, and more specifically in
Theorem

21
, we prove that it does not make any additional recursive calls.
We also prove that it only queries the oracle at most
O(\psi\cdot\zeta)
times, while only querying sets of vertices
U
for which
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/\Lambda)
.

\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
.

The procedure
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}})
,
which we introduced in the previous section in
Section

3
, is a procedure that takes as input a vertex set
V
together with a subset
V_{\Lambda}\subseteq V
and a heaviness threshold
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
.
It computes an approximation for
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
.
Later on, in
Section

5
we provide a formal description of this procedure, and how it is implemented.
We provide an overview of this procedure, which is also recursive, and makes additional calls to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
.

The procedure
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
selects a vertex
v
from
V_{\Lambda}
, approximates its degree
d_{v}
in the hypergraph
G[V]
, denoted by
\hat{d}_{v}
, and then removes
v
from the vertex set
V
. Consequently, all hyperedges containing
v
are removed from
G[V]
. This process repeats until all vertices in
V_{\Lambda}
are removed. The output of
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
is the sum of the approximated degrees,
\sum_{v\in V_{\Lambda}}\hat{d}_{v}
. Removing
v
from
V
after approximating its degree ensures that the same hyperedge is not counted multiple times.

To approximate the degree of a vertex
v
in a
k
-partite hypergraph, we use our main algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
to approximate the number of hyperedges in a newly formed
(k-1)
-partite hypergraph
G_{v}
.
This hypergraph is derived by removing
v
and all vertices in the same vertex set
V^{\mathrm{input}}_{\ell}
that satisfies
v\in V^{\mathrm{input}}_{\ell}
from
G
.
The set of hyperedges of the new graph
G_{v}
is obtained as follows.
For every hyperedge
e
in
E_{V}(v)
, we define a new hyperedge
e^{\prime}=e\setminus\left\{v\right\}
, and add it to the set of hyperedges of the new graph
G_{v}
.
We then approximate the number of hyperedges in
G_{v}
, providing an estimate of
v
’s degree in
G
.
In other words, the call
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}})
makes
\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert
calls to the
algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{k-1}
.
This algorithm is equipped with a new oracle, denoted by
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}^{\prime}
, which operates over
G_{v}
. We can easily simulate
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}^{\prime}
using the original
Hyperedge-Oracle
: Given a set
U
of vertices, we can simulate the query
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}^{\prime}({U})
by
making the query
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}(U\cup\{v\})
.

The base case of this recursion is when
k=1
. In this case, the procedure
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
makes no recursive calls, and directly computes the degree of a vertex
v
using
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}^{\prime}
via the query
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}^{\prime}(\left\{v\right\})
.
This means that at the base case, this procedure queries the
Hyperedge-Oracle
at most
\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert
times, where every such query has query measure of
O(1)
. This completes the overview of the procedure
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
.

We summarize the three types of calls that access the oracle.

1.

A call to the algorithm
\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}(V_{\Lambda},\varepsilon^{\prime})
.
Such a call queries the
Hyperedge-Oracle
at most
\frac{(k\log n)^{O(k)}}{\varepsilon^{\prime 2}}\leq\zeta
times, while querying sets of vertices
U\subseteq V_{\Lambda}
and therefore
\mu(U)\leq\zeta^{k}
.

2.

A call to the procedure
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(U,V_{\Lambda},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}})
over a
1
-partite hypergraph. Such a call queries the
Hyperedge-Oracle
at most
O\left(\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert\right)
times, which is at most
\zeta
. Each such query has query measure of
O(1)
.

3.

A call to the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
.
Such a call queries the
Hyperedge-Oracle
at most
O\left(\psi\cdot\zeta\right)
times,
where each query has query measure of
\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/\Lambda)
.

This completes the overview of the structure of the recursion.

We define the recursion tree of the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
, which will be use to bound the number of queries and the query measure of the algorithm.
Each node in the recursion tree corresponds to a call to a specific algorithm, where the root of the tree corresponds to the call
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
.
The children of a node
x
in the recursion tree correspond to the calls that are made by the algorithm that is associated with the node
x
.
The nodes that query the
Hyperedge-Oracle
are the leaves of the recursion tree.

Recall that the procedure
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
invokes the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
to approximate the number of hyperedges in a
(k-1)
-partite hypergraph.
We use
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}
to emphasize a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
on a
h
-partite hypergraph.
We define the dimension of a node
x
in the recursion tree as follows.
For a node
x
that is associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}(V,L,\varepsilon)
, which means that the vertex set
V
contains
h
vertex sets,
we say that the dimension of
x
is
h
.
For a node
x
that is not associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
,
we define the dimension of
x
as the dimension of its parent node.

4.2
Bounding the Number of Queries

In this subsection, we prove that the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
makes at most
O\left(\log^{6k}n\cdot\zeta^{k}\cdot\psi\right)
queries to the
Hyperedge-Oracle
.

We analyze the recursion tree generated by the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{k}
.
We can ignore the parameters in each call, as the number of queries made by any call in this tree is always bounded by
O(\psi\cdot\zeta)
independently of the parameters given to the call.
It therefore suffices to bound the number of leaves of the recursion tree.

For that, we bound the number of leaves of dimension
h
for
h\in[k]
.
Moreover, we bound the number of nodes of dimension
h
that are associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}
, for
h\in[k]
.
This provides us with a recursive relation that bounds the total number of leaves in the recursion tree.
We need to prove the following properties.

1.

Each leaf queries the
Hyperedge-Oracle
at most
O\left(\psi\cdot\zeta\right)
times.

2.

A call
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}(V,L,\varepsilon)
has at most
\alpha\leq 3\log^{3}n
leaves of the same dimension
h
.

3.

A call
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}(V,L,\varepsilon)
has at most
\beta=\log^{3}n\cdot\zeta
descendant nodes which are associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h-1}
.

Note that the number of queries that are made indirectly by a node
x
which is associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}
is bounded by sum of the number of queries made by the leaves of dimension
h
, and the nodes of dimension
h-1
.

Once we show the above properties, we can bound the number of leaves of dimension
h
in the recursion tree of the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{k}
as follows.
For every
h\in[k]
, the number of leaves of dimension
h
is at most the number of nodes of dimension
h
that are associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}
times the maximum number of leaves of dimension
h
each such node can have.
The number of nodes of dimension
(k-h)
that are associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{k-h}
is at most
\beta^{h}
, and the maximum number of leaves that each such node can have is at most
\alpha
.
Therefore, the total number of leaves of dimension
(k-h)
is at most
\alpha\cdot\beta^{h}
.
Therefore, the total number of leaves in the tree is

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\alpha\cdot\beta^{i}\leq\alpha\cdot 2\beta^{k-1}\;,

where the inequality holds because this is a sum of a geometric series with ratio
\beta\geq 2
.
Using the first property, we get that the number of queries made by the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
is at most

\displaystyle O\left(2\alpha\beta^{k-1}\cdot\psi\cdot\zeta\right)=O\left(\log^{3k}n\cdot\zeta^{k}\cdot\psi\right)\;.

To show that this is the desired bound, we need to show that

\displaystyle\log^{3k}n\cdot\zeta^{k}\cdot\psi=(k\log n)^{O(k^{3})}/\varepsilon^{2k}\;.

Clearly,
\log^{3k}n=(k\log n)^{O(k^{2})}
, so we are left with showing that

\psi=(k\log n)^{O(k^{2})}
, and that
\zeta=(k\log n)^{O(k^{2})}/\varepsilon^{k}
.
Recall that
\psi=4^{k}\cdot k^{2}\log(k)\cdot\log^{k+2}n
, which implies that
\psi=O(4^{k}\cdot\log^{k+3}n)=(k\log n)^{O(k^{2})}
.
To show the bound on
\zeta
, recall that
\zeta=k\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}\cdot\frac{Q}{{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}}\cdot\log^{4}n
, and that
Q=5k\cdot 2^{k}\cdot\log^{3}n
, which means that
\zeta=(k\log n)^{O(k^{2})}/\varepsilon^{2k}
.
Therefore, we conclude that a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
queries the oracle at most
(k\log n)^{O(k^{3})}/\varepsilon^{2k}
times, which proves the first part of
Theorem

17
.

We are left with proving the properties.
The first property follows by the definition of the leaves of the recursion tree.
We explain how to get the bounds on
\alpha
and
\beta
.
The algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}
makes at most
\log n
calls to
\mathbf{MedianApx}
, which in turn makes at most
\log n
calls to
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
.
Simplifying the analysis, we can assume each call to
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,\Lambda)
results in at most
\log^{2}n
calls to
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
.

\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
itself makes up to
\log n
calls each to
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
and
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
, and one call to
\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}
.
Thus, each call of
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}
results in at most
\log^{2}n
calls to
\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}
, and up to
\log^{3}n
calls to both
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
and
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
.
This proves that
\alpha\leq 3\log^{3}n
and therefore the second property holds.
Since each call to
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
results in at most
\zeta
calls to
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h-1}
, we have that
\beta\leq\log^{3}n\cdot\zeta
, which proves that the third property holds.
Therefore, all three properties hold, and we have that the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
makes at most
(k\log n)^{O(k^{3})}/\varepsilon^{2k}
queries to the
Hyperedge-Oracle
, which was the goal of this subsection.

4.3
Bounding the Query Measure

In this subsection, we prove that the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
has query measure at most

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\frac{(k\cdot\log n)^{O(k^{3})}}{\varepsilon^{2k}}\right)\;.

Recall that we say that an algorithm has query measure

R
if every set
U
which is queried by the algorithm satisfies that
\mu(U)\leq R
.
We define the query measure of a node
x
in the recursion tree as the maximum query measure of its direct descendants. In other words, we can ignore the number of times one algorithm calls another, and we only care about the parameters that are given to the call, which determine the query measure of the call.
As before, it suffices to bound the query measure of leaves of the recursion tree, as they are the only nodes that query the
Hyperedge-Oracle
.
The query measure of the call
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
is the maximum query measure of the leaves of the recursion tree of the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
.
The query measure of leaves associated with a call to the algorithms
\mathbf{BBGM\mhyphen Approx}
and
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
is at most
O\left(\zeta^{k}\right)
, which is at most
(k\log n)^{O(k^{3})}/\varepsilon^{2k}
. This value is at most
O\left((\mu(V)/L)\cdot\zeta^{k}\right)
, because
(\mu(V)/L)
is larger than
1
.
We are left with bounding the query measure of leaves associated with a call to the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(U,\Lambda)
.
We will provide an analysis of the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
, in
Section

6
, where we also prove an that the query measure of the the call

\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(U,\Lambda)
is at most
\tilde{O}(\mu(U)/\Lambda)
(
Theorem

21
)
We therefore need to show that for every pair of parameters
U
and
\Lambda
given to the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
, we have that
\mu(U)/\Lambda=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/L)
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
, where
V
and
L
are the parameters given to the call
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,\Lambda,\varepsilon)
, which is associated with the call to root of the recursion tree.
We use
\xi=4^{k}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}
, where
\xi=\Lambda/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
, and let
\rho^{\prime}=\rho\cdot(6\log n)^{k}
.

We split the analysis into two parts.
We show that the following two properties hold for every node
s
of dimension
h
that is associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}(V,L,\varepsilon)
.

1.

The query measure of leaves of the same dimension as the node
s_{0}
is at most
O\left(\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\right)
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.

2.

Every descendant node
s^{\prime}
of dimension
h-1
that is associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h-1}(V^{\prime},L^{\prime},\varepsilon)
satisfies that
\frac{\mu(V^{\prime})}{L^{\prime}}\leq\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\cdot\xi
with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.

These two properties prove that the query measure of every leaf of dimension
(k-h)
in the recursion tree that is associated with the call
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{k}(V,L,\varepsilon)
is at most

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\cdot\left(\rho^{\prime}\cdot\xi\right)^{h}\right)

with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.
Note that the overall size of the recursion tree is at most
(k\log n)^{O(k^{3})}
, as it has at most
(k\log n)^{O(k^{3})}
leaves, and height at most
k
.
Therefore, we can use a union bound on all nodes, and get that with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
the query measure is at most

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\cdot\left(\rho^{\prime}\cdot\xi\right)^{k}\right)\;.

To complete the proof which bounds the query measure of the call
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
, we need to show that

\displaystyle\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\cdot\left(\rho^{\prime}\cdot\xi\right)^{k}=\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\frac{(k\cdot\log n)^{O(k^{3})}}{\varepsilon^{2k}}\;.

This follows as
\rho^{\prime}=(k\log n)^{O(k^{2})}/{\varepsilon^{2}}
, and
\xi=(k\log n)^{O(k^{2})}
. Both hold by the definition of
\rho^{\prime}
and
\xi
:

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=Q/{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}\cdot(6\log n)^{k}=(k\log n)^{O(k)}/\varepsilon^{2k}\;,

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=4^{k}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}=(k\log n)^{O(k^{2})}\;,

which completes the proof that the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V,L,\varepsilon)
has query measure at most
O\left(\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\frac{(k\cdot\log n)^{O(k^{3})}}{\varepsilon^{2k}}\right)
.

We are left with proving the two properties.

Proof of property (1).

We say that a node
y
associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
is

fresh
, if its parent node
y^{\prime}
is not associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
, and instead is associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}
.
The algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}(V,L,\varepsilon)
makes
O(\log^{2}n)
calls to nodes of the same dimension, associated with the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
, where
\Lambda=L/\rho
.
We only count the number of fresh nodes of dimension
h
in the recursion tree of the call
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h}(V,L,\varepsilon)
, which are associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
.
We fix one fresh node
a
, and analyze the query measure of the leaves of the same dimension as
a
, in
a
’s recursion tree, which are associated with a call the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
.
The node
a
has at most
O(\log n)
such descendants
, where each such decedent is associated with a call to the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(U_{i},\Lambda\cdot p^{ki})
,
where the vertex sets
\left\{U_{i}\right\}
are random subsets of
V
that are obtained as follows (See
).
The vertex set
U_{0}
is
U_{0}=V
. The vertex set
U_{i+1}
is obtained by choosing each vertex from
U_{i}
independently with probability
p
, and then removing some vertices from the obtained set. We can ignore this removal step, which can only decrease the size of
U_{i+1}
, and therefore only decrease the query measure of the associated call.
As we previously mentioned, we later on prove (in
Theorem

21
) that the query measure of a call
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(U_{i},\Lambda\cdot p^{ki})
is at most
\tilde{O}(\mu(U_{i})/\Lambda)
.
By standard Chernoff bounds, we have that the size of
U_{i}
is at most
\left\lvert U_{0}\right\rvert\cdot(6\log n)^{k}
, which we proved in
Claim

10

Therefore, the query measure of the leaf associated with the call
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(U_{i},\Lambda\cdot p^{ki})
is at most

O\left(\frac{\mu(V)}{\Lambda}\cdot(6\log n)^{k}\right)
with probability at least
1-k/n^{6}
.
The term
\frac{\mu(V)}{\Lambda}\cdot(6\log n)^{k}
can also be written as
\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
, which completes the proof of the first property.

Proof of property (2).

We use the same notation as in the proof of the first property.
Recall that we have a fresh node
a
associated with a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
, where
a
is of dimension
h
.
Node
a
makes one call to the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
, which outputs a vertex set
V_{\Lambda}
. If this output is
\bot
, then the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
does not make additional calls.
If it is not
\bot
, then the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}
makes a call to the procedure
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}})
, where
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}=\Lambda/(4^{k}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}})
, and another recursive call to itself with the following parameters
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V[p]-V_{\Lambda},\Lambda\cdot p^{k})
, where
p=1/2
.
We unfold
the recursion, and think of
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}_{\bot}(V,\Lambda)
as such that has
\log n
iterations. In iteration
i
it makes one call to the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(U_{i},\Lambda\cdot p^{ki})
, where we denote the output of this call by
V_{\Lambda}^{i}
.
If
V_{\Lambda}^{i}\neq\bot
, then a matching call of
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(U_{i},V_{\Lambda}^{i},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot p^{ki})
is made.
As previously explained, we have
U_{0}=V
and the set
U_{i+1}
is obtained by choosing each vertex from
U_{i}
independently with probability
p
and then removing some vertices from the obtained set. We ignore this removal step, which can only decrease the size of
U_{i+1}
, and therefore only decrease the query measure of the associated call.
We previously proved that the size of
U_{i}
is at most
\left\lvert U_{0}\right\rvert\cdot(6\log n)^{k}
with probability at least
1-k/n^{6}
.

Each call to the procedure
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(U_{i},V_{\Lambda}^{i},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot p^{ki})
generates
\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}^{i}\right\rvert
calls to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h-1}
, with the parameters
\mathbf{GuessApx}_{h-1}(U_{i}^{\prime},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot p^{ki},\varepsilon)
, where
U_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq U_{i}
.
We therefore get that with probability at least
1-k/n^{6}
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\mu(U_{i}^{\prime})}{\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot p^{ki}}\leq\frac{\mu(V)}{\Lambda}\cdot\xi\cdot(6\log n)^{k}\leq\frac{\mu(V)}{L}\cdot\rho^{\prime}\xi\;,

which completes the proof of the second property, and therefore the proof of
Theorem

17
.

5
Implementing the
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
Procedure

In this section, we explain how to implement the
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
procedure, which is given two sets of vertices
V
and
V_{\Lambda}
, a heaviness threshold
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
, and a precision parameter
\varepsilon>0
.
If the set
V_{\Lambda}
contains only
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy vertices, then w.h.p. the algorithm outputs a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation
\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}
for
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
.
The following lemma is the main result of this section.

Theorem 18

(
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
)
.

There exists an algorithm
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}})

that takes as input two sets of vertices
V
and
V_{\Lambda}
, a heaviness threshold
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
, and a precision parameter
\varepsilon>0
.
Assuming the vertex set
V_{\Lambda}
contains only
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy vertices,
the algorithm outputs
\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}
such that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}=(1\pm\varepsilon)m_{U}(V_{\Lambda})\right]\geq 1-\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert/n^{5}\;.

The algorithm makes
\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert
calls to the algorithm
\mathbf{Deg\mhyphen Approx}_{\varepsilon}(v,\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k,V)
, specified in
Lemma

19
, where
v\in V_{\Lambda}
.

Recall that for two sets of vertices
A,B
we use
E_{A}(B)
to denote the set of hyperedges
e
such that
e\in G[A]
and
e\cap B\neq\emptyset
, and we denote the number of hyperedges in
E_{A}(B)
by
m_{A}(B)
.
Roughly speaking, to compute such an approximation we approximate the degree of every vertex
v\in V_{\Lambda}
and sum the approximations of the degrees.
This simplified approach faces a few challenges.

First, the sum of the degrees of vertices in
V_{\Lambda}
is not equal to the number of hyperedges that intersect
V_{\Lambda}
.
This is because some hyperedges might contain more than a single vertex from
V_{\Lambda}
, and therefore the sum of the degrees of the vertices in
V_{\Lambda}
might double count some hyperedges.
To deal with this issue, we choose some arbitrary ordering
\pi
over the vertices in
V_{\Lambda}
, and approximate the degree of the first vertex
v_{1}
in the ordering, then remove
v_{1}
from
V
, to obtain an updated vertex set
U
, and approximate the degree of the next vertex
v_{2}
in the ordering over the new updated graph
G[U]
, and so on.
When we remove a vertex
v_{i}
from
V
, we also remove all the hyperedges
e
for which
v_{i}\in e
from
V
. This way, we avoid double counting the hyperedges, as this ensures that every hyperedge in
E_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
is counted exactly once.

To approximate the degree of a vertex
v
over a
k
-partite hypergraph
G
, we build a new
(k-1)
-partite hypergraph
G_{v}
, which is obtained as follows.
Let
V_{\ell}
be the part in the
k
-partition of
G
’s vertices that contains
v
.
The vertex set of
G_{v}
, denoted by
V^{\prime}
, is defined as
V^{\prime}=V\setminus V_{\ell}
.
The edge set
E^{\prime}
of
G_{v}
is defined as follows.
For every hyperedge
e\in E_{V}(v)
, we define a new hyperedge
e^{\prime}
by
e^{\prime}=e\setminus\left\{v\right\}
, and add
e^{\prime}
to
E^{\prime}
.
Note that the degree of
v
over
G
is equal to the number of hyperedges in
G_{v}
.

We explain how to approximate the degree
d_{v}
of a vertex
v\in V_{\Lambda}
within the subgraph
G
or equivalently, the number of hyperedges in
G_{v}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
.
While it might seem natural to estimate
d_{v}
by approximating the number of hyperedges in
G_{v}
using
Theorem

2
, this might be too costly.
The reason is that the degree of
v
might be significantly smaller than
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
, although initially all vertices in
V_{\Lambda}
are
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy.
This occurs because the algorithm gradually removes the processed vertices from
U
, potentially decreasing the degrees of the remaining vertices.
Therefore, instead of approximating the degree of
v
on
G_{v}
using
Theorem

2
, we approximate the degree of
v
with an additive error of
\varepsilon\cdot\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k
, by utilize our algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}
, detailed in
Theorem

11
, invoked with the parameters
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V^{\prime},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k,\varepsilon)
.
The advantage of using
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V^{\prime},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k,\varepsilon)
lies in its efficiency – the number of queries it requires and its query measure are determined by
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k
and
\varepsilon
and are independent of the number of hyperedges in the graph it is operates on.
This comes at the price of getting an additive approximation instead of a multiplicative one which the algorithm
\mathbf{Hyperedge\mhyphen Approx}
specified in
Theorem

2
obtains. Recall that the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V^{\prime},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k,\varepsilon)
outputs either
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
or a value
\hat{d}_{v}
such that
\hat{d}_{v}=m_{V}(v)\pm\varepsilon\cdot\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k
.
In other words, the guarantee of the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V^{\prime},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k,\varepsilon)
is weaker than the guarantee of
Theorem

2
.

We provide a simplified overview on why such an approximation is sufficient for our needs.
For every vertex
v\in V_{\Lambda}
, define a vertex set
V_{\pi(v)}
that contains all vertices in
V
without the vertices
u\in V_{\Lambda}
for which
\pi(u)<\pi(v)
.
We use
d^{\pi}_{v}
to denote the degree of
v
over
G[V_{\pi(v)}]
, and
\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}
to denote the output of the algorithm which approximates the degree of
v
over
G[V_{\pi(v)}]
.
The approximation
\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}
is such that with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
we have that
\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}=d_{v}\pm\varepsilon\cdot\sigma_{v}
, where

\sigma_{v}\triangleq\max\{d^{\pi}_{v},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k\}
.
We partition the set of vertices
V_{\Lambda}
into two disjoint sets
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}
and
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}
.
A vertex
v\in V_{\Lambda}
is in
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}
if
d^{\pi}_{v}\geq\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k
and is in
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}
otherwise.
Note that for every vertex
v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}
we have that
d^{\pi}_{v}\geq\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k
and therefore
\sigma_{v}=d^{\pi}_{v}
, whereas for every vertex
v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}
we have that
\sigma_{v}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k
.
In other words, the approximation
\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}
is a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
d^{\pi}_{v}
for vertices in
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}
, and an approximation with an additive error of
\varepsilon\cdot\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k
for vertices in
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}
.

Finally, we show that
\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}
is a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})
, and that
\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}=m_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})\pm\varepsilon m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
. We therefore get that the sum of the approximations
\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}
is a
(1\pm 2\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
.

We also keep in mind that the set
V_{\Lambda}
is obtained from a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
, which means that
\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert
is at most
\zeta
, where recall that
\zeta=2^{k}\cdot 5k^{2}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}\cdot\log^{7}n/{\varepsilon^{\prime}}^{2}
.

Recall that for every vertex
v\in V
, we use
\chi(v)
to denote the index of the vertex set that
v
belongs to. In other words,
v\in V_{\chi(v)}
.
The following lemma provides an algorithm that approximates the degree of a vertex.

Lemma 19
.

There exists an algorithm
\mathbf{Deg\mhyphen Approx}_{\varepsilon}(v,\Lambda^{\prime},V)
that takes as input a single vertex
v
, a heaviness threshold
\Lambda^{\prime}
, a subset of vertices
V
, and a precision parameter
\varepsilon>0
.
The algorithm outputs a value
\hat{d}_{v}
such that

\hat{d}_{v}=d_{U}(v)\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
, where
\sigma_{v}\triangleq\max\{d_{U}(v),\Lambda^{\prime}\}
.
The algorithm makes a single call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(U^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},\varepsilon)
specified in
Theorem

11
, where
U^{\prime}=U\setminus V_{\chi(v)}
, and might make a single call to the algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(U^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},V\right)
specified in
Lemma

16
.

We prove
Theorem

18
using
Lemma

19
.

Proof of
Theorem

18
using
Lemma

19
.
We start with a description of the algorithm
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}})
.

Algorithm 3

The procedure
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}})
specified in
Theorem

18
.

Input:

Two sets of vertices
V
and
V_{\Lambda}
, a heaviness threshold
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
, and a precision parameter
\varepsilon>0
.

Output:

An approximation
\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}
. If
V_{\Lambda}
contains only
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy vertices, then
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\hat{m}_{V}(V_{\Lambda})=(1\pm\varepsilon)m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})\right]\geq 1-\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert/n^{5}
.

1

\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}\leftarrow 0,\;\;\Lambda^{\prime}\leftarrow\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k
.

2

for

v_{i}\in V_{\Lambda}

do

3

\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}\leftarrow\mathbf{Deg\mhyphen Approx}_{\varepsilon}(v_{i},\Lambda^{\prime},V)
;

4

\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}\leftarrow\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}+\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}
;

5

V\leftarrow V\setminus\left\{v_{i}\right\}
;

6

return

\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}
.

We use the following notation.
Let
\pi
denote the order in which the vertices in
V_{\Lambda}
are processed. Any ordering can be used, and we choose an arbitrary one.
Recall that we use
\pi(v)
to denote the index of the vertex
v
in the ordering
\pi
,
and that
V_{\pi(v)}
denotes the vertex set
U
after removing all vertices
u
for which
\pi(u)<\pi(v)
.
Recall that
d^{\pi}_{v}
denotes the degree of a vertex
v
over
G[V_{\pi(v)}]
, and that
\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}
denotes the output of the algorithm
\mathbf{Deg\mhyphen Approx}_{\varepsilon}(v,\Lambda^{\prime},V_{\pi(v)})
, where
\Lambda^{\prime}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/k
.
For every vertex
v\in V_{\Lambda}
, let
\sigma_{v}\triangleq\max\{d_{v},\Lambda^{\prime}\}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
denote the event that for every vertex
v\in V_{\Lambda}
we have

\displaystyle\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}=d^{\pi}_{v}\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon\;.

The event
\mathcal{F}
occurs with probability at least
1-\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert/n^{5}
, by
Lemma

19
and a union bound over all vertices in
V_{\Lambda}
.
We prove that if the event
\mathcal{F}
occurs, then the output
\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}
of the algorithm
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,V_{\Lambda},\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}})
is a
(1\pm 2\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
.

We partition the vertices in
V_{\Lambda}
into two sets
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}
and
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}
.
A vertex
v\in V_{\Lambda}
is in
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}
if
d^{\pi}_{v}\geq\Lambda^{\prime}
, and is in
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}
otherwise.
We emphasize that the algorithm does not know which vertex is in
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}
or
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}
and we only make this distinction for the analysis.
We define the following values.

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})\triangleq\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}\;,

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})\triangleq\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}\;.

Note that
\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}
is equal to
\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})+\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})
:

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}=\sum_{v\in V_{\Lambda}}\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}+\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}=\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})+\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})\;.

Our goal is to show that the following hold.

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})=m_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon)\;,

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})=m_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})\pm m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})\cdot\varepsilon\;.

(6)

Using
Equation

6
, we can complete the proof, as we get that

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{V_{\Lambda}}=\hat{m}_{W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}+\hat{m}_{W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}=m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})\cdot(1\pm 2\varepsilon)\;.

We are thus left with proving
Equation

6
.
First, we prove that
m_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}d^{\pi}_{v}
and
m_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}d^{\pi}_{v}
, as follows.
The ordering
\pi
defines a partition
\left\{E_{V_{\pi(v)}}(v)\right\}_{v\in V_{\Lambda}}
of the hyperedges
E_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
.
To prove that this is a partition, we show that every edge
e
is in exactly one set
E_{V_{\pi(v)}}(v)
.
Fix some edge
e\in E_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
.
Te prove that
e
is in at least one set of the partition, let
v
be the first vertex in the ordering
\pi
such that
v\in e
. Then,
e\in E_{V_{\pi(v)}}(v)
, as every vertex
u\in e
satisfies
\pi(u)\geq\pi(v)
and therefore
u\in V_{\pi(v)}
.
To prove that
e
is in at most one set of the partition, assume towards a contradiction that
an edge
e
is in two sets
E_{V_{\pi(v)}}(v)
and
E_{V_{\pi(u)}}(u)
for some
u\neq v
, where
\pi(v)<\pi(u)
. Then,
v\notin V_{\pi(u)}
and therefore
e
is not in
G[V_{\pi(u)}]
, which implies that

e\notin E_{V_{\pi(u)}}(u)
.
This completes the proof that
\left\{E_{V_{\pi(v)}}(v)\right\}_{v\in V_{\Lambda}}
forms a partition of the hyperedges
E_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
.

We therefore have that

\displaystyle m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})=\sum_{v\in V_{\Lambda}}\left\lvert E_{V_{\pi(v)}}(v)\right\rvert=\sum_{v\in V_{\Lambda}}d^{\pi}_{v}\;.

Since we did not use any property of the set
V_{\Lambda}
, the same claim holds for every subset
W\subseteq V_{\Lambda}
. That is, for any set
W\subseteq V_{\Lambda}
, we have that the collection
\left\{E_{V_{\pi(v)}}(v)\right\}_{v\in W}
is a partition of the hyperedges
E_{V}(W)
.
We therefore get that

\displaystyle m_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}\left\lvert E_{V_{\pi(v)}}(v)\right\rvert=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}d^{\pi}_{v}

(7)

\displaystyle m_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}\left\lvert E_{V_{\pi(v)}}(v)\right\rvert=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}d^{\pi}_{v}\;,

(8)

as both
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}
and
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}
are subsets of
V_{\Lambda}
.
This proves that
m_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}d^{\pi}_{v}
and
m_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}d^{\pi}_{v}
.

Second, we show that

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}d^{\pi}_{v}\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon)\;,

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})=(\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}d^{\pi}_{v})\pm m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})\cdot\varepsilon\;.

(9)

We conditioned on the event
\mathcal{F}
, which means that for every vertex
v\in V_{\Lambda}
, we have
\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}=d^{\pi}_{v}\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon
, where
\sigma_{v}\triangleq\max\{d^{\pi}_{v},\Lambda^{\prime}\}
.
Note that by the definition of
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}
, for every vertex
v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}
we have
d^{\pi}_{v}\geq\Lambda^{\prime}
, and therefore
\sigma_{v}=d^{\pi}_{v}
.
At the same time, for every vertex
v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}
we have
\sigma_{v}=\Lambda^{\prime}
.
We therefore get

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}})=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}(d^{\pi}_{v}\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon)=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}(d^{\pi}_{v}\pm d^{\pi}_{v}\cdot\varepsilon)=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen heavy}}}d^{\pi}_{v}(1\pm\varepsilon)\;,

and

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{V}(W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}})=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}\hat{d}^{\pi}_{v}=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}(d^{\pi}_{v}\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon)=\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}(d^{\pi}_{v}\pm\Lambda^{\prime}\cdot\varepsilon)=(\sum_{v\in W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}}d^{\pi}_{v})\pm\Lambda^{\prime}\cdot\varepsilon\cdot\left\lvert W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}\right\rvert\;.

To complete the proof of
Equation

9
, we show that
\Lambda^{\prime}\cdot\left\lvert W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}\right\rvert\leq m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
.
First note that
W_{\pi\mathrm{\mhyphen light}}\subseteq V_{\Lambda}
. Therefore, it suffies to show that
\Lambda^{\prime}\cdot\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert\leq m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
.
Since every vertex in
V_{\Lambda}
is
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy, we have that
\sum_{v\in V_{\Lambda}}d_{v}\geq\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert
.
Moreover,
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})\geq\sum_{v\in V_{\Lambda}}d_{v}/k
as every hyperedge contains exactly
k
vertices.
We thus get that
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})\geq\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\cdot\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert/k=\Lambda^{\prime}\cdot\left\lvert V_{\Lambda}\right\rvert
, which proves
Equation

9
. Together with
Equations

7
and
8
, this completes the proof of
Equation

6
.

∎

We are left with proving
Lemma

19
.
To this end, we provide a recursive implementation for
\mathbf{Deg\mhyphen Approx}_{\varepsilon}
.
We start with a
k
-partite hypergraph
G[V_{\pi(v)}]
where we want to approximate the degree of a vertex
v
, and
reduce the problem to approximating the number of hyperedges in
a new
(k-1)
-partite hypergraph.
This way, we reduce the dimension of the hypergraph by one in each recursive call.
The stopping condition for the recursion is when
k=1
and the hypergraph is
1
-partite, i.e., every hyperedge contains exactly one vertex.
In this degenerate case the degree of a vertex is either zero or one,
and then we can exactly compute the degree of a vertex
v
by invoking the query
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}(\left\{v\right\})
.
When
k>1
, we use
Theorem

11
to approximate the number of hyperedges in a new
(k-1)
-partite hypergraph as we formally prove in what follows.

Proof of
Lemma

19
using
Theorem

11
.

If
G
is a
1
-partite hypergraph, then we complete the proof as previously explained:
We output
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}(\left\{v\right\})
which is either zero or one as the output of the algorithm
\mathbf{Deg\mhyphen Approx}_{\varepsilon}(v,\Lambda,V)
.
Clearly, the output is equal to
d_{v}
so the correctness follows.

We prove the case that
k>1
.
We explain how to implement a call to the algorithm
\mathbf{Deg\mhyphen Approx}_{\varepsilon}(v,\Lambda^{\prime},V)
using the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},\varepsilon)
specified in
Theorem

11
.
For that, we define a new hypergraph
G_{v}
, which is a
(k-1)
-partite hypergraph, with the property that the degree of
v
in
G[V]
is equal to the number of hyperedges in
G_{v}
.
The vertex set of the new hypergraph
G_{v}
is defined as
V^{\prime}\triangleq V\setminus V_{\chi(v)}
, where
\chi(v)
is an index in
[k]
such that
v\in V^{\mathrm{input}}_{\chi(v)}
.
The hyperedge set of
G_{v}
, denoted by
E^{\prime}
, is defined by
E^{\prime}=\left\{e\setminus\left\{v\right\}\mid e\in E\wedge v\in e\right\}
.
In words, for every hyperedge
e\in E_{V}(v)
, we define a new hyperedge
e^{\prime}
obtained by removing
v
from
e
. We then add
e^{\prime}
to
E^{\prime}
.
Clearly,
\left\lvert E^{\prime}\right\rvert
is equal to the degree of
v
in
G[V]
.

We use
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}^{\prime}
to denote a new oracle over
G_{v}
.
We explain how to simulate
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}^{\prime}
over
G_{v}
, using a
Hyperedge-Oracle
over
G
.
To simulate the query
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}^{\prime}(U)
, we query the set
U\cup\left\{v\right\}
to
Hyperedge-Oracle
and output accordingly.
We execute the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}(V^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},\varepsilon)
on the graph
G_{v}
, to
get an approximation for the number of hyperedges in
G_{v}
with additive error
\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon
, where
\sigma_{v}\triangleq\max\{d_{v},\Lambda^{\prime}\}
.

Let
\hat{s}
denote the output of the call
\mathbf{GuessApx}\left(V^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},\varepsilon\right)
.
We need to prove that
\hat{s}=d_{v}\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
.
If
\hat{s}\neq``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
, then
\hat{s}=d_{v}(1\pm\varepsilon)
with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
, by the correctness of
Theorem

11
.
Since
d_{v}(1\pm\varepsilon)\subseteq d_{v}\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon
, we get that
\hat{s}=d_{v}\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
.

We are left with the case that the output
\hat{s}
is
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
.
If
\Lambda^{\prime}\leq d_{v}/2
, then the probability that
\hat{s}=``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
is at most
1/n^{6}
. This means that if
\Lambda^{\prime}\leq d_{v}/2
, then
\hat{s}=d_{v}\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
.
So, if
\hat{s}
is
``L\text{ is too large}^{\prime\prime}
, we can assume that
\Lambda^{\prime}>d_{v}/2
.
In this case, we proceed by executing the algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(V^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},\varepsilon\right)
, detailed in
Lemma

16
, with access to the detection oracle
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}^{\prime}
. Let
\hat{s}_{2}
denote its output.
We output
\hat{s}_{2}
as the output of the algorithm
\mathbf{Deg\mhyphen Approx}_{\varepsilon}(v,\Lambda^{\prime},V)
.
We prove that
\hat{s}_{2}=d_{v}\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
.
Since
\Lambda^{\prime}\geq d_{v}/2
the algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(V^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},\varepsilon\right)
outputs
\hat{s}_{2}=d_{v}\pm\Lambda^{\prime}\cdot\varepsilon/8
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
, by the correctness of
Lemma

16
.
Note that
\Lambda^{\prime}\geq d_{v}/2
implies that
\sigma_{v}\leq 2\Lambda^{\prime}
,
and therefore the output
\hat{s}_{2}=d_{v}\pm\sigma_{v}\cdot\varepsilon/4
with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
. This completes the proof of the correctness of the algorithm
\mathbf{Deg\mhyphen Approx}_{\varepsilon}(v,\Lambda^{\prime},V)
.

We analyze the running time. The algorithm
\mathbf{Deg\mhyphen Approx}_{\varepsilon}(v,\Lambda,V)
makes a single call to the algorithm
\mathbf{GuessApx}\left(V^{\prime},\Lambda/2,\varepsilon\right)
, and a single call to the algorithm
\mathbf{MedianApx}\left(V^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime},\varepsilon\right)
,
which completes the proof of
Lemma

19
.
∎

6
Implementation of the
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
Procedure

In this section we implement the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
, using the
Hyperedge-Oracle
.
We use the following notation. For every
\Lambda
let
\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}=\Lambda/(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}=\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}/4^{k}
. Recall that we say that a vertex is
\Lambda
-heavy if its degree is at least
\Lambda
, and say that it is
\Lambda
-light otherwise.
Recall that we defined the measure
\mu
on a subset
U\subseteq V^{\mathrm{input}}
by
\mu(U)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}|U\cap V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}|
.
The main theorem of this section is the following.

Theorem 20

(
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
)
.

There exists a randomized algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
that gets as input the set of vertices
V
and a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
.
The algorithm outputs a set of vertices
V_{\Lambda}
that contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices and no
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices, with probability at least
1-1/n^{4}
.
The algorithm makes at most
O\left(k^{3}\log(k)\cdot\log^{k+2}n\cdot m_{v}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}\right)
queries to the
Hyperedge-Oracle
, while querying only sets of vertices
U
for which
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/\Lambda)
.

A corollary from
Theorem

20
, which we use in
Section

3
, is the following.

Theorem 21

(
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
)
.

There exists a randomized algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
that gets as input a set of vertices
V
, a heaviness threshold
\Lambda
, and a threshold
\zeta
.
The algorithm either outputs
\bot
, or a set of vertices
V_{\Lambda}
containing at most
\zeta
vertices, such that with probability at least
1-1/n^{4}
, the set
V_{\Lambda}
contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices and no
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices.
If
km_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\leq\zeta
, then the probability that the algorithm outputs
\bot
is at most
1/n^{4}
.
The algorithm makes at most
O(\psi\cdot\zeta)
queries to
Hyperedge-Oracle
, where
\psi\triangleq 4^{k}\cdot k^{2}\log(k)\cdot\log^{k+2}n
.
The algorithm does so
while querying only sets of vertices
U
for which
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/\Lambda)
.

Informally, the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
is a truncated version of the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
.
It simulates the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
, but stops the simulation if the number of queries made by the algorithm exceeds
O\left(\psi\cdot\zeta\right)
, or the computed set is of size larger than
\zeta
and outputs
\bot
.
Otherwise, the algorithm outputs the set of vertices computed by
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
.
If the condition
km_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\leq\zeta
holds, then the probability that the algorithm outputs
\bot
is at most
1/n^{4}
.
We need the truncated version of the algorithm to get keep the number of queries made to the
Hyperedge-Oracle
small, while also only querying sets of vertices
U
for which
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/\Lambda)
.
Following is a formal proof.

Proof of
Theorem

21
using
Theorem

20
.
Let
C
be the constant in the statement of
Theorem

20
.
That is, the number of times the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
queries the
Hyperedge-Oracle
is at most
(C\cdot k^{2}\log(k)\cdot\log^{k+2}n\cdot m_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
, where this number of queries can also be written as
(\psi\cdot km_{V}/\Lambda)
.
The algorithm for
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
works as follows.
It simulates the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
and stops the simulation if the number of times the procedure queries the oracle exceeds
C\cdot\psi\cdot\zeta
.
If the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
does not complete within
C\cdot\psi\cdot\zeta
queries, or it does but the computed set of heavy vertices is of size larger than
\zeta
, then the algorithm outputs
\bot
.
Otherwise, the algorithm outputs the set of vertices computed by
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
.

To complete the proof, we need to show that if
km_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\leq\zeta
, then the probability that the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}
outputs
\bot
is at most
1/n^{4}
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
denote the event that the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
computes a set of vertices
V_{\Lambda}
that contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices and no
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices, which happens with probability at least
1-1/n^{4}
.
In other words, the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
denotes the event that the set
V_{\Lambda}
contains only
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy vertices and therefore contains at most
km_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
vertices, which is at most
\zeta
by the assumption.
Recall that by the guarantee of
Theorem

20
the number of queries made by the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
is at most
C\cdot\psi\cdot km_{V}
, which is at most
C\cdot\psi\cdot\zeta
, by the assumption that
km_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}\leq\zeta
.
This means that if
\mathcal{E}_{1}
occurs, then the simulation of the execution of the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
by the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
completes within
C\cdot\psi\cdot\zeta
queries, and with a set of vertices
V_{\Lambda}
is of size at most
km_{v}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
which is at most
\zeta
.
Therefore, conditioned on the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
outputs the set of vertices
V_{\Lambda}
computed by
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
, and not
\bot
, with probability
1
.
As the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}
occurs with probability at least
1-1/n^{4}
, we get that the probability that the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}_{\bot}^{\zeta}(V,\Lambda)
outputs
\bot
is at most
1/n^{4}
.
∎

It thus remains to prove
Theorem

20
. We first define the required notation, and then provide an overview of the algorithm specified in the theorem.
Let
V^{\mathrm{input}}=(V^{\mathrm{input}}_{1},\ldots,V^{\mathrm{input}}_{k})
be a set of vertices and let
E
be a set of
k
-uniform hyperedges, where each hyperedge
e\in E
contains exactly one vertex from each
V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}
for
i\in[k]
.
For a vector
P=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\subseteq[0,1]^{k}
, we define the weight of
P
, denoted by
w(P)
, as
\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}
. We say that a vector
P
is a
simple
if for every
i\in[k]
, we have
p_{i}=2^{-j}
for some
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\left\lceil\log(n)\right\rceil\right\}
.
We also define the following set of vectors.

Definition 2

(
\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda)
)
.

For every integer
k
let
\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda)
be a subset of vectors in
[0,1]^{k}
, which contains all vectors
P\in[0,1]^{k}
that satisfy
w(P)\leq 1/\Lambda
, and are simple. A vector
P=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
is simple if for every
i\in[k]
, we have
p_{i}=2^{-j}
for some
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\left\lceil\log(n)\right\rceil\right\}
.

Note that the set
\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda)
contains at most
(\log(n)+2)^{k}\leq\log^{k}(4n)
vectors, as there are at most
\log(n)+2
possible values for each coordinate of a vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda)
.

The key to proving
Theorem

20
lies in sampling an induced subhypergraph of
G
using the vectors in
\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda)
.
For a vector
P\in[0,1]^{k}
, we define the random varaible
V[P]
to be the set of vertices obtained by sampling each vertex
v\in V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}
with probability
p_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
. We call this the
P
-discovery experiment.
We denote by
A_{V[P]}
the set of non-isolated vertices in the induced random graph
G[V[P]]
, which are the vertices with non-zero degree in
G[V[P]]
. We also say that each vertex in
A_{V[P]}
is discovered by
P
or
P
-discovered.
The next lemma provides a characterization on the probability that a vertex is discovered by a vector
P
.

Lemma 22

(Sampling Lemma)
.

Let
G
be a
k
-partite hypergraph with
n
vertices and
m
hyperedges.
Then, there exists a
simple
vector
P\in[0,1]^{k}
such that
m\cdot w(P)\geq(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}
, for which the random induced graph
G[V[P]]
contains at least one hyperedge with probability at least
(1-1/e)^{k}
.

We use this lemma to show that for any
\Lambda
-heavy vertex

v
there exists a vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
such that
v
is discovered by
P
with probability at least
(1-1/e)^{k}
.
We use
v[P]
to denote the probability that
v
is discovered by
P
.
To relate the lemma which operates on the entire graph to a specific vertex we can make the following reduction.
Fix some vertex
v
, and define a new subhypergraph
H
of
G
, which contains only the hyperedges from
G
that contain
v
.
We claim that the probability that
H[V[P]]
contains at least one edge is equal to
v[P]
, the probability that
v
is discovered by
P
over
G
.
We can now get a a lower bound on
v[P]
by applying the sampling lemma over
H
.

The main application of the sampling lemma is to find a superset of the
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy vertices, which contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices.
Using the sampling lemma, we know that by performing the
P
-discovery experiment for a vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
, we can find a superset of the
\Lambda
-vertices.
We later show that the probability that a
