\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertex is discovered by some vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
is at most
1/4^{k}
.

Roadmap.

The rest of this section is organized as follows.
In
Section

6.1
we provide some claims we need for the proof of
Theorem

20
.
In
Section

6.2
we prove
Theorem

20
given
Lemma

22
, and in

Section

6.3
we prove

Lemma

22
.

6.1
Properties of the
P
-Discovery Experiment

In this subsection we prove several properties of the discovery experiment.
We show in
Lemma

24
that if a vertex is
\Lambda
-heavy then there exists a vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
that discovers it with probability at least
(1-1/e)^{k}
.
If a vertex is
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light then for any vector
P
with
w(P)\leq 1/\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}
, the probability that the vertex is discovered by
P
is at most
(1-1/e)^{k}/2
.
These two properties allow us to distinguish between
\Lambda
-heavy vertices and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices, by approximating the discovery probability of a vertex by all vectors
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
.
Also, in
Lemma

25
, we show that the expected number of vertices that are discovered by a vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
is at most
w(p)\cdot 2km_{V}\cdot\log(2km_{V})
.

For a vector
P\in[0,1]^{k}
and vertex
v\in V
, we use
v[P]
to denote the probability that
v\in A_{V[P]}
. We refer to this event as the event that
v
is discovered by
P
.
For each vector
P
we define the set
S_{P}
to be the set of vertices for which
v[P]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
.
The following claim, used in the proofs of both lemmas, provides a simple upper bound on
v[P]
using Markov’s inequality.

Claim 23
.

For every vertex
v
and every vector
P\in[0,1]^{k}
, we have that
v[P]\leq d_{v}\cdot w(P)
.

Proof.
Let
X
be a random variable equal to degree of
v
over
G[V[P]]
.
Clearly, we have that
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]=d_{v}\cdot w(P)
, as every hyperedge
e
is also in
V[P]
with probability
w(P)
.
Since
X
is a non-negative random variable that takes integral values, we get that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X>0\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X\geq 1\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]
.
We use Markov’s inequality and get that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X>0\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]
.
Overall, we get

\displaystyle v[P]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X>0\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]=d_{v}\cdot w(P)\;,

as claimed.
∎

The next lemma draws a connection between the heaviness of a vertex and its discovery probability.

Lemma 24
.

Fix some
\Lambda
. Recall that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}=\Lambda/(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}
and let
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}=\Lambda/(4^{k}(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}})
.

1.

For every
\Lambda
-heavy vertex
v
there exists a vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
such that
v[P]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
.

2.

For every
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertex
v
and any vector
P
with
w(P)\leq 1/\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}
, we have that
v[P]\leq(1-1/e)^{k}/2
.

Proof.
We start with Item (1).
Fix a
\Lambda
-heavy vertex
v
.
Define an auxiliary hypergraph
G_{v}
with the vertex set
V
, and the edge set
E(v)
, defined as the set of hyperedges
e\in E
for which
v\in e
.
Note that
\left\lvert E(v)\right\rvert=d_{v}
, where
d_{v}
is the degree of
v
, which satisfies
d_{v}\geq\Lambda
since
v
is
\Lambda
-heavy.
We apply the sampling lemma (
Lemma

22
) on
G_{v}
, and get that there exists a simple vector
P
with
w(P)\leq\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}

such that the probability that
G_{v}[V[P]]
contains at least one hyperedge is at least
(1-1/e)^{k}
.
This follows as
d_{v}\cdot w(P)\geq d_{v}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}=(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}
, and therefore the conditions of the sampling lemma are satisfied.
Fix a vertex set
U
. Note that
G_{v}[U]
contains an hyperedge, then the degree of
v
in
G[U]
is at least one.
We have that
G_{v}[V[P]]
contains at least one hyperedge with probability at least
(1-1/e)^{k}
, therefore
v
is non-isolated in
G[V[P]]
with probability at least
(1-1/e)^{k}
. In other words,
v[P]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
.
This completes the proof of Item (1).
Item (2) follows from
Claim

23
, as

\displaystyle v[P]\leq d_{v}\cdot w(P)\leq\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}=1/4^{k}\leq(1-1/e)^{k}/2\;,

where we use the fact that
d_{v}\leq\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
.
∎The following claim provides a bound on the expected number of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices that are discovered by some vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
.
A trivial bound we get from
Claim

23
is that this expectation is at most
n/4^{k}
, which is too weak for our purposes.

Lemma 25
.

Fix some simple vector
P
.
Sample a random set of vertices
V[P]
, then
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert A_{V[P]}\right\rvert\right]\leq 2km_{V}\cdot w(P)\cdot\log(2km_{V})
.

Proof.
We partition the vertices of
V
into classes based on their degrees, as follows.
Let
W_{j}=1/(2^{j}\cdot w(P))
for every
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\log(km_{V})\right\}
, and define

\displaystyle Q_{j}=\left\{v\in V\mid d_{v}\in[W_{j},W_{j-1})\right\},\;Q_{0}=\left\{v\in V\mid d_{v}\geq W_{0}\right\}.

We claim that:

(1)

For every
j
, we have
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\leq km_{V}/W_{j}
.

(2)

For every
j
and every vertex
v\in Q_{j}
, we have that
v[P]\leq 2/2^{j}
.

Using the above claims, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert A_{U}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in V}v[P]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\log(km_{V})}\sum_{v\in Q_{j}}v[P]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\log(km_{V})}\sum_{v\in Q_{j}}2/2^{j}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=0}^{\log(km_{V})}\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert/2^{j}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=0}^{\log(km_{V})}(km_{V}/W_{j})\cdot(1/2^{j})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j=0}^{\log(km_{V})}km_{V}\cdot w(P)

\displaystyle=2km_{V}\cdot w(P)\cdot(\log(km_{V})+1)

\displaystyle=2km_{V}\cdot w(P)\cdot\log(2km_{V})\;.

which completes the proof.
The first inequality follows by Item
(2)
and the second inequality follows by Item
(1)
.

We now prove Items (1) and (2).
Item (2) follows from
Claim

23
, which states that
v[P]\leq d_{v}\cdot w(P)
.
Fix a vertex
v\in Q_{j}
, and note that
d_{v}\in[W_{j},W_{j-1})
, where
W_{j}=1/(2^{j}\cdot w(P))
. Therefore, we get that

v[P]\leq\frac{W_{j-1}}{w(P)}=2/2^{j}
.

To prove Item (1), we use a counting argument.
Note that the sum of the degrees of all vertices in a
k
-partite hypergraph
is equal to
km_{V}
, as every edge contributes
k
to this sum.
Therefore, for every positive
X
there are at most
km_{V}/X
vertices with degree at least
X
. In our case, all vertices in
Q_{j}
have degree at least
W_{j}
and therefore we get that
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\leq km_{V}/W_{j}
.
∎

6.2
Implementation of the Procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}

In this subsection we explain how to implement the procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
using a Hyperedge-Oracle.
The procedure
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
finds a superset of the
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy vertices, which contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices.
The procedure queries Hyperedge-Oracle at most
\tilde{O}(km/\Lambda)
queries.
It gives Hyperedge-Oracle only sets of vertices
U
for which
\mu(U)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}|U\cap V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}|\leq\tilde{O}(n^{k}/\Lambda)
.
We explain the connection between the heaviness threshold
\Lambda
and the parameters
\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
.
An ideal algorithm would output a set of vertices that contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices and no
(\Lambda-1)
-light vertices.
However, we need to allow some slack in the heaviness threshold. Thus,
our algorithm finds a superset
V_{\Lambda}
of the
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-heavy vertices (thus does not contain any
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices), which contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices. To get the desired set of vertices, we run the
P
-discovery experiment
for every vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
.
Each
\Lambda
-heavy vertex is discovered with probability at least
\alpha
by some vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
, as shown in
Lemma

24
. Also by

Lemma

24
, each
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertex is discovered with probability at most
\alpha/2
, by any vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
.
Intuitively, we need the weight of the vector
P
to be between the heaviness thresholds
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
.

A rough description of the
algorithm presented in this subsection is as follows.
Run the
P
-discovery experiment for every vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
and output the set of vertices that are discovered sufficiently many times.
On top of this, we need to add a truncated search, which simulates the previous algorithm, with a twist. Whenever the truncated search realizes that the current iteration is going to take too many queries, or the computed set is too large, it stops this iteration and moves on to the next one.

The following aforementioned theorem is the main result of this section.
See
20

We need the following lemma to prove the theorem, which can be thought of as an algorithmic version of
Lemma

24
.

Lemma 26
.

There exists a randomized algorithm
\mathbf{Discovery\mhyphen Experiment}(V,P)
that gets as input the set of vertices
V
and a vector
P\in[0,1]^{k}
.
The algorithm outputs a set of vertices
\hat{S}_{P}
, such that with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
, for every
v\in V
, we have that

1.

If
v
satisfies
v[P]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
, then
v\in\hat{S}_{P}
.

2.

If
v
satisfies
v[P]\leq(1-1/e)^{k}/2
, then
v\not\in\hat{S}_{P}
.

The algorithm queries the oracle at most
O\left(k^{3}\log(k)\cdot\log^{2}n\cdot m\cdot w(P)\right)
times, while querying only sets of vertices
U
for which
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)\cdot w(P))
.

Recall that for a vector
P=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
, we use
w(P)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}
.
We explain how to use
Lemma

26
to prove
Theorem

20
.
Let
S_{H}
denote the set of vertices, for which there exists a vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
such that
v[P]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
, and let
S_{L}
denote the set of vertices for which every vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
satisfies
v[P]\leq(1-1/e)^{k}/2
.
The two sets are disjoint by definition.
Clearly, we can compute a set
\hat{S}
that contains all vertices in
S_{H}
and no vertices in
S_{L}
, by running the algorithm
\mathbf{Discovery\mhyphen Experiment}(V,P)
for every vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
and taking the union of all sets
\hat{S}_{P}
.
To prove
Theorem

20
, it suffices to show that the computed set
\hat{S}
contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices and no
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices, which follows from
Lemma

24
.

Proof of
Theorem

20
using
Lemma

26
.
The algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V,\Lambda)
works as follows.
For every vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
execute the algorithm
\mathbf{Discovery\mhyphen Experiment}(V,P)
and let
\hat{S}_{P}
be its output.
Compute the set
\hat{S}
, which is the union of all sets
\hat{S}_{P}
, and output
\hat{S}
.

We prove that the set
\hat{S}
contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices and no
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices, with probability at least
1-1/n^{4}
, based on the properties of
Lemma

26
.

Lemma

24
promises that for every
\Lambda
-heavy vertex
v
, there exists a vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
such that
v[P]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
.
Fix a vertex
v
which is
\Lambda
-heavy and a vector
P
that satisfies
v[P]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
.
By
Lemma

26
, we have that every vertex
v
for which
v[P]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
is in the set
\hat{S}_{P}
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
.
We apply a union bound over all
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
to get that the set
\hat{S}
contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices with probability at least
1-\left\lvert\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})\right\rvert/n^{5}
.

Next, we show that for every
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
, we have that
\hat{S}_{P}
does not contain any
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertex.

Lemma

24
promises that for every
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertex
v
and any vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
, we have
v[P]\leq(1-1/e)^{k}/2
.
Fix a vertex
v
which is
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light and a vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
.
By
Lemma

26
, we have that every vertex
v
for which
v[P]\leq(1-1/e)^{k}/2
is in the set
\hat{S}_{P}
, with probability at most
1/n^{5}
.
We apply a union bound over all
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
to get that the set
\hat{S}
contains a
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertex with probability at most
\left\lvert\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})\right\rvert/n^{5}
.

Overall, we get that the set
\hat{S}
contains all
\Lambda
-heavy vertices and no
\Lambda_{\mathrm{low}}
-light vertices, with probability at least
1-(2\log n)^{k}/n^{5}\geq 1-1/n^{4}
, where
\left\lvert\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})\right\rvert\leq(\log(4n))^{k}
, which follows from its definition.

To bound the running time of the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
, note that the described algorithm makes
\left\lvert\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})\right\rvert
calls to the algorithm
\mathbf{Discovery\mhyphen Experiment}(V,P)
, and that the number of queries to
Hyperedge-Oracle
made by each such call is at most

\displaystyle O\left(k^{3}\log(k)\cdot\log^{2}n\cdot m_{v}\cdot w(P)\right)=O\left(k^{3}\log(k)\cdot\log^{2}n\cdot m_{v}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}\right)\;.

Therefore, the total number of queries to
Hyperedge-Oracle
is at most

\displaystyle O\left(k^{3}\log(k)\cdot\log^{k+2}n\cdot m_{V}/\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}\right)\;.

The sets of vertices that are queried by the algorithm
\mathbf{Discovery\mhyphen Experiment}(V,P)
are sets
U
for which
\mu(U)=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)\cdot w(P))=\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
, where the last equality follows because
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}})
, and therefore
w(P)\leq{1/\Lambda_{\mathrm{mid}}}
.
This gives us the desired bound on the running time of the algorithm
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
.
∎

To prove
Lemma

26
, we use the following deterministic proposition.

Proposition 27
.

For a set of vertices
U
, let
S
denote the set of non-isolated vertices over
G[U]
.
There exists a deterministic algorithm
\mathbf{Find\mhyphen Non\mhyphen Isolated}(U,\sigma)
, that takes as input a set of vertices
U
and a threshold
\sigma\geq 2
.
If
\left\lvert S\right\rvert\geq\sigma
then the algorithm outputs
\bot
. Otherwise, the algorithm outputs the set
S
.
The algorithm makes at most
O\left(k\cdot\sigma\cdot\log n\right)
queries to the
Hyperedge-Oracle
, while only querying sets of vertices
U^{\prime}
that are contained in
U
.

We explain how to use
Proposition

27
to prove
Lemma

26
.
The core of the algorithm
\mathbf{Discovery\mhyphen Experiment}(V,P)
specified in
Lemma

26
is to
run the
P
-discovery experiment, which includes sampling a subset of vertices
U\leftarrow V[P]
and computing the set of vertices
A_{U}
, which is the set of non-isolated vertices in the induced subgraph
G[U]
.
To compute this set, we use the algorithm
\mathbf{Find\mhyphen Non\mhyphen Isolated}
.

We add two additional steps on top of the
P
-discovery experiment, which are amplification and doubling.
We describe them in the following.
We fix some threshold
\sigma
and repeat the discovery experiment
r=\Omega(\log n)
times.
If the number of iterations in which the algorithm outputs
\bot
is too large, then we increase the threshold
\sigma
by a factor of
2
and repeat the process.
Otherwise, we output the set of vertices that are discovered in at least
3r\cdot q/4
iterations, where
q=(1-1/e)^{k}
.

We use doubling to find the correct threshold for the following reason.
If the threshold is too low, then the algorithm might output
\bot
even if
all vertices
v
for which
v[P]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
are discovered. On the other hand, if the threshold is too high, then the query-measure of the algorithm might be too high.

We note that the expected number of discovered vertices is at most
km_{V}\cdot w(P)\cdot\log(2km_{V})
, by
Lemma

25
.
We also prove that w.h.p. the final threshold we use is indeed bounded by

O(km_{V}\cdot w(P)\cdot\log(2km_{V}))
.
The formal proof is as follows.

Proof of
Lemma

26
using
Proposition

27
.
We provide an implementation for the algorithm

\mathbf{Discovery\mhyphen Experiment}
specified in
Lemma

26
, using the algorithm
\mathbf{Find\mhyphen Non\mhyphen Isolated}
specified in
Proposition

27
.

Algorithm 4

Implementation of the algorithm
\mathbf{Discovery\mhyphen Experiment}(V,P)
, specified in
Lemma

26
.

Input:

A set of vertices
V
and a vector
P\in[0,1]^{k}
.

Output:

A set of vertices
\hat{R}
, containing all vertices
v
for which
v[P]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
and no vertex
v
for which
v[P]\leq(1-1/e)^{k}/2
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
.

1

Let
q\leftarrow(1-1/e)^{k}
, and
r\leftarrow 600\log n/q
;

2

for

j=0
to
j=\log n

do

3

Set
\hat{c}_{v}\leftarrow 0
for every vertex
v
,
b_{j}\leftarrow 0
, and
\sigma=2^{j}
;

4

for

i=1
to
i=r

do

5

Sample a set of vertices
U_{i,j}\leftarrow V[P]
\quad\triangleright

Let
S_{i,j}
denote the set of non-isolated vertices in
G[U_{i,j}]
.

6

if

\left\lvert U_{i,j}\right\rvert\geq\mathbb{E}\left[U_{i,j}\right]\cdot(6\log n)^{k}

then

Continue to the next iteration

7

Define
\hat{S}_{i,j}\leftarrow\mathbf{Find\mhyphen Non\mhyphen Isolated}(U_{i,j},\sigma\cdot 100/q)
;

8

if

\hat{S}_{i,j}=\bot

then

b_{j}\leftarrow b_{j}+1

9

for

every vertex
v\in\hat{S}_{i,j}

do

10

\hat{c}_{v}\leftarrow\hat{c}_{v}+1
.

11

if

b_{j}\leq r\cdot q/10

then

12

\hat{R}_{j}\leftarrow\left\{v\mid\hat{c}_{v}\geq 3rq/4\right\}
\quad\triangleright

The set
\hat{R}_{j}
to be the set of vertices
v
for which
\hat{c}_{v}\geq 3rq/4
.

13

return

\hat{R}_{j}
.

We use the following notation.
For every
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\log n\right\}
and
i\in[r]
we use
U_{i,j}
to denote the set of vertices sampled in the
i
-th iteration with threshold
\sigma=2^{j}
.
We also use
S_{i,j}
to denote the set of non-isolated vertices over
G[U_{i,j}]
,
and
\hat{S}_{i,j}
to denote the output of the call
\mathbf{Find\mhyphen Non\mhyphen Isolated}(U_{i,j},2^{j}\cdot 100/q)
.
For fixed
j
the algorithm maintains a counter
\hat{c}_{v}
for every vertex
v
, which counts the number of times the vertex
v
is contained in the set
\hat{S}_{i,j}
.
The algorithm also maintains a counter
b_{j}
that counts the number of times the algorithm outputs
\bot
, with threshold
\sigma=2^{j}
.
We also define a set
\hat{S}_{P}
to be the set of vertices
v
for which
\hat{c}_{v}\geq 3rq/4
,
For the analysis, we keep a counter
c_{v}
for every vertex that counts the number of times the vertex
v
is in the set
S_{i,j}
.
We emphasize that we do not have access to the value of
c_{v}
or to the set
S_{i,j}
, and we only use them for the analysis.
We do have access to the counters
\hat{c}_{v}
and the sets
\hat{S}_{i,j}
.

We make the following observation.
If
\hat{S}_{i,j}
is not
\bot
, then the sets
\hat{S}_{i,j}
and
S_{i,j}
are equal.
This follows by the guarantee of the algorithm
\mathbf{Find\mhyphen Non\mhyphen Isolated}
, provided in
Proposition

27
, which is a deterministic algorithm.
Therefore, for every
v
and fixed
j
we have
c_{v}-b\leq\hat{c}_{v}\leq c_{v}
.
We use this fact together with a concentration bound on
c_{v}
to prove a concentration bound on
\hat{c}_{v}
.
We prove the correctness of the algorithm by proving the following two properties.

1.

For any
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\log n\right\}
, if
b_{j}\leq rq/10
, then the set
\hat{S}_{P}
contains all vertices
v
with
v[P]\geq q
and no vertex
v
with
v[P]\leq q/2
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
.

2.

For
j
such that
2^{j}\geq 2km_{V}\cdot w(P)\cdot\log(2km_{V})
, the probability that
b_{j}>rq/10
is at most
1/n^{5}
.

We start with proving Property (1). We fix some iteration
j
for which
\hat{S}_{P}\neq\bot
.
We show that

1.

For every vertex
v
with
v[P]\geq q
, we have that
c_{v}\geq q\cdot r\cdot 5/6
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.

2.

For every vertex
v
with
v[P]\geq q/2
, we have that
c_{v}<q\cdot r\cdot 3/4
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.

Note that
c_{v}
is a binomial random variable with
r
trials and success probability
v[P]
.
We now apply Chernoff’s inequality.
For a vertex
v
with
v[P]\geq q
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[c_{v}<q\cdot r\cdot 5/6\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[c_{v}<q\cdot r\cdot(1-1/6)\right]\leq\exp(-q\cdot r/100)\leq 1/n^{6}\;.

For
v
with
v[P]\leq q/2
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[c_{v}\geq q\cdot r\cdot 3/4\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[c_{v}\geq q\cdot r/2\cdot(1+1/2)\right]\leq\exp(-q\cdot r/10)\leq 1/n^{6}\;.

We apply a union bound over all
n
vertices, and get that for every vertex
v
with
v[P]\geq q
we have that
c_{v}\geq 5r/6
, and for every vertex
v
with
v[P]\geq q/2
we have that
c_{v}\leq 3r/4
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
.
Using the assumption that
b_{j}\leq rq/10
, we get that if
c_{v}\geq r\cdot 5/6
then
\hat{c}_{v}\geq 3rq/4
, and if
c_{v}\leq r\cdot 3/4
then
\hat{c}_{v}<3rq/4
.
Therefore, we conclude that if
b_{j}\leq rq/10
, then the set
\hat{S}_{P}
contains all vertices
v
with
v[P]\geq q
and no vertex
v
with
v[P]\leq q/2
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{5}
.

We now prove Property (2).
That is, we prove that for
2^{j}\geq 2km_{V}\cdot w(P)
, the probability that
b>rq/10
is at most
1/n^{5}
.
Let
X
be a random variable that is equal to
A_{V[P]}
.
By
Lemma

25
, we have that
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]\leq 2km_{V}\cdot w(P)\cdot\log(2km_{V})
. By Markov’s inequality, we have that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X\geq\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]\cdot 100/q\right]\leq q/100\;.

Therefore, if
2^{j}\geq km_{V}\cdot w(P)\cdot\log(2km_{V})
, then
b_{j}
is a binomial random variable with
r
trials and success probability at most
q/100
, and therefore its expectation is at most
r\cdot q/100
, which we denote by
\beta
.
We apply Chernoff’s inequality on
b_{j}
, under the assumption that
\beta\geq\mathbb{E}\left[b_{j}\right]
, and get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[b_{j}\geq r\cdot q/10\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[b_{j}\geq 10\beta\right]\leq 2^{-10\beta}\leq 1/n^{5}\;.

This completes the proof of the two properties.

It remains to analyze the running time of the algorithm.
The number of calls to the algorithm
\mathbf{Find\mhyphen Non\mhyphen Isolated}
is at most
r\cdot\log n
.
In each such call the threshold is at most
km_{V}\cdot w(P)
by
Lemma

25
.
This means that each such call makes at most
O\left(k^{2}\cdot m_{V}\cdot w(P)\cdot\log(2km_{V})\cdot\log n\right)
queries to the oracle
Hyperedge-Oracle
, by
Proposition

27
.
Therefore, the number of queries to the oracle
Hyperedge-Oracle
is at most

\displaystyle O\left(k^{2}\cdot m_{V}\cdot w(P)\cdot\log(2km_{V})\cdot\log n\cdot r\right)=O\left(k^{3}\log(k)\cdot m\cdot w(P)\cdot\log^{2}n\right)\;,

where we use the fact that
r=O(\log n)
, and that
m_{V}\leq n^{k}
.
We also note that the algorithm only queries sets of vertices
U
obtained by sampling
V[P]
. Using
Claim

10
, we have that
\mu(U)\leq O\left(\mu(V)\cdot w(P)\cdot(6\log n)^{k}\right)
, with probability at least
1-1/n^{6}
.
Therefore, the algorithm only queries sets of vertices
U
for which
\mu(U)\leq\tilde{O}(\mu(V)\cdot w(P))
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In what follows we prove
Proposition

27
.
Given a set
U
, and a threshold
\sigma
we would like to find the subset of vertices
S
of non-isolated vertices over
G[U]
, assuming
\left\lvert S\right\rvert\leq\sigma
, where otherwise we output
\bot
.
We focus on finding
S\cap V^{\mathrm{input}}_{1}
, that is the set of vertices in
S
that are contained in
V^{\mathrm{input}}_{1}
, where we compute
S\cap V^{\mathrm{input}}_{i}
seperately for
i\in[k]
.
Informally, we look at a single part
U_{1}=V^{\mathrm{input}}_{1}\cap U
, and partition it into
\sigma
disjoint sets
L^{1},\ldots,L^{\tau}
.
We then query the
Hyperedge-Oracle
on the sets
R_{i}=(U\setminus U_{1})\cup L^{i}
for
i\in[\sigma]
. In words,
R_{i}
is the entire set
U
, where the only set of vertices from
V^{\mathrm{input}}_{1}
that are in
R_{i}
are those in
L^{i}
.
If there are less than
\sigma
vertices in
S
, then there exists at least one set
L^{i}
for which
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}(R_{i})=0
, and therefore we can discard all vertices that are in
L^{i}
, as none of them are in
S
.
Following is the formal proof.

Proof of
Proposition

27
.
We use the following notation. Let
U_{h}=U\cap V^{\mathrm{input}}_{h}
for
h\in[k]
.
We explain how to either find the set of vertices
S\cap U_{1}
, or find a witness for
\left\lvert S\cap U_{1}\right\rvert\geq\sigma
, in which case we output
\bot
.
The same process can be applied to find
S\cap U_{h}
for other values
h\in[k]
.
We initialize a set
L\leftarrow U_{1}
, and repeat the following process:

1.

Partition the vertices of
L
into
\sigma
disjoint sets
L^{1},\ldots,L^{\sigma}
, as balanced as possible, such that each set has size in
\left\{\lfloor\left\lvert L\right\rvert/\sigma\rfloor,\lceil\left\lvert L\right\rvert/\sigma\rceil\right\}
.
For each
i\in[\sigma]
, define the set
R_{i}=(U\setminus U_{1})\cup L^{i}
.

2.

Query the
Hyperedge-Oracle
on every set
R_{i}
, i.e., invoke
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}(R_{i})
for each
i\in[\sigma]
.

3.

Remove from
L
all vertices that are not contained in a set
L^{i}
for which
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}(R_{i})=1
. If no such set exists, we exit the loop, and otherwise go back to step 1.

We first analyze the case where
\left\lvert S\cap U_{1}\right\rvert<\sigma
.
In this case, as long as there are at least
\sigma
non-empty sets in
L
, there exists at least one set
L^{i}
which does not contain a vertex from
S
, and therefore
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}(R_{i})=0
.
Therefore, the process removes at least a
1/(\sigma+1)
fraction of the vertices in
L
in each iteration, which means that after at most
2\sigma\log n
iterations, there less than
\sigma
vertices left in
L
.
We then make
\left\lvert L\right\rvert
trivial queries:
we invoke the query
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}((U\setminus U_{1})\cup\left\{v\right\})
, for
v\in L
, which is the set of all vertices not in
U_{1}
together with a single vertex
v
from
L
.
Define
S_{1}
to be the set of vertices
v
for which
\text{Hyperedge-Oracle}\left((U\setminus U_{1})\cup\left\{v\right\}\right)=1
. We then output the set
S_{1}
.

We now analyze the case where
\left\lvert S\cap U_{1}\right\rvert\geq\sigma
.
In this case, There exists an iteration
j
in which no set is removed from
L
,
and there are at least
\sigma
non-empty sets in
L
at this iteration.
The sets
R_{1},\ldots,R^{\sigma}
are the witnesses that
\left\lvert S\cap U_{1}\right\rvert\geq\sigma
, and therefore the algorithm outputs
\bot
.
∎

6.3
Proof of
Lemma

22

In this subsection we prove
Lemma

22
.
See
22

We work with the following notation.
Recall that we consider the following set of vectors.
See
2

For every vector
P\subseteq[0,1]^{k}
, we define the random variable
\mathcal{X}(V[P])
as an indicator random variable for the event that the random induced graph

G[V[P]]
contains at least one hyperedge.
Therefore, a different formulation of
Lemma

22
is as follows.

Lemma 28

(Equivalent Sampling Lemma)
.

If
G
has at least
m=m^{\prime}\cdot(k\log(4n))^{k^{2}}
edges then there exists a simple vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(m^{\prime})
such that
\mathcal{X}(V[P])=1
with probability at least
(1-1/e)^{k}
.

We prove
Lemma

28
by induction on
k
.
The base case is when
k=2
, which we prove in the following.

Proof of the Base Case.

Let
K_{0}=\log(m^{\prime})+1
, and let
W_{i}\triangleq m^{\prime}/2^{i}
.
For
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
, let
P_{i}=(\tfrac{2^{i}}{m^{\prime}},\tfrac{1}{2^{i}})
, and let

Z_{i}=e(G[P_{i}])
, denote a random variable that counts the number of edges in the random induced subgraph
G[P_{i}]
.
We partition
V_{2}
into classes as follows. For
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
, let

\displaystyle Q_{i}=\left\{u\in V_{2}\mid\deg(u)\in\left[W_{i},2\cdot W_{i}\right)\right\}\;,

\displaystyle Q_{0}=\left\{u\in V_{2}\mid\deg(u)\geq W_{0}\right\}\;.

In words,
u\in Q_{i}
if and only if its degree is at least
W_{i}
and less than
2W_{i}
. The set
Q_{0}
consist of all vertices
u\in V_{2}
, with degree at least
W_{0}=m^{\prime}
.
We prove the following two claims.

1.

There exists
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
such that
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\geq 2^{j}
.

2.

For such
j
, we have
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Z_{j}>0\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{2}
.

Proving both of these claims completes the proof of the proposition.

We start with a proof of the second claim.
Fix
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
where
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\geq 2^{j}
.
For any non-empty subset
S\subseteq Q_{j}
, let
\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)
denote the event that
\left\{Q_{j}\cap V_{2}[2^{-j}]=S\right\}
.
That is,
\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)
denote the event the set of vertices that were sampled from
V_{2}\cap Q_{j}
is exactly the set
S
.
Let
N(S)
denote the neighbors of the set
S
(in
V_{1}
)
Let
\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)
denote the event that
\left\{V_{1}[1/W_{j}]\cap N(S)\neq\emptyset\right\}
.
That is,
\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)
denote the event that the set of vertices that were sampled from
V_{1}\cap N(S)
is not empty.
Next, we show that

\displaystyle v[P_{j}]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Z_{j}>0\right]=\sum_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{2}\;.

(10)

For every fixed
S\subseteq Q_{j}
, which is not empty, the events
\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)
are independent, since
\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)
addresses sampling vertices from
V_{2}
, while
\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)
addresses sampling vertices from
V_{1}
, where the two samples are independent of each other.
Also note that
\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)
is contained in the event that
Z_{j}
is positive, and since the events
\left\{\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\right\}_{S\subseteq Q_{j}}
are pairwise disjoint, so are the events
\left\{\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right\}_{S\subseteq Q_{j}}
.
Therefore, the event that
Z_{j}
is positive, contains the union of the following pairwise disjoint events
\{\bigcup_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j}}\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\}
.
We get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Z_{j}>0\right]

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\bigcup_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j}}\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\right]{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]\;.

To complete the proof, we need to show that

1.

\sum_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\right]\geq 1-1/e

2.

for every
S\subseteq Q_{j}
, which is not empty, we have
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]\geq 1-1/e
.

The first claim follows as

\displaystyle\sum_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Q_{j}[2^{-j}]\neq\emptyset\right]=1-(1-2^{-j})^{\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert}\geq 1-1/e\;,

where the inequalities hold for the following reasons. The first two equalities follows from definition, and the last inequality follows from the assumption that
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\geq 2^{j}
.

The second claim follows as every non-empty subset
S\subseteq Q_{j}
satisfies
\left\lvert N(S)\right\rvert\geq W_{j}
;
To see this, fix some vertex
u\in S
, and note that
N(u)\subseteq N(S)
, and
\deg(u)=\left\lvert N(u)\right\rvert\geq W_{j}
because
u\in Q_{j}
.
Therefore, for any such
S
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[N(S)[1/W_{j}]\neq\emptyset\right]=1-(1-1/W_{j})^{\left\lvert N(S)\right\rvert}\geq 1-(1-1/W_{j})^{W_{j}}\geq 1-1/e\;.

This completes the proof of
Equation

10
.

To complete the proof of the induction base, it remains to show that the first claim indeed holds, i.e., that there exists
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
such that
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\geq 2^{j}
.
Assume towards a contradiction that this is not the case, that is we have
\left\lvert E\right\rvert\geq m
, the set
Q_{0}
is empty, and for every
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
, we have
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert<2^{j}
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lvert E\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in[V_{2}]}\deg(u)

\displaystyle<\sum_{u\in[V_{2}]}\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{0}}\chi_{\left\{u\in Q_{j}\right\}}\cdot 2\cdot W_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{0}}\sum_{u\in[V_{2}]}\chi_{\left\{u\in Q_{j}\right\}}\cdot 2\cdot W_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq k\leq K_{0}}\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\cdot 2\cdot W_{j}

Because we assumed towards a contradiction that for every
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
we have
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert<2^{j}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{0}}\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\cdot 2\cdot W_{j}<\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{0}}2^{j}\cdot 2\cdot W_{j}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{0}}2m^{\prime}=2K_{0}\cdot m^{\prime}\;.

This is a contradiction, as

\displaystyle 2K_{0}\cdot m^{\prime}=2m^{\prime}(\log(m^{\prime})+1)=2\cdot\frac{m}{6\log n}\cdot(\log(\frac{m}{6\log n})+1)\leq 2\cdot\frac{m}{6\log n}\cdot(2\log n)\leq 2m/3<m\;.

The first equality follows from the definition of
K_{0}
, the next one by the fact that
m^{\prime}=m/(6\log n)
.
This completes the proof of the induction base.

Next, we prove the induction step.
We use the following notation.
Let
\beta=k\log 4n
, and
B=m^{\prime}\cdot\beta^{(k-1)^{2}}
.
Recall that
m^{\prime}=\frac{m}{\beta^{k^{2}}}
. We also define
K_{0}=\left\lceil\log(n)\right\rceil
,
W_{j}=B/2^{j}
for
j\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
, and finally
\gamma\triangleq(\log 4n)^{k-1}
, where
\gamma\geq\left\lvert\mathsf{Product}_{k}(B)\right\rvert
.

Proof of the Induction Step of
Lemma

28
.

For every vertex
v
define
\mathcal{E}(v)=\left\{e\in\mathcal{E}\mid v\in e\right\}
, and let
d_{v}=\left\lvert\mathcal{E}(v)\right\rvert
, which we call the degree of
v
.
We partition the vertices of
V_{k}
into classes by their degrees.
For
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
, define the sets
Q_{j}
as follows.

\displaystyle Q_{j}=\left\{u\in V_{h}\mid d_{u}\in[W_{j},2W_{j})\right\}\;,

\displaystyle Q_{0}=\left\{u\in V_{h}\mid d_{u}\geq W_{0}\right\}\;.

In words,
u\in Q_{j}
if and only if its degree is at least
W_{j}
and less than
2W_{j}
. The set
Q_{0}
consist of all vertices
u\in V_{2}
, with degree at least
W_{0}=m^{\prime}
.

We claim that at least one of the following must hold.
The set
Q_{0}
is not empty, or there exists a value
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
such that
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\geq 2^{j}\cdot\gamma
.
Assuming this is true, we can complete the proof.

We first prove this assuming that the set
Q_{0}
is not empty, and let
u
denote a vertex in
Q_{0}
.
We define a new
(k-1)
-partite graph
H_{u}
as follows.
The vertex set of
H_{u}
is
V(H_{u})=V\setminus V_{k}
, and the edge set of
H_{u}
is
E(H_{U})=\left\{e\setminus\left\{u\right\}\mid e\in\mathcal{E}(u)\right\}
.
Note that
H_{u}
has at least
d_{u}
edges, where
d_{u}
is the degree of
u
in
G
.
By the induction hypothesis there exists a simple vector
P_{u}\in\mathsf{Product}_{k-1}(d_{u}^{\prime})
, where
d_{u}^{\prime}=\frac{d_{u}}{\beta^{(k-1)^{2}}}
, such that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(V(H_{u})[P_{u}])=1\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k-1}
.
To complete the proof, we extend
P_{u}
to a vector
Z_{u}\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(m^{\prime})

and show that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(V[Z_{u}])=1\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
.
The vector
Z_{u}
is defined using
P_{u}
as follows.
If
P_{u}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k-1})
, then
Z_{u}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k-1},p_{k})
, where
p_{k}=1
.
We need to show that
Z_{u}\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(m^{\prime})
, which follows as

\displaystyle w(Z_{u})=w(P_{u})=\frac{1}{d_{u}^{\prime}}=\frac{\beta^{(k-1)^{2}}}{d_{u}}\leq\frac{\beta^{(k-1)^{2}}}{B}=\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\;.

Intuitively, the vector
Z_{u}
always samples the vertex
u
.
It then operates as
P_{u}
on the remaining vertices.
To see that, note that for every realization
S\leftarrow V(H_{u})[P_{u}]
, the subgraph
H_{u}[S]
contains an edge if and only if the subgraph
G_{u}[S\cup\left\{u\right\}]
contains an edge.
Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(V[Z_{u}])=1\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(V(H_{u})[P_{u}])=1\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k-1}\;,

which completes the proof of the case in which
Q_{0}
is not empty.

The other cases are a bit more complicated.
The difficulty arises for the following reason. If the “good” set
Q_{j}
, contains more then one vertex, then it might be the case that each vector has different vector that discovers it. Therefore there is no clear way to define
P_{u}
as we did before. Moreover, choosing one such vector and extending it to a
k
-dimensional vector requires that the last coordinate of the extended vector is
1/2^{j}
, which means we choose a sampling vector
P_{u}
for some vertex
u
in
Q_{j}
, but might sample the vertex
u^{\prime}
from
Q_{j}
which has a completely different vector that discovers it.
For that reason we use a stronger assumption, which is that the set
Q_{j}
contains at least
2^{j}\cdot\gamma
vertices. Since the number of possible vectors is at most
\gamma
, we can use the pigeonhole principle to claim that there exists a vector
P
that discovers at least
2^{j}
vertices in
Q_{j}
.

Completing the proof under the assumption.

For every vertex
u\in Q_{j}
we define a new
(k-1)
-partite graph
H_{u}
as follows.
The vertex set of
H_{u}
is
V\setminus V_{k}
, and the edge set of
H_{u}
is
\left\{e\setminus\left\{u\right\}\mid\mathcal{E}(u)\right\}
.
Note that for every
u\in Q_{j}
, has degree exactly
d_{u}
which is
at least
W_{j}
, and therefore,
H_{u}
has at least
W_{j}
edges.
By the induction hypothesis, for each
H_{u}
there exists a simple vector
P_{u}\in\mathsf{Product}_{k-1}(m^{\prime\prime})
, where
m^{\prime\prime}=\frac{W_{j}}{\beta^{(k-1)^{2}}}
, such that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(H_{u}[P_{u}])=1\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k-2}
.
We show that there exists at least one vector
Y\in\mathsf{Product}_{k-1}(m^{\prime\prime})
, such that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(H_{u}[Y])=1\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
, for at least
2^{j}
vertices
u\in Q_{j}
.
We then show that the vector
Y
, which is a
(k-1)
-dimensional vector, can be extended to a
k
-dimensional vector
Z
for which we have
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(V[Z])=1\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
, which completes the proof.

We first explain why
Y
exists.
For every vector
P\in\mathsf{Product}_{k-1}(m^{\prime\prime})
, define a set of vertices
Q_{j,P}\subseteq Q_{j}
as follows.
A vertex
u\in Q_{j}
is in
Q_{j,P}
if
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(H_{u}[P])=1\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k-1}
.
Next, we use the pigeonhole principle, where vertices are pigeons, and vectors are holes, to claim that there exists at least one set
Q_{j,P}
that contains at least
2^{j}
vertices.
Note that the set of vectors
\mathsf{Product}_{k-1}(m^{\prime\prime})
has size at most
(\log n+2)^{k-1}\leq(\log 4n)^{k-1}=\gamma
, while the set
Q_{j}
has at least
2^{j}\gamma
vertices.
Therefore, there exists a vector
Y\in\mathsf{Product}_{k-1}(m^{\prime\prime})
such that
\left\lvert Q_{j,Y}\right\rvert\geq 2^{j}
.

To complete the proof, we extend the vector
Y
that belongs to
\mathsf{Product}_{k-1}(m^{\prime\prime})
into a vector
Z\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(m^{\prime})
as follows.
If
Y=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k-1})
, then
Z=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k-1},p_{k})
, where
p_{k}=2^{-j}
.
To see that
Z\in\mathsf{Product}_{k}(m^{\prime})
, note that

\displaystyle w(Z)=w(Y)/2^{j}\leq\frac{1}{2^{j}\cdot m^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{\beta^{(k-1)^{2}}}{2^{j}\cdot W_{j}}=\frac{\beta^{(k-1)^{2}}}{B}=\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\;.

Next, we prove that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(V[Z])=1\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k}
.
We define a random subset of vertices
R\subseteq V\setminus V_{k}
, obtained by sampling each vertex
v\in V_{i}
with probability
p_{i}
, for every
i\in[k-1]
, where
p_{i}
is the
i
-th coordinate of
Y
.
We claim that for every vertex
u\in Q_{j,Y}
, we have that
R
contains an edge from
H_{u}
with probability at least
(1-1/e)^{k-1}
, this is because the event that
R
contains an edge from
H_{u}
is exactly the event that
\mathcal{X}(H_{u}[Y])=1
.
Next, we define
R_{k}
to be a random subset of vertices obtained by sampling each vertex
v\in V_{k}
with probability
p_{k}=2^{-j}
.
Finally, we define the following events. For every nonempty set
S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}
we define the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)
to be the event that
\left\{R_{k}=S\right\}
, and the event
\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)
to be the event that
R
contains at least one edge from
H_{u}
, for at least one vertex
u\in S
.

Next, we show that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(V[Z])=1\right]\geq\sum_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k-1}\;.

(11)

For every fixed nonempty set
S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}
, the events
\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)
are independent, since
\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)
addresses sampling vertices from
V_{k}
, while
\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)
addresses sampling vertices from
V-V_{k}
, where the two samples are independent of each other.
Also note that
\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)
is contained in the event that
\{\mathcal{X}(V[Z])=1\}
, and since the events
\left\{\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\right\}_{S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}}
are pairwise disjoint, so are the events
\left\{\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right\}_{S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}}
.
Therefore, the event that
\{\mathcal{X}(V[Z])=1\}
, contains the union of the following pairwise disjoint events
\{\bigcup_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}}\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\}
.
We get

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{X}(V[Z]=1)\right]

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\bigcup_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}}\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\right]{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]\;.

To complete the proof, we need to show that

1.

\sum_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\right]\geq 1-1/e

2.

for every
S\subseteq Q_{j,Y}
, which is not empty, we have
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k-2}
.

The first claim follows as

\displaystyle\sum_{S:\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq Q_{j}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{1}(S)\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Q_{j}[2^{-j}]\neq\emptyset\right]=1-(1-2^{-j})^{\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert}\geq 1-1/e\;,

where the inequalities hold for the following reasons. The first two equalities follows from definition, and the last inequality follows from the assumption that
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\geq 2^{j}
.

For the second claim, note that we previously explained why for every
u\in Q_{j,Y}
, we have that
R
contains at least one edge from
H_{u}
with probability at least
(1-1/e)^{k-2}
.
Therefore, since
S
is a non-empty set that is contained in
Q_{j,Y}
,
it contains at least one vertex
u
for which
R
contains an edge from
H_{u}
with probability at least
(1-1/e)^{k-2}
, which means that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{2}(S)\right]\geq(1-1/e)^{k-2}
.
This completes the proof of
Equation

11
.

Proving that either
Q_{0}
is not empty, or
there exists
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
such that
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\geq 2^{j}\cdot\gamma
.

Assume towards a contradiction that this is not the case, that is, that
\left\lvert\mathcal{E}\right\rvert=m=m^{\prime}\cdot\beta^{k^{2}}
and that
Q_{0}
is empty, and for every
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
, we have
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert<2^{j}\cdot\gamma
.
Then,

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in[V_{h}]}d_{u}

\displaystyle<\sum_{u\in[V_{h}]}\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{0}}\chi_{\left\{u\in Q_{j}\right\}}\cdot 2W_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{0}}\sum_{u\in[V_{2}]}\chi_{\left\{u\in Q_{j}\right\}}\cdot 2W_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{0}}\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\cdot 2W_{j}

Because we assumed towards a contradiction that for every
j\in\left\{1,2,\ldots,K_{0}\right\}
we have
\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert<2^{j}\gamma
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{0}}\left\lvert Q_{j}\right\rvert\cdot 2W_{j}<\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{0}}2^{j}\gamma\cdot\frac{2B}{2^{j}}=K_{0}\cdot\gamma\cdot 2B

We have that

1.

K_{0}\leq\log(4n)=\beta/k

2.

\gamma=(\log(4n))^{k-1}\leq(\beta/k)^{k-1}

3.

B=m^{\prime}\cdot\beta^{(k-1)^{2}}

Therefore, we get that

\displaystyle K_{0}\cdot\gamma\cdot 2B\leq(1/k)^{k}\cdot\beta^{k+(k-1)^{2}}\cdot m^{\prime}=(1/k)^{k}\cdot\beta^{k^{2}-k+1}\cdot m^{\prime}<m^{\prime}\cdot\beta^{k^{2}-1}<m\;,

which is a contradiction.

We have thus proved the assumption and this completes the proof of the induction step and therefore of
Lemma

28

7
Applications to specific problems via hyperedge oracles

7.1
k
-Cliques

In this subsection, we explain how to approximate the number
m
of
k
-cliques in a graph
H=(V_{H},E_{H})
.

First, we will employ a standard reduction from
k
-clique to
k
-clique in a
k
-partite graph:

Proposition 29
.

Given a graph
H=(V_{H},E_{H})
with
|V|=n
, one can create in
O(n^{2})
time a
k
-partite graph
H^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E_{H^{\prime}})
such that the number of cliques in
G
is exactly
k!
times the number of cliques in
H
.

The proof of the proposition is folklore: We set
V^{\prime}
to be
V_{1}\cup V_{2}\cup\ldots\cup V_{k}
where each
V_{i}
is an independent set of size
n
and every vertex
v\in V_{H}
has a copy
v_{i}
in
V_{i}
for each
i\in[k]
. For every edge
(u,v)\in E_{H}
we add an edge
(u_{i},v_{j})
to
E_{H^{\prime}}
for every
i\neq j
,
i,j\in[k]
. It is clear that every
k
-clique in
H^{\prime}
corresponds to a
k
-clique in
H
and that every
k
-clique in
H
has
k!
copies in
H^{\prime}
, one for every permutation of its vertices.

From now on we assume that our given graph is
k
-partite.
The main result of this section is the following theorem.

Theorem 30

(
k
-clique)
.

Let
G
be a given graph with
n
vertices and let
k\geq 3
be a fixed integer.
There is a randomized algorithm that outputs an approximation
\hat{m}
for the number
m
of
k
-cliques set in
G
such that

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[(1-\varepsilon)m\leq\hat{m}\leq(1+\varepsilon)m\right]\geq 1-1/n^{2}
, for any constant
\varepsilon>0
. The running time is bounded by
\tilde{O}(f_{\mathrm{clique}}(n^{k}/m))
.

The function
f_{\mathrm{clique}}(M)
is defined in
Equation

12
below.
We also get the following explicit upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{clique}}(M)
.

Corollary 31
.

Let
G
be a graph with
n
vertices and
n^{t}

k
-cliques.
Let
r=k-t
.
If
r\geq 2
, then,

f_{\mathrm{clique}}(n^{r})\leq n^{2}+\max\left\{n^{\frac{\omega(r-1,r-1,r+2)}{3}},n^{\frac{\omega(r-2,r+1,r+1)}{3}}\right\}\leq n^{2}+n^{\frac{\omega(r-1,r,r+2)}{3}}\;.

Combining
Corollary

31
with
Theorem

30
we obtain:

See
3

We also get a better bound for
k=4
.
See
4

To prove
Theorem

30
we consider the
k
-clique approximate counting in the
k
-partite graph
H^{\prime}
as a hyperedge approximate counting problem on the implicit
k
-partite hypergraph
G
defined on the same vertex set as
H^{\prime}
and such that a
k
-tuple of vertices is a hyperedge in
G
if and only if they are a
k
-clique in
H^{\prime}
. We provide a
Hyperedge-Oracle
for
G
and
analyze its running time.
Let
f_{\mathrm{clique}}(M)
denote the running time of our
Hyperedge-Oracle
on a set
U\subseteq V(G)
with
\mu(U)\leq M
. We provide an upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{clique}}(M)
and
then we use
Theorem

2
together with our detection oracle to get a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m
, the number of
k
-cliques in
H^{\prime}
(which in turn gives us a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
k!m
as well, the number of
k
-cliques in the original graph
H
).

We also show that there’s a one to one correspondence between detection oracles for
k
-clique and
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation algorithms:
We show that if there exists an algorithm that computes a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for the number of
k
-cliques
m
in a
k
-partite
G
in time
T
, assuming that
m\geq\tau
for some value
\tau
, then there exists a detection oracle for
k
-cliques on sets
U
with
\mu(U)\leq\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/\tau)
operating in time
\tilde{O}(T)
.

Oracle Implementation.

Let
H^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E_{H^{\prime}})
be a
k
-partite graph with vertex set
V^{\prime}=V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V_{k}
, and let
E^{\prime}
denote the set of
k
-cliques in
H^{\prime}
, where every
k
-clique
e\in E^{\prime}
is a
k
-tuple of vertices,
e=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})
, such that
v_{i}\in V_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
.
This defines an implicit
k
-partite hypergraph
G=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
, as discussed before.

Given a set of vertices
U\subseteq V^{\prime}
, we explain how to implement the
Hyperedge-Oracle
on the hypergraph
G[U]
, i.e., determine if
G[U]
contains a hyperedge, or equivalently if
H^{\prime}[U]
contains a
k
-clique.
To do so, we modify a well-known reduction from
k
-clique detection to triangle detection by Nešetril and Poljak
[
NP85
]
. The original reduction from
k
-clique detection starting from an
n
-node graph
H
forms a new auxiliary graph whose vertices are those
k/3
-tuples of vertices of
H
that are also
k/3
cliques (if
k/3
is not an integer, there are three types of nodes:
\lceil k/3\rceil
,
\lfloor k\rfloor/3\rfloor
and
k-\lceil k/3\rceil-\lfloor k/3\rfloor
-cliques). Then two
k/3
-cliques (i.e. nodes in the auxiliary graph) are connected by an edge if and only if together they form a
2k/3
clique.

Our reduction is similar but with a twist. We start with a
k
-partite graph on parts
U_{1},\ldots,U_{k}
and instead of creating nodes corresponding to cliques of size
k/3
, we consider different sized cliques which have nodes from a pre-specified subset of the
U_{i}
s.

More formally, we create a new graph
F
as follows.
The vertex set
U
of the oracle input is partitioned into
k
sets
U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{k}
, where
U_{i}=V_{i}\cap U
for every
i\in[k]
.
We then partition the index set
[k]
into three sets,
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
, and define the vertex set of
F
as follows. Define

\displaystyle W_{1}=\bigtimes_{j\in I_{1}}U_{j}\;,

\displaystyle W_{2}=\bigtimes_{j\in I_{2}}U_{j}\;,

\displaystyle W_{3}=\bigtimes_{j\in I_{3}}U_{j}\;.

Note that
W_{i}
consists of
|I_{i}|
-tuples of vertices of
U
, one from each
U_{j}
with
j\in I_{i}
.

The vertex set of
F
is composed of three sets
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
, where
A_{i}\subseteq W_{i}
for
i\in[3]
defined as follows.
For every
i\in[3]
and every element
S=(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{|I_{i}|})\in W_{i}

, we add
S
to the set
A_{i}
if
H^{\prime}[S]
is a clique. So
A_{i}
consists of those
|I_{i}|
-tuples of vertices that are
|I_{i}|
-cliques and that have exactly one vertex from
U_{j}
with
j\in I_{i}
.

For any two vertices
S\in A_{i},P\in A_{j}
for
i\neq j
, let
V_{S,P}\subseteq V^{\prime}
denote a subset of vertices from
H^{\prime}
, which are in the vertex tuples
S
or
P
.
We add an edge
(S,P)
to
F
if
H^{\prime}[V_{S,P}]
is a clique.
We emphasize that if both
S
and
P
are in the same set
A_{i}
, then
H^{\prime}[V_{S,P}]
is not a clique,
and therefore the edge
(S,P)
is not in
F
, making
F
a tripartite graph. Also, since
A_{i}
and
A_{j}
are defined on disjoint subsets of
U
when
i\neq j
,
H^{\prime}[V_{S,P}]
is a clique if and only if it is a clique of size
|I_{i}|+|I_{j}|
. Thus, any triangle in
F
corresponds to a clique of size
|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|+|I_{3}|=k
.
As
H^{\prime}[U]
is
k
-partite, every
k
-clique also corresponds to a triangle in
F
, and thus the number of triangles in
F
equals the number of
k
-cliques in
H^{\prime}[U]
.

In what follows, we explain how to verify whether
F
contains a triangle or not. We do so using rectangular matrix multiplication on
F
as follows.
For
i,j\in[3]
, where
i\neq j
, we define a matrix
M_{i,j}
of size
\left\lvert A_{i}\right\rvert\times\left\lvert A_{j}\right\rvert
, where the entry
M_{i,j}[x,y]
is equal to
1
if the
x
-th vertex in
A_{i}
is connected to the
y
-th vertex in
A_{j}
in
F
, and otherwise
M_{i,j}[x,y]=0
.
We compute the product
D=M_{1,2}M_{2,3}M_{3,1}
and check whether the trace of
D
is non-zero.
The output of the
Hyperedge-Oracle
is
1
if the trace of
D
is non-zero, and
0
otherwise.

Running Time.

In the above description, we are free to pick the partition of the index set
[k]
nto
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
and we will do so as to minimize the running time.
There are at most
3^{k}
such partitions, so finding the best one takes
O(3^{k})
time.
The running time for a specific partition
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
is at most

\mathsf{MM}\left(\left\lvert A_{1}\right\rvert,\left\lvert A_{2}\right\rvert,\left\lvert A_{3}\right\rvert\right)
, plus the time to construct the matrices
M_{1,2},M_{2,3},M_{3,1}
, where both are upper bounded by
\mathsf{MM}\left(\left\lvert W_{1}\right\rvert,\left\lvert W_{2}\right\rvert,\left\lvert W_{3}\right\rvert\right)
.

For any positive value
M
, let
f_{\mathrm{clique}}(M)
denote the worst-case running time of the algorithm on a set
U
with
\mu(U)\leq M
.
We need the following notation to provide an upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{clique}}(M)
.
Let
P_{3}([k])
denote the set of all partitions of
[k]
into three sets.
For
P\in P_{3}([k])
, where
P=(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
, and a set
U=(U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{k})
, we define a triplet of numbers
x_{1}(P,U),x_{2}(P,U),x_{3}(P,U)
as follows.

\displaystyle x_{j}(P,U)=

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell\in I_{j}}\left\lvert U_{\ell}\right\rvert\;,\quad\text{for }\ell\in[3]\;.

We thus have the following upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{clique}}(M)
, where recall that
\mu(U)=\prod_{i=1}^{3}x_{i}(P,U)
:

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{clique}}(M)=\max_{U:\mu(U)\leq M}\min_{P\in P_{3}([k])}\left\{\mathsf{MM}\left(x_{1}(P,U),x_{2}(P,U),x_{3}(P,U)\right)\right\}\;.

(12)

Remark 1
.

For any non-negative
z
, the best we could hope for using this technique is

f_{\mathrm{clique}}(n^{z})=n^{\tfrac{\omega\cdot z}{3}}
.

Proof of
Corollary

31
.

To prove
Corollary

31
, we need to analyze
f_{\mathrm{clique}}(n^{r})
.
We need the following two properties.
First, that there exists a partition
P=(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
of
[k]
into three parts such that

\displaystyle x_{1}(P,U)\leq x_{2}(P,U)\leq x_{3}(P,U)\leq n\cdot x_{1}(P,U)\;.

(13)

Second, that for any such partition
P
that satisfies
Equation

13
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{MM}\left(x_{1}(P,U),x_{2}(P,U),x_{3}(P,U)\right)\leq\max\{n^{\omega(r-1,r-1,r+2)/3},n^{\omega(r-2,r+1,r+1)/3}\}\;,

(14)

where
n^{r}=\prod_{i\in[k]}\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert
, and we use
n
to denote the number of vertices in
V
.

Finally, we want to show that

\displaystyle\omega(r-1,r-1,r+2),\omega(r-2,r+1,r+1)\leq\omega(r-2,r,r+2)\;

(15)

To prove the property in
Equation

13
, we analyze the greedy algorithm that partitions
[k]
into three parts that are as “balanced” as possible.
We prove that there exists a partition
P
, that satisfies
Equation

13
.
Proving such a partition exists, is equivalent to showing that the set of numbers
R=\left\{r_{i}\right\}_{i\in[k]}
where
r_{i}=\log_{n}(n_{i})
,
with
\max(R)\leq 1
, can be partitioned into three sets
R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{r\in R_{1}}r\leq\sum_{r\in R_{2}}r\leq\sum_{r\in R_{3}}r\leq 1+\sum_{r\in R_{1}}r\;.

We prove this using a greedy strategy:
Start with a partition in which
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are empty, and
R_{3}=R
.
While there are two sets
R_{i},R_{j}
such that
\sum_{r\in R_{i}}r+1<\sum_{r\in R_{j}}r
, move the largest element from
R_{j}
to
R_{i}
.
Assuming the process stops, the three obtained sets satisfy the required conditions (up to renaming the sets).
We prove the process always stops by induction on the number of elements.
The base case, when there is a single element is trivial.
For the step, assume the process stops for
k-1
elements, and consider
k
elements. Take one element out, denoted by
r_{1}
, and apply the induction hypothesis. Assume that when the process stops, there are three sets
R_{1},R_{2}
and
R_{3}
which satisfy the required conditions.
We add the element
r_{1}
to the set that has the smallest sum, i.e.
R_{1}
.
We now use case analysis. Let
w(R)
denote
\sum_{r\in R}r
.

Case 1:

w(R_{1})+r_{1}\leq w(R_{2})
. Then the claim follows.

Case 2:

w(R_{1})+r_{1}\leq w(R_{3})
. Then the claim follows by renaming:
R_{1}^{\prime}\leftarrow R_{2}
and
R_{2}^{\prime}\leftarrow R_{1}+\left\{r_{1}\right\}
.

Case 3:

w(R_{1})+r_{1}>w(R_{3})
. Then the claim follows by renaming:
R_{1}^{\prime}\leftarrow R_{2}
,
R_{2}^{\prime}\leftarrow R_{3}
and
R_{3}\leftarrow R_{1}+\left\{r_{1}\right\}
. Note that

w(R_{1}^{\prime})+1\geq w(R_{1})+r_{1}=w(R_{3}^{\prime})
as required.

Next we prove property
14
.
We have to show that for every
P\in P_{3}([k])
,
that satisfies
Equation

13
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{MM}\left(x_{1}(P,U),x_{2}(P,U),x_{3}(P,U)\right)\leq\max\{n^{\omega(r-1,r-1,r+2)/3},n^{\omega(r-2,r+1,r+1)/3}\}\;.

For simplicity, we use
n^{a},n^{b},n^{c}
to denote
x_{1}(P,U),x_{2}(P,U),x_{3}(P,U)
, respectively, where without loss of generality we assume that
a\leq b\leq c\leq a+1
.

Recall that the number of
k
-cliques is
n^{t}
and
r=k-t
. As
\mu(U)=n^{a+b+c}\leq n^{r}
, we will assume that
a+b+c=r
as that will maximize
\omega(a,b,c)
and thus maximize
\mathsf{MM}\left(x_{1}(P,U),x_{2}(P,U),x_{3}(P,U)\right)=n^{\omega(a,b,c)}
.

Using the constraint
a+b+c=r
, we get that the set of feasible solutions for the constraints is a 2D plane in 3D space.
Each point on this plane can be written as a convex combination of three points obtained by setting
a=b=c
,
a=b\wedge c=a+1
, and
b=c=a+1
, which are

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{3}\cdot(r,r,r)\;,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{3}\cdot(r-2,r+1,r+1)\;,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{3}\cdot(r-1,r-1,r+2)\;.

In what follows we bound omega on a point
(x,y,z)
that is in the set of feasible solutions.
We claim that the maximum value of
\omega(x,y,z)
is obtained at either the point
(\tfrac{r-2}{3},\tfrac{r+1}{3},\tfrac{r+1}{3})
or
(\tfrac{r-1}{3},\tfrac{r-1}{3},\tfrac{r+2}{3})
.
This follows as the function
\omega
is convex in each of the variables
x,y,z
, and therefore the maximum value is obtained at one of the corners of the feasible set. We can ignore the point
(r/3,r/3,r/3)
as the function omega is smaller as the arguments are closer to each other, as shown in
Claim

32
below.
We get that the maximum value of
\omega(x,y,z)
is obtained at either
(\tfrac{r-2}{3},\tfrac{r+1}{3},\tfrac{r+1}{3})
or
(\tfrac{r-1}{3},\tfrac{r-1}{3},\tfrac{r+2}{3})
.

Finally, we would like to upper bound
\omega(r-1,r-1,r+2)
and
\omega(r-2,r+1,r+1)
by
\omega(r-2,r,r+2)
.

We use the following simple claim:

Claim 32
.

For every
y,x,z\geq 0
,
x>y
and every
\varepsilon\in[0,(x-y)]
,
\omega(x-\varepsilon,y+\varepsilon,z)\leq\omega(x,y,z)
.

Proof.
Notice that

x-\varepsilon=\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{x-y}\right)\cdot x+\frac{\varepsilon}{x-y}\cdot y\textrm{ and }y+\varepsilon=\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{x-y}\right)\cdot y+\frac{\varepsilon}{x-y}\cdot x.

By the convexity of
\omega(\cdot)
and because
\omega(x,y,z)=\omega(y,x,z)
we get

\omega(x-\varepsilon,y+\varepsilon,z)\leq\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{x-y}\right)\cdot\omega(x,y,z)+\frac{\varepsilon}{x-y}\cdot\omega(y,x,z)=\omega(x,y,z).

∎

Applying
Claim

32
with
x=r+2,y=r
and
\varepsilon=1
we get that
\omega(r-2,r+1,r+1)\leq\omega(r-2,r,r+2)
.

Applying
Claim

32
with
x=r,y=r-2
and
\varepsilon=1
we get that
\omega(r-1,r-1,r+2)\leq\omega(r-2,r,r+2)
.

This completes the proof of
Corollary

31
.

We are left with proving
Theorem

4
.

Proof of
Theorem

4
.
Fix
k=4
and
r=k-t
where
n^{t}
is the number of
k
-cliques we want to approximate.
We aim for an better upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{clique}}(n^{r})
for
r>2
since, as explained in the introduction, the interesting regime is when
t<k-2=2
.

When
r\leq 2
we just upper bound the running time by
\tilde{O_{\varepsilon}}(n^{2})
.

We identify every set
U
with
\mu(U)\leq n^{r}
with a four dimensional point
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})
where
a_{i}
is such that
n^{a_{i}}=\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert
for every
i\in[4]
.
We also assume without the loss of generality that
a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq a_{3}\leq a_{4}
(up to renaming the color classes), where we know that
a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+a_{4}=r
.
We analyze the running time of the oracle on a set
U
, under the partition
P=(I_{1},I_{2},I_{3})
, where
I_{1}=\{1,2\}
,
I_{2}=\{3\}
and
I_{3}=\{4\}
.
In other words, the two smallest set are “packed” together, and the two largest sets are separated.
Let
S
denote our universe where

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle\{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})\in[0,1]^{4}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{4}a_{i}=r\;\wedge a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq a_{3}\leq a_{4}\}\;.

\displaystyle S^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\left\{(a_{1}+a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})\mid(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})\in S\right\}\;.

Let
A=a_{1}+a_{2}
.
We need to upper bound the function
\omega(A,a_{3},a_{4})
for
(A,a_{3},a_{4})
under the following constraints:

1.

A+a_{3}+a_{4}=r
.

2.

A\geq 0
.

3.

A\leq 2a_{3}

4.

a_{3}\leq a_{4}
.

5.

a_{4}\leq 1
.

The linear inequalities on
A,a_{3},a_{4}
define a convex set and since
\omega
is convex function over the points
(A,a_{3},a_{4})
, its maxima are attained at corners.

The corners are:

•

(constraints 5 and 4 tight):
a_{4}=a_{3}=1,A=r-2
, giving
\omega(r-2,1,1)
.

•

(constraints 5 and 3 tight):
a_{4}=1,a_{3}=(r-1)/3,A=2(r-1)/3
, giving
\omega(2(r-1)/3,(r-1)/3,1)
.

•

(constraints 5 and 2 tight):
a_{4}=1,A=0,a_{3}=r-1
– not a corner since we assumed that
r>2
so
r-1>1
and
a_{3}\leq a_{4}\leq 1
is violated.

•

(constraints 4 and 3 tight):
a_{3}=a_{4}=A/2=r/4
, giving
\omega(r/2,r/4,r/4)
.

•

(constraints 4 and 2 tight):
a_{4}=a_{3}=r/2
– not feasible.

•

(constraints 3 and 2 tight):
a_{4}=r
– not feasible.

∎

From Fast Approximate Counting to a Fast Oracle.

For any positive value
\tau
the following is true.
We assume that there exists an algorithm
A
that takes as input any graph
F
and a threshold
\tau
, and outputs a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{F}
the number of
k
-cliques in
F
in time
T
, assuming that
m_{F}\geq\tau
.
We show that the existence of
A
implies the existence of a detection oracle for
k
-cliques which takes time
\tilde{O}(T)
on sets
U
with
\mu(U)\leq\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/\tau)
.
In other words, if our detection-oracle implementation for
k
-cliques is optimal, then so is our approximate
k
-clique counting algorithm, up to polylogarithmic factors.

Theorem 33
.

Assume there exists an algorithm
A
that takes as input any graph
F
and a threshold
\tau
.
If
m_{F}\geq\tau
the algorithm outputs a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{F}
in time
T
. If
m_{F}<\tau
the algorithm either outputs a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m_{F}
or output
\bot
.
Then, there exists a detection oracle for
k
-cliques which takes time
\tilde{O}(T)
on sets
U
with
\mu(U)\leq\tilde{O}(\mu(V)/\tau)
.

Proof of
Theorem

33
.
The input graph is
H=(V,E)
, and we would like to implement a detection oracle for
k
-cliques on sets
U\subseteq V
with
\mu(U)\leq\mu(V)/\tau
, using the algorithm
A
.
Let
H^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
be the
k
-partite graph obtained from the reduction.
We explain how to implement the detection oracle on
H^{\prime}
, which also implies the detection oracle on
H
.
Recall that
H^{\prime}
has the vertex set
V^{\prime}=V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V_{k}
and the edge set
E_{H^{\prime}}
, and that we denote the set of
k
-cliques in
H^{\prime}
by
E^{\prime}
.
Let
U\subseteq V^{\prime}
be such that
\mu(U)\leq\mu(V^{\prime})/\tau
.
Recall that
E^{\prime}_{U}
denotes the set of
k
-cliques in the induced graph
H^{\prime}[U]
.
We would like to determine if
E^{\prime}_{U}
is empty or not.
To answer the query “is
E^{\prime}_{U}
is empty” we build a new auxiliary graph
F
, and run the algorithm
A
on
F
.
If
A
outputs that
F
does not contain any
k
-cliques, then we output that
E^{\prime}_{U}
is empty, and if
A
outputs that
F
contains at least
\tau

k
-cliques, then we output that
E^{\prime}_{U}
is not empty.

We explain how to construct the graph
F
.
For every
i
let
U_{i}=U\cap V_{i}
. We define the vertex set
V^{F}
of
F
to be
V^{F}=V^{F}_{1}\sqcup V^{F}_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V^{F}_{k}
, where
V^{F}_{i}
is obtained by duplicating each vertex in
U_{i}
for
\left\lceil\left\lvert V_{i}\right\rvert/\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert\right\rceil
times.
For
i\in[k]
, let
s_{i}=\left\lceil\left\lvert V_{i}\right\rvert/\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert\right\rceil
, and for every vertex
v\in U_{i}
, let
\varphi_{v}:[s_{i}]\to V^{F}_{i}
be a function that maps any index
j\in[s_{i}]
to the
j
-th duplicate of
v
in
V^{F}_{i}
.
For every pair of vertices
x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in V^{F}
, where
x^{\prime\prime}
and
y^{\prime\prime}

are duplicates of two vertices
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in U
, we connect
x^{\prime\prime}
and
y^{\prime\prime}
in
F
if and only if
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
are connected in
H^{\prime}
.
This means that
F
is still a
k
-partite graph.
Constructing
F
takes time
O(\left\lvert V^{\prime}\right\rvert^{2})
.
Let
E^{\prime\prime}
denote the set of
k
-cliques in
F
.

We show that the following holds.

E^{\prime}_{U}
is empty if and only if
E^{\prime\prime}
is empty.
Moreover, if
E^{\prime}_{U}
is not empty, then
E^{\prime\prime}
contains at least
\tau
elements.
Clearly, if
F
contains a
k
-clique
C^{\prime}=(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{k})
, where
v_{i}\in V^{F}_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
, then the set of vertices
C
such that
v_{i}
is the original vertex in
U_{i}
that
v_{i}^{\prime}
was duplicated from, is a
k
-clique in
H^{\prime}
. This proves that if
E^{\prime}_{U}
is empty, then so is
E^{\prime\prime}
.
The exact same argument shows that if
E^{\prime}_{U}
contains a
k
-clique
C
, where
C=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})
,
then
C^{\prime}=(\varphi_{v_{1}}(i_{1}),\varphi_{v_{2}}(i_{2}),\ldots,\varphi_{v_{k}}(i_{k}))
is a
k
-clique in
F
, for every choice of
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}\in[s_{1}]\times[s_{2}]\times\ldots\times[s_{k}]
.
To complete the proof, we need to show that
\prod_{i\in[k]}s_{i}\geq\tau
.
We have

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in[k]}s_{i}=\prod_{i\in[k]}\left\lvert V_{i}\right\rvert/\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert=\mu(V^{\prime})/\mu(U)\;,

and because we assumed that
\mu(U)\leq\mu(V^{\prime})/\tau
, we have that
\prod_{i\in[k]}s_{i}\geq\tau
, which completes the proof.
∎

7.1.1
Conditional Hardness

Two popular hardness hypotheses in fine-grained complexity are that detecting a
k
-clique takes
n^{\omega(\lfloor k/3\rfloor,\lceil k/3\rceil,k-\lceil k/3\rceil-\lfloor k/3\rfloor)-o(1)}
time, and that any combinatorial algorithm for
k
-clique detection takes
n^{k-o(1)}
time
[
VW18
]
.
In
[
JX22
]
an equivalent variant of the combinatorial hypothesis is introduced:

Hypothesis 1

(Unbalanced Combinatorial
k
-Clique Hypothesis
[
JX22
, Hypothesis 2.1]
)
.

Let
G
be a
k
-partite graph with vertex set
V=V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V_{k}
, where
\left\lvert V_{i}\right\rvert=n^{a_{i}}
for every
i\in[k]
.
Let
s=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}
.
In the word-RAM model with
O(\log n)
bits words, any randomized algorithm for
k
-clique detection in
G
needs
n^{s-o(1)}
time.

A natural extension of
Hypothesis

1
to any algorithm instead of only combinatorial algorithms is the following.

Hypothesis 2
.

Let
G
be a
k
-partite graph with vertex set
V=V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V_{k}
, where
\left\lvert V_{i}\right\rvert=n^{a_{i}}
for every
i\in[k]
.
Let
s=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}
.
In the word-RAM model with
O(\log n)
bits words, any randomized algorithm for
k
-clique detection in
G
needs
n^{\frac{\omega\cdot s}{3}-o(1)}
time.

We show that assuming
Hypothesis

2
is true the running time of our algorithm is close to optimal.

Theorem 34
.

Assume
Hypothesis

2
and the Word-RAM model of computation with
O(\log n)
bit words. Let
k\geq 3
be any constant integer.
Any randomized algorithm that, when given an
n
node graph
G
, can
distinguish between
G
being
k
-clique-free and containing
\geq n^{t}
copies of
k
-cliques needs

n^{\omega(k-t)/3-o(1)}
time.

Proof.
We start with a
k
-partite graph
G=(V,E)
with vertex set
V=V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V_{k}
, where
\left\lvert V_{i}\right\rvert=n^{a_{i}}
for every
i\in[k]
. Let
s=\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}
.
We build a new graph
G^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
as follows.
For every
i\in[k]
, we duplicate every vertex in
V_{i}
for
{n^{1-a_{i}}}
times. For every vertex
v
, we denote its duplicates by
(v,1),(v,2),\ldots
.
For every edge
(u,v)\in E
, we add the edge
((u,i),(v,j))
to
E^{\prime}
between every copy of
u
and every copy of
v
.

We show that
G
has a
k
-clique if and only if
G^{\prime}
has
n^{t}

k
-cliques, where
t=k-s
.
For the first direction, note that every
k
-clique in
G
denoted by
C=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})
, where
v_{i}\in V_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
, corresponds to
n^{t}
copies of a
k
-clique in
G^{\prime}
, by replacing each
v_{i}
with one of its
n^{1-a_{i}}
duplicates.
Therefore, the number of
k
-cliques in
G^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in[k]}n^{1-a_{i}}=n^{k-s}=n^{t}\;.

For the other direction, we show that if
G^{\prime}
has a single
k
-clique
C^{\prime}=(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{k})
, then
G
also have a
k
-clique.
For every
i\in[k]
, let
v_{i}
be the vertex in
V_{i}
that
v^{\prime}_{i}
was duplicated from.
Then the set of vertices
C=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})
is a
k
-clique in
G
by definition. This completes the proof of the statements that
G
has a
k
-clique if and only if
G^{\prime}
has
n^{t}

k
-cliques, where
t=k-s
.

To complete the proof of the theorem, we assume towards a contradiction that there exists an algorithm that can distinguish between
G^{\prime}
being
k
-clique-free and containing
\geq n^{t}
copies of
k
-cliques in time
n^{\omega(k-t)/3-\delta}
, for some constant positive
\delta
.
Recall that we defined
t
to be
k-s
, and therefore

k-t=s
.
However, such an algorithm can be used to distinguish between
G
being
k
-clique-free and containing a
k
-clique in time
n^{\omega\cdot s/3-\delta}
, which contradicts
Hypothesis

2
.
∎

We conclude that assuming
Hypothesis

2
, the running time of our algorithm which is bounded by
O(n^{2}+n^{\frac{\omega(s-1,s,s+2)}{3}})

is close to optimal, since the best we could hope for is
O(n^{\frac{\omega\cdot s}{3}-o(1)})
.

7.2
k
-Dominating Sets

In this subsection, we explain how to approximate the number
m
of
k
-dominating sets (henceforth
k
-DS) in a graph
H=(V,E_{H})
.

First, we will employ a standard reduction from
k
-DS to
k
-DS in a
k
-partite graph:

Proposition 35
.

Given a graph
H=(V_{H},E_{H})
with
|V|=n
, one can create in
O(n^{2})
time a
k
-partite graph
H^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E_{H^{\prime}})
such that the number of DS in
G
is exactly
k!
times the number of DS in
H
.

From now on we assume that our given graph is
k
-partite.
The main result of this section is the following theorem.

Theorem 36

(
k
-dominating sets)
.

Let
G
be a given graph with
n
vertices and let
k\geq 3
be a fixed integer.
There is a randomized algorithm that outputs an approximation
\hat{m}
for the number
m
of
k
-dominating sets in
G
such that

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[(1-\varepsilon)m\leq\hat{m}\leq(1+\varepsilon)m\right]\geq 1-1/n^{2}
, for any constant
\varepsilon>0
. The running time is bounded by
\tilde{O}(f_{\mathrm{DS}}(n^{k}/m))
.

The function
f_{\mathrm{DS}}(M)
is defined in
Equation

16
below.
We also get the following explicit upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{ds}}(M)
.

Corollary 37
.

For every
r\geq 2
, we have that

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{ds}}(n^{r})\leq n^{\omega(1,(r-1)/2,(r+1)/2)}\;.

Combining
Corollary

37
with
Theorem

36
we obtain:
See
5

Proof of
Proposition

35
.
We start with a graph
H=(V,E_{H})
with
n
vertices, and define a new graph
H^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E_{H^{\prime}})
.
We count the number of
special

k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
, which we shortly define.
The key point here is that unlike the previous reduction, we don’t need the number of
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
to be equal to the number of
k
-DS in
H
times a fixed factor. Instead, we can count a subset of the
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
.

The vertex set of
H^{\prime}
is
V^{\prime}=V\times[k]
, and the edge set
E_{H^{\prime}}
is defined as follows. For every edge
(u,v)\in E_{H}
,
we add the edge
((u,i),(v,j))
to
E_{H^{\prime}}
for every
i,j\in[k]
, where unlike the previous reduction, we allow for
i=j
.
We define two functions
\varphi:V\to[k]
and
\chi:V\to A
as follows. For every vertex
v^{\prime}=(v,i)\in V^{\prime}
, we set
\varphi(v^{\prime})=i
and
\chi(v^{\prime})=v
.
We say that a set
e\subseteq V^{\prime}
is
colorful
if
\varphi
is injective on
e
, and say that
e
is
distinct
if
\chi
is injective on
e
.
We say that
e
is
special
if
e
is both colorful and distinct.

Let
E
denote the set of
k
-DS in
H
and let
m
denote its size.
In addition, let
E^{\prime}
denote the set of
special

k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
and let
m^{\prime}
denote its size.
We encode every
k
-DS in
H
as a
k
-tuple of vertices,
e=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})
, ordered lexicographically.

Let
\Pi_{k}
denote the set of all permutations of
[k]
.
For every
k
-DS
C=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})
in
H
, where the vertices in
C
are ordered lexicographically, and every permutation
\pi\in\Pi_{k}
, we use the notation
C_{\pi}=((v_{1},\pi(1)),(v_{2},\pi(2)),\ldots,(v_{k},\pi(k)))
.

We show the following holds.

C\in E
if and only if there exists a permutation
\pi\in\Pi_{k}
such that
C_{\pi}\in E^{\prime}
.

To see the first direction, let
C
be a
k
-DS in
H
.
We show that for every
\pi\in\Pi_{k}
, the set
C_{\pi}
is a special
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
.
Clearly,
C_{\pi}
is colorful as
\varphi
is injective on
C_{\pi}
, and
C_{\pi}
is distinct as
\chi
is injective on
C_{\pi}
, as
\chi(C_{\pi})=C
, because
C
contains
k
distinct vertices.
We are left with proving that
C_{\pi}
is a
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
.
We fix some vertex
u^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
and show it is in
C_{\pi}
or has a neighbor in
C_{\pi}
. If it is in
C_{\pi}
then the proof is complete, and we therefore assume that
u^{\prime}\notin C_{\pi}
.
We use
u^{\prime}=(u,i)
, i.e.,
\chi(u^{\prime})=u
and
\varphi(u^{\prime})=i
.
Since
C
is a
k
-DS in
H
, there exists a vertex
v\in C
which is a neighbor of
u
.
Therefore, there must be a vertex
v^{\prime}\in C_{\pi}
for which
\chi(v^{\prime})=v
, which means that
v^{\prime}
is a neighbor of
u^{\prime}
in
H^{\prime}
, which proves that
C_{\pi}
is a
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
.

To see the other direction, let
C_{\pi}
be a special
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
.
We prove that
C
is a
k
-DS in
H
.
Note that
C=\{\chi(v^{\prime})\mid v^{\prime}\in C_{\pi}\}
. Since
C_{\pi}
is distinct, the set
C
contains exactly
k
(distinct) vertices.
We are left with proving that
C
is a
k
-DS in
H
.
We fix some vertex
u\in V
and show that it is in
C
or has a neighbor in
C
. If it is in
C
then the proof is complete, and we therefore assume that
u\notin C
.
We look at the vertex
(u,1)
in
H^{\prime}
, which is either in
C_{\pi}
or has a neighbor in
C_{\pi}
. If
(u,1)\in C_{\pi}
then
u\in C
, so we assume that
(u,1)\notin C_{\pi}
.
Since
C_{\pi}
is dominating, there exists a vertex
v^{\prime}\in C_{\pi}
which is a neighbor of
(u,1)
.
Let
v=\chi(v^{\prime})
, and note that
v\in C
and
v
is a neighbor of
u
in
H
, which proves that
C
is dominating.

∎

This completes the reduction from a general graph
H
to a new graph
H^{\prime}
. We have that if
\hat{m}
is a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for the number of special
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
, then
\hat{m}/(k!)
is a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for the number of
k
-DS in
H
, as required.

Oracle Implementation.

We explain how to implement the
Hyperedge-Oracle
on a
k
-partite hypergraph
G
obtained from
H^{\prime}
by considering every special
k
-DS as a hyperedge.
That is we provide an algorithm that takes as input a set
U\subseteq V^{\prime}
, and determines if
H^{\prime}[U]
contains a special
k
-DS. The oracle has access to
H
,
H^{\prime}
and the two functions
\varphi
and
\chi
, and can evaluate them on any vertex in
H^{\prime}
in
O(1)
time.

To do so, we use a well-known reduction from the
k
-DS problem to matrix multiplication, shown in
[
EG04
]
.
In other words, we first find all
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
and we then filter the non-special ones from this set.

The vertex set
U
is partitioned in
k
sets
U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{k}
, where
U_{i}=V_{i}\cap U
for every
i\in[k]
, where
V_{i}=V\times\left\{i\right\}
.
We partition the index set
[k]
into two sets,
I_{1},I_{2}
, and build a new graph
F
as follows. We define two supersets
W_{1},W_{2}
as follows.

\displaystyle W_{1}=\bigtimes_{j\in I_{1}}U_{j}\;,

\displaystyle W_{2}=\bigtimes_{j\in I_{2}}U_{j}\;.

We say that an element
S\in W_{i}
is special if it is colorful and distinct.
Let
A_{i}
denote the set of all special elements in
W_{i}
for every
i\in[2]
.
Note that every element
S\in W_{i}
is a subset of
\left\lvert I_{i}\right\rvert
vertices, which is also colorful. However,
S
might not be distinct – the function
\chi
might not be injective on
S
.
We keep in
A_{i}
only the distinct elements in
W_{i}
.
For
i\in[2]
we define a new matrix
M_{i}
of size
\left\lvert A_{i}\right\rvert\times\left\lvert V\right\rvert
, where the entry
M_{i}[x,y]
is equal to
0
if the
y
-th vertex in
V
has a neighbor in the
x
-th element in
A_{i}
, and otherwise it is equal to
1
.
Let
(M_{2})^{T}
denote the transpose of
M_{2}
.
We then compute the product
D=M_{1}(M_{2})^{T}
.
The following holds:

\displaystyle D[i,j]=\sum_{h=1}^{n}M_{1}[i,h]\cdot(M_{2})^{T}[h,j]\;,

and therefore
D[i,j]=0
if and only if for every
h\in[n]
, the
h
-th vertex in
V
is connected to the
i
-th element in
A_{1}
or to the
j
-th element in
A_{2}
.
For every zero entry
D[i,j]=0
, the union of the
i
-th element in
A_{1}
and the
j
-th element in
A_{2}
is a colorful
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
, however, it might not be distinct. In what follows we explain how to filter the non-distinct sets.

Fix
i
and
j
, and let
S
denote the
i
-element in
A_{1}
, and
S^{\prime}
denote the
j
-element in
A_{2}
.
Then
S\cup S^{\prime}
is a colorful
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
if and only if
D[i,j]=0
.
Testing whether
S\cup S^{\prime}
is distinct takes
O(1)
time, as we can evaluate
\chi
on
S\cup S^{\prime}
in
O(1)
time, and
S\cup S^{\prime}
is distinct if and only if
\chi
is injective on
S\cup S^{\prime}
.
Therefore, The number of special
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
is equal to the number of zero entries in
D
, which are associated with a distinct set of vertices.
Finding all such sets take at most
O(\left\lvert A_{1}\right\rvert\cdot\left\lvert A_{2}\right\rvert)
time.
Therefore, the output of the
Hyperedge-Oracle
is
1
if we find a zero entry in
D
, which is associated with a special
k
-DS in
H^{\prime}
, and it is
0
otherwise.

Running Time.

The running time of the oracle is
\mathsf{MM}\left(n,\left\lvert A_{1}\right\rvert,\left\lvert A_{2}\right\rvert\right)
, which is bounded by
\mathsf{MM}\left(n,\left\lvert W_{1}\right\rvert,\left\lvert W_{2}\right\rvert\right)
.
We choose the partition of the index set
[k]
into
I_{1},I_{2}
that minimizes the running time of the oracle.

For any positive value
M
, let
f_{\mathrm{DS}}(M)
denote the worst-case running time of the oracle on a set
U
with
\mu(U)\leq M
.
We need the following notation to provide an upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{DS}}(M)
.
Let
P_{2}([k])
denote the set of all partitions of
[k]
into two sets.
For
P\in P_{2}([k])
, where
P=(I_{1},I_{2})
, and a set
U=(U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{k})
, we define a pair of numbers
x_{1}(P,U),x_{2}(P,U)
as follows.

\displaystyle x_{1}(P,U)=

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I_{1}}\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert\;,

\displaystyle x_{2}(P,U)=
