\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I_{2}}\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert\;.

We have the following upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{DS}}(M)
:

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{DS}}(M)=\max_{U:\mu(U)\leq M}\min_{P\in P_{2}([k])}\left\{\mathsf{MM}\left(x_{1}(P,U),x_{1}(P,U),n\right)\right\}\;.

(16)

This concludes the proof of
Theorem

36
.
In what follows we prove
Corollary

37
.

For every partition
P\in P_{2}([k])
, where
P=(I_{1},I_{2})
let
W_{i}=\bigtimes_{j\in I_{i}}\left\lvert U_{j}\right\rvert
for every
i\in[2]
, and define
X_{i}=\left\lvert W_{i}\right\rvert
.
We therefore can always find a partition
I_{1},I_{2}
such that
X_{1}\leq X_{2}\leq n\cdot X_{1}
, using a greedy algorithm.
Because
X_{1}\cdot X_{2}=M
and
X_{2}\leq X_{1}\cdot n
there exists
s\in[0,1/2]
such that
X_{1}=\sqrt{M}\cdot n^{s-1/2}
and
X_{2}=\sqrt{M}\cdot n^{1/2-s}
.
We therefore have

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{DS}}(M)\leq\mathsf{MM}\left(n,\sqrt{M/n}\cdot n^{s},\sqrt{M\cdot n}/n^{s}\right)\leq\mathsf{MM}\left(n,\sqrt{M/n},\sqrt{Mn}\right)\;,

and to complete the proof of
Corollary

37
, we plug in
M=n^{r}
.

Remark 2
.

The best we can hope for is
f_{\mathrm{DS}}(M)=\mathsf{MM}\left(n,\sqrt{M},\sqrt{M}\right)
.

We are left with proving
Theorem

6

Proof.
We prove that for
k=3
it holds that

f_{\mathrm{DS}}(n^{3-t})\leq n+n^{\psi}
, where

\displaystyle\psi=\max\left\{\omega(1,1-t/3,2(t-1)/3),\omega(1,1,2-t)\right\}\leq\omega(1,1,2(1-t/3))\;.

We are given a set
U\subseteq V
where
U
is partitioned into
3
sets
U_{1},U_{2},U_{3}
, where
U_{i}=V_{i}\cap U
for every
i\in[3]
.
We assume that
\mu(U)=n^{3-t}
, for some
t\leq 2
.
We use
n^{s_{i}}
to denote
\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert
for every
i\in[3]
, where we assume without the loss of generality that
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3}
,
as well as that
0\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3}\leq 1
, and that
s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}=3-t
.

To prove the theorem, we partition the index set
[3]
into two sets
I_{1},I_{2}
, by choosing
I_{1}=\left\{1,2\right\}
and
I_{2}=\left\{3\right\}
.
This induces two vertex sets
W_{1},W_{2}
, where
W_{1}=U_{1}\times U_{2}
and
W_{2}=U_{3}
.
We have that
\left\lvert W_{1}\right\rvert=n^{s_{3}}
, and
\left\lvert W_{2}\right\rvert=n^{s_{1}+s_{2}}
, and therefore
f_{\mathrm{DS}}(n^{3-t})\leq\mathsf{MM}\left(n,n^{s_{3}},n^{s_{1}+s_{2}}\right)
.
We can write
s_{1}+s_{2}=3-t-s_{3}
, and therefore

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{DS}}(n^{3-t})\leq n^{\omega(1,s_{3},3-t-s_{3})}\;.

Note that due to the convexity of omega, we have that
the maximum of
\omega(1,s_{3},3-t-s_{3})
is achieved at the endpoints, which are when
s_{3}
is maximal, i.e.,
s_{3}=1
, or when
s_{3}
is minimal, i.e.,
s_{3}=(3-t)/3=1-t/3
, which completes the proof.
∎

7.3
k
-Sum

In this subsection, we explain how to approximate the number
m
of
k
-sums in an array
A
of
n
numbers, where a
k
-sum is a
k
-tuple of elements in
A
that sum to
0
.

First, we will employ a standard reduction from
k
-sum to colorful
k
-sum, which is defined as follows.
Let
A=A_{1},A_{2}\ldots A_{k}
be a set of
k
-arrays of integers and let
k\geq 3
be a fixed integer.
Compute a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for the number of colorful
k
-sums,
where a
k
-tuple
s=(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k})
is considered as a colorful
k
-sum if
a_{i}\in A_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}=0
.

For that, we prove the following proposition.

Proposition 38
.

Given an array
A
of
n
numbers, one can create in
O(n)
time
k
arrays
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{k}
such that the number of
k
-sums in
A
is exactly
k!
times the number of (colorful)
k
-sums in
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{k}
.

Proof.
We start with an instance
A
of the
k
-Sum problem, which has
m

k
-sums, and get a new instance
A^{\prime}
of the Colorful-
k
-Sum problem, which has
m^{\prime}
colorful
k
-sums, where
m^{\prime}=(k!)m
. The reduction is oblivious to the
m
.

Let
A
be an array of
n
integers, and let
k\geq 3
be a fixed integer.
Assume that all elements in
A
fall inside the interval
[-U,U]
, where
U
is a positive number.
We build a new instance
A^{\prime}
of the Colorful-
k
-Sum problem as follows.
We define a new array
A_{i}=A+3^{i}\cdot 10U
for every
i\in[k-1]
, and let
A_{k}=A-U^{\prime}
, where
U^{\prime}=10U\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}3^{i}
.
This completes the reduction.

For any solution
s=(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k})
to the
k
-Sum problem, where
a_{i}\in A
for every
i\in[k]
, and every permutation
\pi\in\Pi_{k}
,
we have that
s^{\prime}=(a_{\pi(1)}+3^{1}\cdot 10U,a_{\pi(2)}+3^{2}\cdot 10U,\ldots,a_{\pi(k)}-10U^{\prime})
is a solution to the Colorful-
k
-Sum problem.
Note that the offsets we added to each
A_{i}
ensures that
for every
s\subseteq A^{\prime}
that satisfies
\sum_{a\in s}a_{i}=0
, means that
s
is colorful.
∎

From now on we assume that our given input contains k-arrays. The main result of this section is the following theorem.

See
7

The function
f_{\mathrm{clique}}(M)
is defined in
Equation

17
below.
We also get the following explicit upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{sum}}(M)
.

Corollary 39
.

Let
A
be an array of
n
integers, and let
k\geq 3
be a fixed integer.
Assume that
A
contains
n^{t}
solutions for the
k
-sum problem.
Let
r=k-t
.
If
r\geq 2
, then,

f_{\mathrm{sum}}(n^{r})\leq\tilde{O}(n^{(r+1)/2})\;.

Our final result in this section is the following theorem.
See
8

To prove
Theorem

7
we consider the colorful
k
-sum approximate counting over the arrays
A^{\prime}=(A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{k})
as a hyperedge approximate counting problem on the implicit
k
-partite hypergraph
G
, whose vertices are the elements of
A^{\prime}
and the hyperedges are exactly the set of colorful
k
-sums. We provide a
Hyperedge-Oracle
for
G
and analyze its running time.
Let
f_{\mathrm{sum}}(M)
denote the running time of our
Hyperedge-Oracle
on a set
U\subseteq V(G)
with
\mu(U)\leq M
. We provide an upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{sum}}(M)
and then we use
Theorem

2
together with our detection oracle to get a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
m
, the number of colorful
k
-sum in
H^{\prime}
(which in turn gives us a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
k!m
as well, the number of
k
-sum in the original array
A
).

Oracle Implementation.

Let
V
be a set of
k
-arrays
V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{k}
, and let
E
denote the set of colorful
k
-tuple of numbers,
e=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})
, such that
v_{i}\in V_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
, for which
\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}=0
.
Then
G=(V,E)
be a
k
-partite hypergraph.

Given a set of vertices
U\subseteq V
, we explain how to implement a query on
U
over the hypergraph
G[U]
, i.e., determine if
G[U]
contains a hyperedge, or equivalently if
U
contains a
k
-tuple from
E
.
The vertex set
U
is partitioned in
k
pairwise disjoint sets
U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{k}
, where
U_{i}=V_{i}\cap U
for every
i\in[k]
.

To do so, we modify a well-known algorithm for the
k
-sum problem, which
first builds two sets
W_{1},W_{2}
as follows, by having
W_{1}=\bigtimes_{i\in[k/2]}U_{i}
and
W_{2}=\bigtimes_{i\in\{k/2+1,\ldots,k\}}U_{i}
, and for every element
s\in W_{1}\cup W_{2}
computing
\sum_{a\in s}a
.
Let
R_{1}=\{\sum_{a\in s}a\mid s\in W_{1}\}
and
R_{2}=\{\sum_{a\in s}a\mid s\in W_{2}\}
.
Assume without the loss of generality that
\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert
.
The algorithm proceeds by iterating over all elements in
R_{1}
, and for every
x\in R_{1}
checking if
-x\in R_{2}
.
If such elements exist, the algorithm outputs
1
, and
0
otherwise.
The running time of this algorithm is
O(\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert\log(\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert)+\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert\log(\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert))
.

Our reduction is similar but with a twist. We start with a
k
-partite graph on parts
U_{1},\ldots,U_{k}
and instead of creating nodes corresponding to elements of size
k/2
, we consider different sized tuples which have numbers from a pre-specified subset of the
U_{i}
s.

More formally, we create two new sets
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
as follows.
We partition the index set
[k]
into two sets,
I_{1},I_{2}
, and define the
two sets

\displaystyle W_{1}=\bigtimes_{j\in I_{1}}U_{j}\;,

\displaystyle W_{2}=\bigtimes_{j\in I_{2}}U_{j}\;.

Note that
W_{i}
consists of
|I_{i}|
-tuples of elements from
U
, one from each
U_{j}
with
j\in I_{i}
.
We then define two sets
R_{1},R_{2}
as before:

\displaystyle R_{j}=\{\sum_{a\in s}a\mid s\in W_{j}\}\;,\quad j\in\left\{1,2\right\}\;.

From this point forward our reduction is the same as the standard one.
It involves sorting the sets
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
, and then checking if
for every
x\in R_{1}
there exists an element
-x\in R_{2}
.

Running Time Analysis.

We choose the partition of the index set
[k]
into
I_{1},I_{2}
that minimizes the running time of the algorithm.
Let
f_{\mathrm{sum}}(M)
denote the running time of the algorithm for a set
U
with
\mu(U)\leq M
. We provide an upper bound on
f_{\mathrm{sum}}(M)
.
Let
\mathcal{U}
denote the collection of sets
U
with
\mu(U)\leq M
for some value
M
. Then the running time of the algorithm is

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{sum}}(M)=\max_{U:\mu(U)\leq M}\min_{P\in P_{2}([k])}\left\{x_{1}(P,U),x_{2}(P,U)\right\}\;,

(17)

Where recall that
P_{2}([k])
is the set of all partitions of
[k]
into two sets.
This completes the proof of
Theorem

7
.

In the following, we provide an explicit bound on
f_{\mathrm{sum}}(M)
, which proves
Corollary

39
.
We can provide an explicit bound on
f_{\mathrm{sum}}(M)
, using the fact that
\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert\leq n
for every
i\in[k]
.
We therefore can always find a partition
I_{1},I_{2}
where
\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert\cdot n
.
This follows by a greedy approach that starts with a trivial partition where all elements are in
I_{2}
and while
\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert
, picks an index from
I_{2}
and moves it to
I_{1}
.
By the previous discussion, we can assume that there exists a partition
I_{1},I_{2}
that induces two sets
R_{1},R_{2}
such that
\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert\cdot\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert=M
and
\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert\cdot n
, where
n=\left\lvert V\right\rvert
.
We can also write
\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert
and
\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert
as follows.
There exists
s\in[0,1/2]
such that
\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert=\sqrt{M}\cdot n^{s-1/2}
and
\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert=\sqrt{M}\cdot n^{1/2-s}
.
If
s=0
, then
\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert
and if
s=1/2
, then
\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert\cdot n
.
The running time of the algorithm is therefore at most
O(\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert\cdot\log(\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert))
which is at most
O(\sqrt{M}\cdot n^{1/2-s}\cdot\log(n))
.
By plugging in
M=n^{k}/m
we get that the running time of the algorithm is at most
\sqrt{n^{k+1}/m}
.

Refined Analysis for Small Values of
k
.

In what follows we prove
Theorem

8
.
See
8

Proof of
Theorem

8
..
We prove that
f_{\mathrm{sum}}(n^{3-t})\leq n+n^{2(1-t/3)}
.
In other words, we prove that given three arrays
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
, where

A_{i}
has cardinality
n^{s}_{i}
for every
i\in[3]
, and
s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}=3-t
,
we can compute the number of colorful
3
-sums in
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
in time
O(n+n^{2(1-t/3)})
.
We assume without the loss of generality that
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3}
, and consider the partition
I_{1}=\{1,2\},I_{2}=\{3\}
.
This partition induces two sets
W_{1}=A_{1}\times A_{2},W_{2}=A_{3}
, and two sets
R_{1},R_{2}
, where

\displaystyle R_{1}=\{\sum_{a\in s}a\mid s\in W_{1}\}\;,

\displaystyle R_{2}=\{\sum_{a\in s}a\mid s\in W_{2}\}\;.

Clearly,
\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert\leq n
.
Moreover, we have that
\left\lvert R_{1}\right\rvert\leq n^{s_{1}+s_{2}}
, and using the following facts

1.

s_{1}+s_{2}+s_{3}=3-t

2.

0\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{3}\leq 1

we get that the expression
s_{1}+s_{2}=3-t-s_{3}
is maximized when
s_{1}
is large as possible, and
s_{3}
is as small as possible, which occurs when
s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}=(3-t)/3=1-t/3
.
Therefore the running time of constructing and sorting
R_{1}
is at most
O(n^{2(1-t/3)}\log n)
, and the running time of iterating over every element
x
in
R_{2}
and checking if
-x\in R_{2}
takes at most
O(\left\lvert R_{2}\right\rvert)=O(n)
time. Overall, the running time is at most
O(n+n^{2(1-t/3)})
, which completes the proof of
Theorem

8
.
∎

7.4
General Problems

We characterize a class of problems for which the reduction from approximate counting to oracle detection implementation holds.
We think of a “problem”
\mathcal{P}
as a family of functions taking as input a
k
-partite graph
H=(V,E_{H})
, and a coloring
\varphi:V\to[k]
, where
V_{i}
denotes the set of vertices in
V
that are mapped to
i
by
\varphi
.
The “problem”
\mathcal{P}
outputs a subset
E\subseteq\bigtimes_{i\in[k]}V_{i}
.
We write
\mathcal{P}(H)=E
to denote that
E
is the output of
\mathcal{P}
on input
H
.
Note that
E\subset V^{k}
, and for every
e\in E
we have
\varphi(e)=[k]
.
We denote by
Duplicatable
the class of problems for which the reduction from approximate counting to oracle detection holds.
We explain which problems are in the class
Duplicatable
.
Fix some
k
-partite graph
H=(V,E_{H})
a
k
-coloring
\varphi
and let
E=\mathcal{P}(H)
.
Define a new graph
H^{\prime}
obtained from
H
by adding to it a new vertex
v^{\prime}_{i}
which is a duplicate of
v_{i}\in V_{i}
. That is, we extend
\varphi
such that
\varphi(v^{\prime}_{i})=\varphi(v_{i})
.
We also add every edge
(u,v^{\prime}_{i})
to the graph, where
u\in N(v_{i})
.
Let
E^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}(H^{\prime})
.
Then the problem
\mathcal{P}
is in the class
Duplicatable
if the following holds.
For every
e\in E
we have
e\in E^{\prime}
.
Moreover, for any
e\in E
, if
v_{i}\in e
and
e^{\prime}=(e\setminus\left\{v_{i}\right\})\cup\left\{v^{\prime}_{i}\right\}
then
e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.
In words, in every element
e\in E
we can replace every vertex
v_{i}
with its duplicate
v^{\prime}_{i}
and get a new element
e^{\prime}
that is also in
E^{\prime}
.

The main result of this subsection is the following.

Theorem 40
.

Fix a problem
\mathcal{P}
taken from the class
Duplicatable
. Let
H=(V,E_{H})
be any graph, and let
\varphi
be a
k
-coloring of
V
.
If there exists an algorithm that can distinguish whether
\mathcal{P}(H)=E
is empty or contains at least
\tau
elements, in time
T
, then there exists an implementation of the detection oracle in time
\tilde{O}(T)
over sets
U
with
\mu(U)\leq\mu(V)/\tau
.

Corollary 41
.

For every problem
\mathcal{P}
in the class
Duplicatable
, if there exists an algorithm that approximates the size of
\left\lvert E\right\rvert
in time
T
, assuming that
\left\lvert E\right\rvert\geq\tau
, then there exists an implementation of the detection oracle in time
\tilde{O}(T)
over sets
U
with
\mu(U)\leq\mu(V)/\tau
.

Proof of
Theorem

40
.
Let
H=(V,E_{H})
and let
\varphi
be a
k
-coloring of
V
, where
V_{i}
denotes the set of vertices in
V
that are mapped to
i
by
\varphi
.
We also use
E=\mathcal{P}(H)
to denote a set of
k
distinct vertices from
V
where we would like to compute a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
approximation for
\left\lvert E\right\rvert
.
Fix some positive value
\tau
.
Let
U\subseteq V
be such that
\mu(U)\leq\mu(V)/\tau
.
We would like to determine if
E_{U}\subseteq E\cap U^{k}
.
We assume we have an algorithm
A
that takes as input a graph
H
and can determine if
E=\mathcal{P}(H)
is empty or contains at least
\tau
elements in time
T
.
We have no guarantee on the output of the algorithm if
E
is not empty and contains less than
\tau
elements.

To answer the query “is
E_{U}
empty”, we build a new auxiliary graph
H^{\prime}
, and let
E^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}(H^{\prime})
. We show that
E_{U}
is empty iff
E^{\prime}
is empty. Moreover, if
E_{U}
is not empty, then
E^{\prime}
contains at least
\tau
elements.
We then run the algorithm
A
on the graph
H^{\prime}
.
If it outputs that
E^{\prime}
is empty, then we output that
E_{U}
is empty as well, and if it outputs that
E^{\prime}
is not empty, then we output that
E_{U}
is not empty.

We explain how to construct the graph
H^{\prime}
.
For every
i
let
U_{i}=U\cap V_{i}
. We define the vertex set
V^{\prime}
of
H^{\prime}
to be
V^{\prime}=V_{1}^{\prime}\sqcup V_{2}^{\prime}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V_{k}^{\prime}
, where
V_{i}^{\prime}
is obtained by duplicating each vertex in
U_{i}
for
\left\lceil\left\lvert V_{i}\right\rvert/\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert\right\rceil
times.
For every pair of vertices
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
, where
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
are duplicates of two vertices
x,y\in U
, we connect
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
if at least one of the following holds.

1.

x
and
y
are connected in
H
.

2.

x=y
.

Constructing the graph
H^{\prime}
takes time
O(\left\lvert V\right\rvert^{2})
.
Since
\mathcal{P}
is in the class
Duplicatable
, we have that
E_{U}
is empty if and only if
E^{\prime}
is empty.
Moreover, if
E_{U}
is not empty, then
E^{\prime}
contains at least
\prod_{i\in[k]}\left\lvert V_{i}\right\rvert/\left\lvert U_{i}\right\rvert=\mu(V)/\mu(U)
elements.
Therefore, for sets
U
of measure
\mu(U)\leq\mu(V)/\tau
, we have that
E^{\prime}
is of size at least
\mu(V)/\mu(U)\geq\tau
, which means that if
E_{U}
is not empty, then
E^{\prime}
contains at least
\tau
elements.
On the other hand if
E_{U}
is empty, then
E^{\prime}
is empty as well.
To see why, assume towards contradiction that
E^{\prime}
contains a an element
e=(u_{1}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,u_{k}^{\prime})
, where
u_{i}^{\prime}\in V_{i}^{\prime}
for every
i\in[k]
. Let
u_{i}
be the “original” vertex in
U_{i}
that
u_{i}^{\prime}
was duplicated from. Then
(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k})
must be in
E_{U}
, since
\mathcal{P}
is in the class
Duplicatable
, which is a contradiction.
∎

8
Proof of
Lemma

15

This section is dedicated to the proof of
Lemma

15
, which follows the same lines as in
[
CEV24
]
, and we repeat it here with only minor modifications.
See
15

For this subsection, we use
q\triangleq p^{k}
.
We now prove the following lemma on the probability that the algorithm
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
outputs a good approximation for the number of hyperedges in
G
, following the line of proof in
[
CEV24
]
.

Lemma 42

(Induction Hypothesis)
.

Given a hypergraph
G
and
\varepsilon
, we set
p=1/2
, and
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\min\left\{1,\varepsilon\right\}}{4\log n}
.
Fix
\gamma
where
\gamma=o(n)
.
Define the following events:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(V,\Lambda)\triangleq\left\{\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(V,\Lambda\right)=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\pm\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)\cdot(2\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime})\right\}\;.

We have that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(V,\Lambda)\right]\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}{q\cdot\gamma}
.

Next, we prove
Lemma

15
using
Lemma

42
.

Proof of
Lemma

15
using
Lemma

42
.
Set
\gamma=Q/\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)
. We get that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(V,\Lambda)\right]\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}{q\cdot\gamma}=1-\frac{4k\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)^{2}}{qQ}\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot 2^{k}\cdot\log^{2}n}{\gamma}\;,

which completes the proof.
∎We prove
Lemma

42
using induction on the depth of the recursion.

Base Case: (
\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)=0
).

In this case we simply return
m_{V}
.
The induction hypothesis holds trivially, as the algorithm always outputs the exact count of hyperedges in
G
.

Induction Hypothesis:

The induction hypothesis is that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(V,\Lambda)\right]\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}{q\cdot\gamma}
.

We also define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\triangleq\left\{\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(U,\Lambda\cdot q\right)=m_{U}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}\pm q\cdot\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)\cdot(2\gamma\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime})\right\}\;.

For a subset of vertices
U\subseteq V
, we use the notation
m_{U}
to denote the number of hyperedges in
G
that are contained in
U
.
The statement
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\right]\geq 1-\frac{12\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}{\gamma q}
, is the induction hypothesis on the random subset of vertices
U
, which is defined in
Algorithm

1
.
To prove the step, we assume the induction hypothesis holds on
U
,
and show that the induction hypothesis also holds on
V
.

Step:

We first define some notation and events.
We have three subset of vertices:
U\subseteq V^{\prime}\subseteq V
, where
V
is the vertex set of
G
,
V^{\prime}
is the set of vertices without the
\Lambda
-heavy vertices, and
U
is a uniformly random subset of
V^{\prime}
, obtained by keeping each vertex from
V^{\prime}
independently with probability
p
.
Let
\hat{m}_{V}
denote the output of
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(V,\Lambda\right)
, and let
\hat{m}_{U}
denote the output of
\mathbf{RecursiveApx}\left(U,\Lambda\cdot q\right)
.
Recall that we use
m_{\Lambda}
to denote
m_{V}(V_{\Lambda})
, i.e., the number of hyperedges (contained in
V
) that contain at least one vertex from
V_{\Lambda}
.
Let
\hat{m}_{\Lambda}
denote the output of
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}(V_{\Lambda},\Lambda)
.
Note that
\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)\geq\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)+1
(unless
\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)=0
, in which case
\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)=\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)=0
).

Intuition.

We compute two values,
\hat{m}_{\Lambda}
and
\hat{m}_{U}
.
We then output
\hat{m}_{v}=\hat{m}_{\Lambda}+\hat{m}_{U}/q
as an approximation for
m_{V}
.
By the guarantees of the two procedures
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
and
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
,
we have that
\hat{m}_{\Lambda}
is a good approximation for
m_{\Lambda}
. What is left is to show that
\hat{m}_{U}/q
is a good approximation for
m_{V^{\prime}}
. We split this into two parts. First, we show that
m_{U}/q
is “close” to the value of
m_{V^{\prime}}
(See
Claim

45
).
Next, we use the induction hypothesis, to show that
\hat{m}_{U}
is a good approximation of
m_{U}
. We need to show that the composition of these approximations also serves as a good approximation for
m_{V}
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}
denote the event that
{V_{\Lambda}}
contains a superset of the
\Lambda
-heavy vertices, without any
\Lambda/\log^{(k^{2})}n
-light vertices, and that
\hat{m}_{\Lambda}=(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})m_{\Lambda}
.
Note that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{{2}}{n^{4}}
, which follows from the guarantees of the two procedures
\mathbf{Find}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
and
\mathbf{Count}\mhyphen\mathbf{Heavy}
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\triangleq\left\{m_{U}/q=m_{V^{\prime}}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})\pm\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right\}
. This denotes the event that the random variable
t_{U}
is not too far from its expectation.
We prove that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{6}{\gamma q}
.
Using those facts, we can complete the proof of the lemma by showing that

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(V,\Lambda)\;.

(18)

Proof of
Equation

18
.

Define three intervals
\mathcal{I}_{V}
,
\mathcal{I}_{V^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{U}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{V}

\displaystyle\triangleq m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\pm 2\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{V^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\triangleq m_{V^{\prime}}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\pm 2\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{U}

\displaystyle\triangleq m_{U}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}\pm 2\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)\gamma\cdot(\Lambda\cdot q)/\varepsilon^{\prime}

Recall that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(V,\Lambda)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\hat{m}_{V}=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\pm 2\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\hat{m}_{U}=m_{U}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}\pm 2\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)\cdot\gamma\cdot(\Lambda\cdot q)/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right\}\;,

where the event
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(V,\Lambda)
can also be written as
\left\{\hat{m}_{V}\in\mathcal{I}_{V}\right\}
and the event
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)
can be written as
\left\{\hat{m}_{U}\in\mathcal{I}_{U}\right\}
, which follows by their definitions.
Recall that
\hat{m}_{V}=\hat{m}_{\Lambda}+\hat{m}_{U}/q
.
We prove the following claim:
If
\hat{m}_{\Lambda}
falls inside the interval
t_{\Lambda}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})
, and
\hat{m}_{U}/q
falls inside the interval
\mathcal{I}_{V^{\prime}}
, then
\hat{m}_{V}
falls inside the interval
\mathcal{I}_{V}
.
To prove this, we show that
t_{\Lambda}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})+\mathcal{I}_{V^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{V}
:

\displaystyle m_{\Lambda}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})+\mathcal{I}_{V^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=m_{\Lambda}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})+m_{V^{\prime}}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\pm\left(2\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq(m_{\Lambda}+m_{V^{\prime}})(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\;\quad\pm\left(2\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=m_{V}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\;\quad\quad\quad\quad\pm\left(2\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)=\mathcal{I}_{V}\;.

In what follows, we show that each of the events
\left\{\hat{m}_{\Lambda}\in m_{\Lambda}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})\right\}
, and
\left\{\hat{m}_{U}/q\in\mathcal{I}_{V^{\prime}}\right\}
contains the event
\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\}
. In other words, we show that if the event
\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\}
occurs, then so do the events
\left\{\hat{m}_{\Lambda}\in m_{\Lambda}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})\right\}
and
\left\{\hat{m}_{U}/q\in\mathcal{I}_{V^{\prime}}\right\}
.

By definition, the event that
\left\{\hat{m}_{\Lambda}\in m_{\Lambda}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})\right\}
contains the event
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}
.
We show that event that
\left\{\hat{m}_{U}/q\in\mathcal{I}_{V^{\prime}}\right\}
contains the event
\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\right\}
, and complete the proof.
We assume that the event
\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\}
occurs, and show that the event
\left\{\hat{m}_{U}/q\in\mathcal{I}_{V^{\prime}}\right\}
occurs:

\displaystyle\dfrac{\hat{m}_{U}}{q}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\in}\dfrac{\mathcal{I}_{U}}{q}=\dfrac{m_{U}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}\pm\left(2\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)\gamma q\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)}{q}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{m_{U}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}}{q}

\displaystyle\pm\left(\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)\cdot(2\gamma\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{=}\;\left[m_{V^{\prime}}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})\pm\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right](1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}

\displaystyle\pm\left(\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)\cdot(2\gamma\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq m_{V^{\prime}}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\pm\left(\gamma\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)\cdot(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}

\displaystyle\pm\left(\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)\cdot(2\gamma\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\subseteq}m_{V^{\prime}}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\pm\left(2\gamma\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\pm\left(\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)\cdot(2\gamma\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=m_{V^{\prime}}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\pm\left(2\gamma\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)(1+\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q))

\displaystyle=m_{V^{\prime}}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}\pm\left(\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)\cdot 2\gamma\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right)=\mathcal{I}_{V^{\prime}}\;.

The
(i)
inequality follows since we assumed the event
{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)}
occurs.
The
(ii)
inequality follows since we assumed the event
{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}}
occurs.
The
(iii)
transition is true because we chose sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{\prime}
such that
(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})^{\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}
is contained in both the interval
[-1/2,3/2]
and the interval
[1-\varepsilon/2,1+\varepsilon/2]
.
This completes the proof.
∎

To complete the proof of
Lemma

42
,
we are left with proving that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}{q\cdot\gamma}\;,

which proves that
\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(V,\Lambda)\geq\frac{4k\cdot\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}{q\cdot\gamma}\right\}
and therefore completes the proof of
Lemma

42
.
To show this, we need the following claim.

Claim 43
.

Define two events as follows.
A_{1}=\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\},
and
A_{2}=\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\}
.
Then, the following inequalities hold:

(1)

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[A_{1}\right]\geq 1-\frac{k}{2q\cdot\gamma}
.

(2)

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[A_{2}\right]\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)+1}{q\cdot\gamma}
.

(3)

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[A_{1}\cap A_{2}\right]\geq 1-{\frac{4k\cdot\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)-3k+1}{q\cdot\gamma}}
.

Proving this claim and using it to prove
Lemma

42
mostly involves algebraic manipulations. The only non-trivial part is to show
(1)
, which we prove in
Claim

45
.
We first prove
Lemma

42
using
Claim

43
.

Proof of
Lemma

42
using
Claim

43
.

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]\cdot{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[A_{1}\cap A_{2}\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]\cdot{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(1-\frac{4k\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)-3k+1}{q\cdot\gamma})\cdot(1-\frac{{2}}{n^{4}})\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)}{q\cdot\gamma}\;.

∎Next, we prove that
(3)
holds, given that
(1)
and
(2)
hold.

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[A_{1}\cap A_{2}\right]\geq 1-({\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\overline{A_{1}}\right]+{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\overline{A_{2}}\right])\geq 1-\frac{k+4k\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)+1}{q\cdot\gamma}=1-\frac{4k\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)-3k+1}{q\cdot\gamma}\;.

The first inequality follows by union bound.
The first inequality follows by parts
(1)
and
(2)
of
Claim

43
.
The last inequality follows as
\mathrm{Depth}\left(\Lambda\right)=\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)+1
.
We are left with proving
(1)
and
(2)
, which we prove in
44
and
45
respectively.

Claim 44
.

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[A_{2}\right]\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)+1}{q\cdot\gamma}
.

Proof.

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[A_{2}\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]/{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cap\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\overline{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\cup\overline{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 1-({\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\overline{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\right]+{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\overline{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}}\right])

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 1-(\frac{4k\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}{\gamma q}+\frac{2}{n^{4}})\geq 1-\frac{4k\cdot\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)+1}{q\cdot\gamma}\;.

The penultimate inequality follows as
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{ind}}(U,\Lambda\cdot q)\right]\geq 1-\frac{4k\hat{\mathsf{D}}(\Lambda q)}{\gamma q}
by the induction hypotheses, and
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{{2}}{n^{4}}
.
The last inequality follows from the fact that
\gamma=o(n)
.
∎

Claim 45
.

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[A_{1}\right]\geq 1-\frac{k\cdot}{2\gamma q}
.

Recall that
A_{1}=\left\{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\}
, which is the event that the random variable
m_{U}
is not too far from its expectation, assuming that the event
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}
occurs - which is the event that
V^{\prime}
does not contain any
\Lambda
-heavy vertices.

To prove
Claim

45
we will use also the following claim.

Claim 46
.

Let
G
be a
k
-partite hypergraph with vertex sets
V_{1}\sqcup V_{2}\sqcup\ldots\sqcup V_{k}
, and a set of hyperedges
E
, where every hyperedge
e\in E
intersects every vertex set
V_{i}
in exactly one vertex.
We denote the number of vertices by
n
and the number of hyperedges by
m
.
Let
P=\left\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}\right\}
be some sampling vector with
p_{i}\in[0,1]
for every
i\in[k]
.
Let
q=\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}
.
Let
U
be a random subset of vertices, where each vertex
v\in V_{i}
is sampled independently with probability
p_{i}
.
Let
X
denote the number of hyperedges in
G[U]
.
Then,
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]=m\cdot q
and
\mathsf{Var}\left[X\right]\leq k\cdot\Delta\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]
, where
\Delta
is maximum degree of a vertex in
G
.

Proof.
Computing the expectation of
X
is immediate;
\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]=\sum_{e\in E}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[e\in G[U]\right]=\sum_{e\in E}\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}=m\cdot q
.
Next, we compute
\mathbb{E}\left[X^{2}\right]
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
denote the set of ordered pairs of hyperedges.
We partition
\mathcal{M}
into two sets,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{1}
contains the ordered pairs of hyperedges that are disjoint in vertices, and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
contains the ordered pairs of hyperedges that share at least one vertex.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X^{2}\right]=\sum_{(e_{1},e_{2})\in\mathcal{M}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[e_{1},e_{2}\in G[U]\right]

We partition this sum into two parts, one for the ordered pairs in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and one for the ordered pairs in
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{(e_{1},e_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[e_{1},e_{2}\in G[U]\right]=q^{2}\cdot\left\lvert\mathcal{M}_{1}\right\rvert\leq q^{2}\cdot m^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]^{2}\;.

We are left with bounding the sum over the ordered pairs in
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{(e_{1},e_{2})\in\mathcal{M}_{2}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[e_{1},e_{2}\in G[U]\right]\leq q\left\lvert\mathcal{M}_{2}\right\rvert\;.

Next, we demonstrate that the cardinality of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
is bounded by
k\cdot m\cdot\Delta
. We represent each pair of intersecting hyperedges from
\mathcal{M}_{2}
as a tuple within the space
[k]\times[m]\times[\Delta]
. To identify the first hyperedge in the pair, we use
m
possibilities. Once the first hyperedge is established, we have
k
choices to determine the first (in lexicographic order) vertex
u
common to both hyperedges. Given vertex
u
, we then have
\deg(u)
choices to identify the second hyperedge that includes
u
, where
\deg(u)\leq\Delta
. This completes the proof.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Claim

45
.

Proof of
Claim

45
.
Recall that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sample}}\triangleq\left\{m_{U}/q=m_{H}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})\pm\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\right\}
. Assuming that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}
occurs, we have that
\Delta_{V^{\prime}}
which is
the maximum degree over
V^{\prime}
, is at most
\Lambda
.
Let
\sigma=q(m_{V^{\prime}}\varepsilon+\gamma\Lambda/\varepsilon)
.
We can write
\left\{A_{1}\right\}
as
\left\{\left\lvert m_{U}/q-m_{V^{\prime}}\right\rvert\leq\sigma\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\}
:

\displaystyle\left\{A_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{m_{U}/q=m_{V^{\prime}}(1\pm\varepsilon^{\prime})\pm\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left\lvert m_{U}/q-m_{V^{\prime}}\right\rvert\leq m_{V^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\prime}\pm\gamma\cdot\Lambda/\varepsilon^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left\lvert m_{U}/q-m_{V^{\prime}}\right\rvert\leq\sigma\mid\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{high}}\right\}\;.

We apply Chebyshev’s inequality on
m_{U}
and get that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\left\lvert m_{U}/q-m_{V^{\prime}}\right\rvert\leq\sigma\right]\leq\frac{\mathsf{Var}\left[m_{U}\right]}{\sigma^{2}}\leq\frac{k\cdot\Delta_{V^{\prime}}\cdot m_{V^{\prime}}\cdot q}{2q^{2}\cdot m_{V^{\prime}}\cdot\gamma\cdot\Lambda}\leq\frac{k}{2q\gamma}\;.

The first inequality follows from Chebyshev’s inequality, and the second inequality follows from the fact that
\mathsf{Var}\left[m_{U}\right]\leq k\cdot\Delta_{V^{\prime}}\cdot m_{V^{\prime}}
, as shown in
Claim

46
.
The next inequality follows from the fact that
\Delta_{V^{\prime}}\leq\Lambda
, and reordering the terms. This completes the proof.
∎

9
The Median Trick

Here we prove a claim on the median of
r
samples of a random variable
Y
,
which we use in the proof of
Lemma

16
.

Claim 47
.

Let
Y
be a random variable, and let
[a,b]
be some interval, where
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Y\in[a,b]\right]\geq 2/3
.
Define
Z
to be the median of
r=400\log n
independent samples of
Y
. Then,
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Z\in[a,b]\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{n^{5}}
.

Proof.
Let
Y_{i}
be the
i
-th sample. We define an indicator random variable
X_{i}
to be
1
if
Y_{i}\in[a,b]
for
i\in[r]
.
Let
X=\sum_{i\in[r]}X_{i}
.
We claim that if more than half of the samples fall inside the interval
[a,b]
then so is the median of all samples. In other words, the rest of the samples do not affect the median.
Therefore,
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[Z\in[a,b]\right]\geq{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X>\frac{r}{2}\right]
.
We lower bound the probability that
{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X>\frac{r}{2}\right]
.
Note that
X
is the sum of
r
independent
p
-Bernoulli random variables with
p\geq 2/3
. Then,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X\leq\frac{r}{2}\right]\leq{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathsf{Bin}\left({r},{\frac{2}{3}}\right)\leq\frac{r}{2}\right]={\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathsf{Bin}\left({r},{\frac{2}{3}}\right)\leq\frac{2r}{3}\cdot(1-\frac{1}{4})\right]\;,

and by the following version of Chernoff’s inequality:

{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[X\leq(1-\delta)\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\delta^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]}{2}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Pr}}\left[\mathsf{Bin}\left({r},{\frac{2}{3}}\right)\leq\frac{2r}{3}\cdot(1-\frac{1}{4})\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{r}{48}\right)\leq\exp\left(-8\log n\right)\leq n^{-5}\;.

∎

10
Some Matrix Multiplication Tools

Recall that we write
\mathsf{MM}\left(n^{a},n^{b},n^{c}\right)
or
n^{\omega(a,b,c)}
for the running time of the matrix multiplication algorithm that multiplies
n^{a}\times n^{b}
by
n^{b}\times n^{c}
matrices.

Claim 48
.

For any
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in[0,1]
we have

\mathsf{MM}\left(np_{1},np_{2},np_{3}\right)\leq\mathsf{MM}\left(n,n,n\cdot(p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\cdot p_{3})\right)
.

Proof of
Claim

48
.
Let
p_{i}=n^{\ell_{i}}
for
i=1,2,3
.
We want to show that
\omega(1+\ell_{1},1+\ell_{2},1+\ell_{3})\leq\omega(1,1,1+\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})
. This follows from two applications of
Claim

32
which states that for every
y,x,z\geq 0
,
x>y
and every
\varepsilon\in[0,(x-y)]
,
\omega(x-\varepsilon,y+\varepsilon,z)\leq\omega(x,y,z)
.

First, we apply
Claim

32
with
\varepsilon=\ell_{1}
,
x=1+\ell_{1}+\ell_{3},y=1,z=1+\ell_{2}
:

\omega(1+\ell_{1},1+\ell_{2},1+\ell_{3})=\omega(1+\ell_{3},1+\ell_{1},1+\ell_{2},)\leq\omega(1+\ell_{1}+\ell_{3},1,1+\ell_{2})=\omega(1+\ell_{1}+\ell_{3},1+\ell_{2},1).

We then apply
Claim

32
with
\varepsilon=\ell_{2}
,
x=1+\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3},y=1,z=1
:

\omega(1+\ell_{1}+\ell_{3},1+\ell_{2},1)\leq\omega(1+\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3},1,1)=\omega(1,1,1+\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}),

and putting it all together we get
\omega(1+\ell_{1},1+\ell_{2},1+\ell_{3})\leq\omega(1,1,1+\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3})
.

∎
